The directed landscape from Brownian motion
Duncan Dauvergne, Bálint Virág

Abstract
We construct an almost sure bijection that recovers the directed landscape on the half-plane from a sequence of independent Brownian motions. This map is the natural scaling limit of the Robinson–Schensted–Knuth (RSK) correspondence. The Brownian motions arise as the marginals of the multi-path stationary horizon associated with the directed landscape. The inverse map is fully explicit and yields a natural coupling in which Brownian last-passage percolation converges in probability to the directed landscape. As an application, we prove that the directed landscape restricted to a strip can be reconstructed from the parabolic Airy line ensemble, resolving a conjecture of the first author and Zhang. Along the way we develop two new versions of RSK in the semi-discrete setting, introduce a general theory of sorting via Pitman operators that generates a faithful action of the biHecke monoid, and establish key identities for the multi-path stationary horizon for both the directed landscape and Brownian last-passage percolation.



1
Introduction

Since Robinson’s 1938 paper
[
Rob38
]
, versions of the Robinson–Schensted–Knuth (RSK) correspondence have been central to representation theory, algebraic combinatorics, and probability. The classical Robinson-Schensted correspondence gives a bijection between permutations and pairs of standard Young tableaux of the same shape.

Via Greene’s theorem this bijection admits a last-passage percolation interpretation on the permutation matrix: the shape records the lengths of the longest disjoint increasing subsequences, realized as maximal path sums in the matrix between a corner and two boundaries. This last-passage viewpoint extends naturally to general environments and forms the foundation for the continuum RSK theory, the main topic of this paper.

Baik, Deift, and Johansson showed that the normalized length of the longest increasing subsequence under the uniform measure on permutations converges to the GUE Tracy-Widom distribution
[
BDJ99
]
. More generally, the full directed metric given by last-passage values on
[n]^{2}
itself converges in the scaling limit to the directed landscape
\mathcal{L}
on the plane,
[
DV21b
]
.

By the KPZ universality conjecture, models with homogeneous locally independent planar noise are expected to scale to
\mathcal{L}
. This has been established for several exactly solvable models, many of which possess an underlying RSK structure. We therefore expect pre-limiting RSK correspondences to converge to a limiting RSK theory for the directed landscape.

The main technical achievement of this paper is to establish this theory and to prove that the limiting map remains a bijection. Since classical inverses lose all meaning in the limit, we construct the inverse using a suite of new geometric, probabilistic, and algebraic results.

In the rest of the introduction we highlight the main results; more precise statements appear in Section
2
.

1.1
RSK in the directed landscape on a bounded time interval: existing and new results

The original construction of the directed landscape relies on a version of RSK restricted to the time interval
[0,1]
. Here the analogue of the pair of Young tableaux is the parabolic Airy line ensemble
\mathfrak{A}
. It has long been known that
\mathcal{L}(0,0;x,1)=\mathfrak{A}_{1}(x)
. More generally,
[
DZ21
]
established the multi-path formula

\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathfrak{A}_{i}(x)=\mathcal{L}(0,0^{k};x^{k},1),

where the right-hand side denotes the maximal
\mathcal{L}
-length of
k
disjoint paths between
(0,0)
and
(x,1)
.
This is the direct analogue of the RSK map on the strip. Dauvergne and Zhang have the following natural conjecture.

Conjecture 1.1

(
[
DZ21
]
, Conjecture 1.10)
.

The map
\mathcal{L}|_{t\in[0,1]}\mapsto\mathfrak{A}
is invertible.
That is, for
s<t\in[0,1]
and
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
,
\mathcal{L}(x,s;y,t)
is a measurable function of
\mathfrak{A}
.

The original construction of
\mathcal{L}
gives hope:
\mathcal{L}(x,0;y,1)
can be recovered as a last-passage limit in the environment
\mathfrak{A}
. Full bijectivity on the strip has remained open. As a corollary (Corollary
6.10
) to our main result, we get the following.

Theorem 1.2

(RSK on the strip)
.

RSK on the strip is an almost sure bijection: Conjecture
1.1
holds.

In the strip case the inverse is not fully explicit, leaving open the following problem.

Problem 1.3
.

Express the inverse map in terms of last passage on
\mathfrak{A}
.

The RSK correspondence simplifies dramatically, and the inverse becomes fully explicit, when the directed landscape is restricted to a half-plane
\{t\leq 0\}
(the case
\{t\geq 0\}
is symmetric).

1.2
The main result: RSK for the directed landscape on
\{t\leq 0\}

For the lower half-plane the RSK map may be viewed as a normalized limit of the strip map. It sends
\mathcal{L}|_{\{t\leq 0\}}
to a sequence of paths
B_{i}
defined by the
multi-path Busemann function

\sum_{i=1}^{k}B_{i}(x)=\lim_{t\to-\infty}\mathcal{L}(0^{k},t;x^{k},0)-\mathcal{L}(0^{k},t;0^{k},0).

(1)

Busemann techniques together with the Brownian Gibbs property yield the following (part of Theorem
2.5
).

Proposition 1.4
.

The limits in (
1
) exist almost surely and the
B_{i}
are independent two-sided Brownian motions (of variance 2, a convention in KPZ and here).

Inverting the map
\mathcal{L}|_{\{t\leq 0\}}\mapsto B
therefore gives a natural construction of the directed landscape from independent randomness. This is the main result of the paper.

To state the inverse we work in the last-passage percolation setting. Let
f
be a sequence of continuous functions. The last-passage value
f[(x,n)\to(y,m)]
(and its multi-path version) is defined in the usual way, see (
5
). The fundamental convergence result of
[
DOV22
]
is:

Theorem 1.5

(Theorem 1.5,
[
DOV22
]
)
.

For every
a\in\mathbb{R}
, let
B^{a}
be an environment of independent two-sided Brownian motions of variance 2 and common drift
a
. With the scaling
(x,s)_{a}=(x-as/4,\lfloor sa^{3}/8\rfloor+1)
, we have

\mathcal{L}^{a}(x,s;y,t):=B^{a}[(x,s)_{a}\to(y,t)_{a}]-\frac{a^{2}}{4}(t-s)\to\mathcal{L}(x,s;y,t)

in distribution as
a\to-\infty
.

In our coupling the environments
B^{a}
are constructed directly from a fixed zero-drift Brownian environment
B
via multi-path Busemann functions. For
a\geq 0
, define the
Busemann shear

\sum_{i=1}^{k}B^{a}_{i}(x)=\lim_{t\to-\infty}B[(t,\lfloor\theta|t|\rfloor)^{k}\to(x,1)^{k}]-B[(t,\lfloor\theta t\rfloor)^{k}\to(0,1)^{k}],\qquad\theta=a|a|/2,

with the case
a<0
defined analogously by reversal, see (
12
) and Figure
3
.

Theorem 1.6

(Main)
.

RSK for the directed landscape on
\{t\leq 0\}
admits an explicit inverse. With
B^{a}
defined as above, the convergence of Theorem
1.5
holds in probability on
\{s<t\leq 0\}
. Moreover,
B
and
\mathcal{L}|_{\{t\leq 0\}}
are coupled through the Busemann relation (
1
).

For
a\neq 0
the Busemann shear
B\mapsto B^{a}
itself forms an RSK correspondence on the Brownian environment and satisfies a simple group law.

Theorem 1.7
.

For
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
we have
(B^{a})^{b}=B^{a+b}
almost surely. In particular,
(B^{a})^{-a}=B
.

1.3
The multi-path stationary horizon

The map sending the zero-drift environment
B
to the first
k
lines of
B^{a}
is closely related to the
stationary horizon
. In the directed landscape setting, the stationary horizon was defined in
[
BSS24
]
as the joint law of Busemann functions in several directions at a fixed time.

We develop this theory by viewing the independent Brownian motions appearing in the horizon construction as differences of multi-path Busemann functions in the directed landscape. The following result highlights the structure (a special case of Theorems
2.4
and
2.5
). For
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
increasing, let
\mathfrak{B}^{\theta}
denote the
multi-path stationary horizon
, the
k
-path Busemann function in direction
\theta
defined through the natural generalization (
18
) of (
1
).

Theorem 1.8
.

\mathfrak{B}^{\theta_{1}},\mathfrak{B}^{(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})}-\mathfrak{B}^{\theta_{1}},\ldots,\mathfrak{B}^{\theta}-\mathfrak{B}^{(\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{k-1})}

(2)

are independent Brownian motions with drifts
\theta_{1}/2,\ldots\theta_{k}/2
respectively.
For the
2^{k}-1
nonempty subsequences
\eta
of
\theta
,
\mathfrak{B}^{\eta}
can be recovered from (
2
) via an action of the biHecke monoid
\mathfrak{D}_{k}
.

Up to parametrization, the same result holds for Brownian last passage percolation, see Theorem
2.4
.
Our main algebraic contribution is the introduction of the action of the biHecke monoid
\mathfrak{D}_{k}
on last passage percolation. This is explained in detail in the next subsection.

1.4
Proof strategy

The proof of Theorem
1.6
has four main steps and requires a combination of new algebraic, probabilistic and geometric ideas.

The first step is devoted to developing an algebraic language built around the biHecke monoid. Underlying the algebra is the observation that RSK is fundamentally a sorting operation, see
[
BBO05
]
. The Pitman transform acts as a conditional swap for neighboring disordered lines. Iterating these operators generates a faithful action of the
0-Hecke monoid

\mathfrak{M}_{n}
introduced in
[
Nor79
]
. Adjoining the reverse operators yields the
biHecke monoid

\mathfrak{D}_{n}
, first introduced in
[
HST13
]
. We prove that the Pitman and reverse Pitman operators give an action of
\mathfrak{D}_{n}
in the last-passage setting. This algebraic structure is essential for establishing isometries and identifying inverses.

The second step proves generalized isometries relating multi-path Busemann values in the landscape and in the Brownian environment. A specific case of this isometry states that the drifted Brownian motions
B^{a}
, defined as multi-path Busemann values in the environment
B
in Theorem
1.6
are in fact multi-path Busemann values in
\mathcal{L}
:

Theorem 1.9

(Isometry between
\mathcal{L}|_{t\leq 0}
and
B
, part 1)
.

\sum_{i=1}^{k}B_{i}^{a}(x)=\lim_{t\to-\infty}\mathcal{L}((at/2,t)^{k};(x,0)^{k})-\mathcal{L}((at/2,t)^{k};(0,0)^{k})

(3)

The third step supplies the core geometric argument of the paper. For large
k
the
k
-path optimizers occupy a remarkably rigid, almost deterministic “truss” shape. By carefully sending starting points to
-\infty
at a controlled rate and driving directions also to
-\infty
, these trusses converge in the
k\to\infty
limit to the horizontal line
\ell=[-\infty,0]\times\{-1\}
.

t=0
t=-1
t\to-\infty
y
extra optimizer from
y
truss

Figure 1
:
Truss formed by large-
k
disjoint optimizers in the directed landscape.

Introducing a new optimizer started from a bounded point
y
then reveals that the incremental change in the
(k+1)
-path optimizer is essentially identical to the change in
\mathcal{L}(0,-1;y,0)
, because only the portion after time
-1
varies significantly while the earlier portion is forced to hug the truss, see Figure
1
. This rigidity allows full reconstruction of
\mathcal{L}(x,s;y,0)
for all
x,y
and
s<0
. A two-slit variant extends the reconstruction to the complete
\mathcal{L}(x,s;y,t)
for
s<t\leq 0
.

The final step employs a novel abstract measure-theoretic argument to upgrade convergence in law to convergence in probability under the explicit coupling.

1.5
Future research directions

We hope this work opens new avenues both for understanding the directed landscape and for proving convergence results. Several directions appear particularly promising with current techniques.

Problem 1.10
.

Construct the directed landscape directly from Brownian last passage percolation without any appeal to determinantal formulas.

Problem 1.11
.

Upgrade the convergence in Theorem
1.6
to quantitative rates: for compact
K\subset\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\uparrow}
show the existence of
\alpha>0
such that

\mathbb{E}\sup_{u\in K}|\mathcal{L}^{a}(u)-\mathcal{L}(u)|\leq|a|^{-\alpha+o(1)}

as
a\to-\infty
, and optimize
\alpha
.

Discrete models whose multi-type stationary distributions admit queuing-theoretic descriptions (colored TASEP, q-PushTASEP and relatives) are natural candidates for an RSK-based convergence proof to the landscape.

Problem 1.12
.

Prove that discrete particle systems whose multi-type stationary distributions have queuing-theoretic descriptions converge to the directed landscape.

The techniques developed here also extend naturally to “zigzag” Brownian last-passage models with alternating drifts.

Problem 1.13
.

Let
B=(B_{i},i\in\mathbb{Z})
be an environment of independent Brownian motions of drift
1
if
i
is even and drift
-1
if
i
is odd. For points
(x,n),(y,m)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}
with
n>m
define

B[(x,n)\rightsquigarrow(y,m)]=\sup_{z_{n+1}=x,\dots,z_{m}=y}\sum_{i=m}^{n}B_{i}(z_{i})-B_{i}(z_{i+1}),

where in the supremum, we require
z_{i+1}\leq z_{i}
if
i
is odd, and
z_{i+1}\geq z_{i}
if
i
is even. This requirement along with the drift condition on the Brownian paths means the above supremum is always finite.
Prove that under some scaling, this model converges to the directed landscape.

Establishing convergence for such models would provide a clean test case for the present ideas.

1.6
Background and related work

We give a brief review of the literature on KPZ, RSK, and the directed landscape, focusing on papers closely related to the present work. For a gentle introduction to KPZ suitable for a newcomer to the area, see
[
Rom15
]
or the introductory articles
[
Cor16
,
Gan22
]
. Review articles and books that go into more depth include
[
FS10
,
Qua11
,
WFS17
,
Zyg18
]
.

The importance of RSK to problems in the KPZ universality class goes back at least to
[
LS77
,
VK77
]
, who identified the law of large numbers for the longest increasing subsequence in a uniform permutation. The Baik-Deift-Johansson theorem
[
BDJ99
]
went further, analyzing exact formulas for the Young tableaux arising from RSK to identify the one-point distribution
\mathcal{L}(0,0;0,1)
as a GUE Tracy-Widom random variable, see also
[
Joh00
]
. Prähofer and Spohn expanded on these methods
[
PS02
]
to prove convergence to the parabolic Airy line ensemble for the PNG droplet, see also
[
Joh03
]
. The Prähofer-Spohn theorem can be viewed as taking the KPZ scaling limit of the Young tableaux side of the RSK correspondence. More recent approaches to studying KPZ limits have found exact formulas for multi-time distributions, see
[
JR20
,
BL19
]
and
[
MQR21
]
for the construction of the KPZ fixed point.

A series of papers by O’Connell and coauthors
[
OY01
,
OY02
,
O’C03
,
BBO05
]
developed RSK in the semi-discrete setting and started to understand the correspondence in terms of Pitman transforms. One of the upshots of this work was a version of the RSK isometry which had also been observed in
[
NY04
]
. This isometry was used in
[
DOV22
]
as the key tool for constructing the directed landscape, and of course drives much of the present paper.

The relevance of Busemann functions to studying KPZ models goes back to Hoffman
[
Hof05
,
Hof08
]
in first passage percolation. See
[
ADH17
, Section 5]
for discussion surrounding open problems on Busemann functions in that setting. In solvable models of last passage percolation, Busemann functions have been used to study competition and coexistence, to construct stationary solutions and establish uniqueness of these solutions, and to establish results about semi-infinite geodesics, see
[
CP12
,
CP13
,
BCK14
,
GRASY15
,
GRAS17a
,
GRAS17b
]
for a sample of recent work in this direction.
The last-passage/queuing-theoretic description for the law of the stationary horizon in (
14
) has its roots in the work of Ferrari and Martin
[
FM07
,
FM06
]
on coloured TASEP and the Hammersley process. A similar description in exponential LPP was given by Seppäläinen and Fan
[
FS20
]
before being adapted to Brownian LPP
[
SS23a
]
. Busemann functions in the directed landscape were first studied in
[
RV21
]
. The law of the whole stationary horizon found in
[
Bus21
,
SS23b
]
, connected to the directed landscape in
[
BSS24
]
, and shown to be the scaling limit for stationary measures in discrete models in
[
BSS22
,
BSS23
]
.

The use of multi-path last passage values and disjoint optimizers to extract information about underlying geometry in KPZ is a more recent phenomenon. Hammond
[
Ham22
,
Ham20
]
first connected exact statistics about disjoint optimizers from a common point in Brownian LPP to the rarity of disjoint geodesics. A precise combinatorial connection between disjoint optimizers and the RSK isometry was exploited in
[
DOV22
]
, and used there as a key tool in the original construction of the directed landscape. The theory of disjoint optimizers at the level of the directed landscape was developed in
[
DZ21
]
. This was used to help classify geodesic networks in the directed landscape in
[
Dau23a
]
, and to prove structural results about the spacetime difference profile in
[
GZ22
]
. The phenomenon of optimizer rigidity for large
k
that is so important in the proof of Theorem
2.5
was anticipated in the watermelon scaling exponents uncovered in
[
BGHH22
]
.

2
Main results in detail: the three limits of RSK

To introduce the RSK correspondence, we need to first define multi-path LPP across a functional environment. Consider a continuous function
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{I}
, where
I\subset\mathbb{Z}
is an interval. For a nonincreasing right-continuous function
\pi:[x,y]\to\{m,\dots,n\}
(henceforth a
path
from
(x,n)
to
(y,m)
) define its
length
with respect to
f
by

\|\pi\|_{f}=\sum_{i=m}^{n}f_{i}(t_{i-1})-f_{i}(t_{i})

(4)

where
t_{i}=\inf\{t\in[x,y]:\pi(t)\leq i\}
and
t_{m-1}=y
. We then define the
last passage value

f[(x,n)\to(y,m)]=\max_{\pi:(x,n)\to(y,m)}\|\pi\|_{f},

(5)

where the maximum is over all paths from
(x,n)
to
(y,m)
. Last passage percolation (LPP) is best thought of as a kind of directed metric on the set
\mathbb{R}\times I
with distances distorted by the environment
f
, and where we maximize rather than minimize path length. With this perspective in mind, paths that achieve the maximum in (
5
) are called
geodesics
. We also need to define multi-path last passage values. First, for a linearly ordered set
X
, we write

X^{k}_{\leq}=\{(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})\in X^{k}:x_{1}\leq x_{2}\leq\cdots\leq x_{k}\},

and similarly define
X^{k}_{<},X^{k}_{\geq},X^{k}_{>}
. Consider
\mathbf{p}=(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{n}),\mathbf{q}=(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{m})\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}\times I^{k}_{\leq}
. We say that
\pi=(\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{k})
is a
disjoint
k
-tuple
from
\mathbf{p}
to
\mathbf{q}
if each
\pi_{i}
is a path from
(x_{i},n_{i})
to
(y_{i},m_{i})
and if
\pi_{i}(t)<\pi_{i+1}(t)
for
t\in(x_{i},y_{i})\cap(x_{i+1},y_{i+1})
. If there is at least one disjoint
k
-tuple from
\mathbf{p}
to
\mathbf{q}
, we can define the multi-path last passage value

f[\mathbf{p}\to\mathbf{q}]=\sup_{\pi:\mathbf{p}\to\mathbf{q}}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\|\pi_{i}\|_{f},

where the supremum is over all disjoint
k
-tuples from
\mathbf{p}
to
\mathbf{q}
. We call a
k
-tuple achieving the above supremum a
(disjoint) optimizer
. To simplify the notation for multi-path last passage values in the situations that occur most, we write
x^{k}:=(x,\dots,x)\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
. Typically, we will consider multi-path last passage when all the points in
\mathbf{p}
lie on a common vertical or horizontal line (and similarly for
\mathbf{q}
). In this case, we will write
\mathbf{p}=(\mathbf{x},n):=(\mathbf{x},n^{k})
and
\mathbf{p}=(x,\mathbf{n}):=(x^{k},\mathbf{n})
. See Figure
2
for illustrations of this definition.

5
4
3
2
1

Figure 2
:
A disjoint optimizer for
k=3
from
((0,0,0.2),5)
to
((0.7,0.9,1),1)
. Note that in our coordinate system, we view paths as moving up and to the right across the page.

Via Greene’s theorem
[
Gre74
]
, we can describe many classical versions of RSK in terms of multi-path LPP across a continuous environment
f:[a,b]\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Essentially, the RSK map takes
f
to a pair of environments
W^{U}f,W^{R}f
which record multi-path last passage values from the bottom corner
(a,n)
to the upper (U) boundary
[a,b]\times\{1\}
and the right (R) boundary
\{b\}\times\{1,\dots,n\}
of
[a,b]\times\{1,\dots,n\}
, respectively. More precisely, letting
T_{n}:=\{(i,j)\in\{1,\dots,n\}^{2}:i\leq n-j+1\}
, define two functions
W^{U}f:[a,b]\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
W^{R}f:T_{n}\to\mathbb{R},(i,j)\mapsto(W^{R}f)_{i}(j)
as follows:

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{k}(W^{U}f)_{i}(x)

\displaystyle=f[(a^{k},n)\to(x^{k},1)],\qquad

\displaystyle 1\leq k\leq n,\;\;x\in[a,b].

(6)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{k}(W^{R}f)_{i}(j)

\displaystyle=f[(a^{k},n)\to(b^{k},j)],\qquad

\displaystyle(k,j)\in T_{n}.

The map
f\mapsto Wf:=(W^{U}f,W^{R}f)
has many remarkable properties. We highlight three that are particularly important for this paper:

Invertibility:

The RSK map
W
is invertible on its image with an
explicit inverse
. Moreover, its image is easy to describe: a pair
(h,g)
equals
Wf
for some continuous
f
if and only if
h:[a,b]\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
is continuous function into the ordered set
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{\leq}
with
h(a)=0
,
g
satisfies the Gelfand-Tsetlin inequalities
g_{i}(j)\geq g_{i}(j+1)\geq g_{i+1}(j)
, and
h(b)=g_{\cdot}(1)
. The inequalities in this description of the image imply that
h,g
can be viewed as path representations of Young tableaux, and the condition
h(b)=g_{\cdot}(1)
says that these tableaux have the same shape. By restricting to certain classes of piecewise linear functions
f
in our framework we can recover classical matrix versions of RSK (i.e. the Robinson-Schensted correspondence for permutations, usual RSK, dual RSK).

Measure Preservation:

The bijectivity of RSK implies that it maps nice measures to nice measures. For example, if
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}
and
f
is an environment of independent Bernoulli walks, then
W^{U}f
consists of
n
independent Bernoulli walks conditioned to stay ordered. Similarly, if
B:[a,b]\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
is an environment of independent Brownian motions, then
W^{U}B
consists of
n
non-intersecting Brownian motions. There are similar interpretations for
W^{R}
.

Isometry:

The map
W^{U}
preserves bottom-to-top last passage values. More precisely, we have the
boundary isometry

f[(\mathbf{x},n)\to(\mathbf{y},1)]=W^{U}f[(\mathbf{x},n)\to(\mathbf{y},1)]

(7)

for all
f,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}
. Similarly,
W^{R}
preserves left-to-right passage values.

We refer the reader to
[
DNV22
]
for precise versions of the statements above and a presentation of RSK in this framework, and
[
Ful97
,
Sta99
]
for more classical presentations.

In this paper, we will take three successive limits of RSK so that at every stage we have useful and interesting notions of invertibility, measure preservation, and isometry. Our final limit of the RSK correspondence will make explicit the function in Theorem
1.6
.

2.1
The first limit: RSK for functions
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}

In Section
4
, we define an RSK map on the domain

\mathcal{C}^{n}(\mathbb{R}):=\{f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}:\lambda(f):=\lim_{x\to\pm\infty}\frac{f(x)}{x}\text{ exists}\}.

This map is the limit of RSK on functions
f:[a,b]\times\{1,\dots,n\}
where we take
a\to-\infty,b\to\infty
. We restrict to functions with an asymptotic slope for simplicity; the map could be defined on a larger space, but we will not need this here. The motivation for taking this limit is that when we let
[a,b]
tend to
(-\infty,\infty)
, the RSK correspondence becomes simpler and picks up additional symmetries. In particular, the inverse map in this setting becomes a straightforward conjugation of the forward map.

Since our right boundary
\{b\}\times\{1,\dots,n\}
is tending to infinity, the information from
W^{R}f
should disappear in this limit, and the correspondence should now produce just a single function
Wf=(Wf_{1},\dots,Wf_{n})
, which takes the place of
W^{U}f
.
The corner
(a,n)
that played the key anchoring role in (
6
) also converges to
(-\infty,n)
in this limit. For this reason, we need to define last passage from
-\infty
: for
\mathbf{m}\in\{1,\dots,n\}^{k}_{<}
, let

f[(-\infty,\mathbf{m})\to(\mathbf{x},1)]:=\lim_{t\to-\infty}f[(t,\mathbf{m})\to(\mathbf{x},1)]+\sum_{i=1}^{k}f_{m_{i}}(t).

(8)

This limit does not necessarily exist, but if we ask that
f\in\mathcal{C}^{n}_{<}(\mathbb{R}):=\{f\in\mathcal{C}^{n}(\mathbb{R}):\lambda(f)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}_{<}\}
then it always will. With the above setup in place, we can now take the limit of the RSK formula (
6
) to describe
Wf
when
f\in\mathcal{C}^{n}_{<}(\mathbb{R})
:

\sum_{i=1}^{k}Wf_{i}(x)=f[(-\infty,(n,\dots,n-k+1))\to(x^{k},1)].

(9)

With this definition, we have versions of invertibility, measure preservation, and isometry.

Theorem 2.1
.

The map
W
in (
9
) has the following properties:

1.

(Invertibility)
W
maps
\mathcal{C}^{n}_{<}(\mathbb{R})
bijectively onto
\mathcal{C}^{n}_{>}(\mathbb{R}):=\{f\in\mathcal{C}^{n}(\mathbb{R}):\lambda(f)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}_{>}\}
. Moreover, the inverse map is given by
RWR:\mathcal{C}^{n}_{>}(\mathbb{R})\to\mathcal{C}^{n}_{<}(\mathbb{R})
, where
R:\mathcal{C}^{n}(\mathbb{R})\to\mathcal{C}^{n}(\mathbb{R})
is the reflection map
Rf(x)=f(-x)
.

2.

(Measure preservation) Let
\mu_{\lambda}
denote the law on
\mathcal{C}^{n}(\mathbb{R})
of
n
independent Brownian motions with drift vector
\lambda
and variance
2
. Then if
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{n}_{<}
and
B\sim\mu_{\lambda}
, then
WB-WB(0)\sim\mu_{\lambda^{*}}
where
\lambda^{*}=(\lambda_{n},\dots,\lambda_{1})
.

3.

(Isometry) The isometry (
7
) holds with
Wf
in place of
W^{U}f
.

Parts of Theorem
2.1
are not completely new. Indeed, the isometry is essentially immediate from the isometry in the finite setting, and the fact that
RWR
inverts
W
is related to a similar description of the inverse RSK map on functions
f:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
given in
[
DNV22
]
.
The bijection
W
turns out to also be the same as a certain queuing map described in Sorensen’s Ph.D. thesis
[
Sor23
]
, see Section 2.3.3 and Lemma 2.3.18 therein. The description in terms of multi-path LPP and the connection with RSK is not discussed there.

When
n=2
, the measure preservation property in Theorem
2.1
is well-known: this is one aspect of the so-called
Brownian Burke theorem
, first observed in
[
HW90
]
and applied to LPP in
[
OY01
]
. To move from measure preservation for
n=2
to general
n
, we will build the
n
-line map by repeatedly applying the
2
-line map to pairs of adjacent lines. This approach to RSK was first developed in the closely related context of functions
f:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
by Biane, Bougerol, and O’Connell
[
BBO05
]
, building on work of
[
OY01
,
OY02
,
O’C03
]
. This approach was further studied in
[
DOV22
,
DNV22
]
. We expand on these ideas here, and along the way describe a whole family of useful and interesting maps on the space of functions
\mathcal{C}^{n}(\mathbb{R})
which generalize the maps
W
and
RWR
. This broader family will be crucial for understanding the next limit transition. Each of these maps has nice invertibility, measure-preservation, and isometry properties and are useful for studying different aspects and models of LPP.

To describe things more precisely, define the
Pitman transform

\mathcal{P}:\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R})\to\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R})
by letting
\mathcal{P}|_{\mathcal{C}^{2}_{<}(\mathbb{R})}
be the
2
-line map
W
described above, and setting
\mathcal{P}|_{\mathcal{C}^{2}_{\geq}(\mathbb{R})}=\text{id}
. Next, for
i=1,\dots,n-1
, define
sorting operators

\tau_{i},\bar{\tau}_{i}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
by

\tau_{i}(x)=\begin{cases}x,\qquad&x_{i}\geq x_{i+1},\\
(x_{1},\dots,x_{i+1},x_{i},\dots,x_{n}),\qquad&x_{i}<x_{i+1}\end{cases}

and

\bar{\tau}_{i}(x)=\begin{cases}x,\qquad&x_{i}\leq x_{i+1},\\
(x_{1},\dots,x_{i+1},x_{i},\dots,x_{n}),\qquad&x_{i}>x_{i+1}.\end{cases}

Equivalently,
\bar{\tau}_{i}=R\tau_{i}R
, where
Rx=-x
. Let
\mathfrak{D}_{n}
denote the monoid generated by the
\tau_{i},\bar{\tau}_{i},i=1,\dots,n-1
. We then define operators
\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{i}},\mathcal{P}_{\bar{\tau}_{i}}:\mathcal{C}^{n}(\mathbb{R})\to\mathcal{C}^{n}(\mathbb{R})
by setting

\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{i}}(f_{1},\dots,f_{n})=(f_{1},\dots,f_{i-1},\mathcal{P}(f_{i},f_{i+1}),f_{i+2},\dots,f_{n}),

and letting
\mathcal{P}_{\bar{\tau}_{i}}=R\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{i}}R
. It turns out that these definitions extend to an action of the whole monoid
\mathfrak{D}_{n}
. For an arbitrary element
\sigma=\pi_{1}\cdots\pi_{k}\in\mathfrak{D}_{n}
where
\pi_{i}\in\{\tau_{i},\bar{\tau}_{i},i=1,\dots,n-1\}
, we can define
\mathcal{P}_{\sigma}=\mathcal{P}_{\pi_{1}}\cdots\mathcal{P}_{\pi_{k}}
; this definition is independent of the choice of the decomposition
\pi_{1}\cdots\pi_{k}
of
\sigma
, and we have the following theory for this monoid action, which can be viewed as a generalization of Theorem
2.1
.

Theorem 2.2
.

The maps
\mathcal{P}_{\sigma},\sigma\in\mathcal{D}_{n}
have the following properties.

1.

(Invertibility via last passage) Let
f\in\mathcal{C}^{n}_{\leq}(\mathbb{R})
. Let
O(f)
denote the orbit of
f
under the action of
\mathcal{P}_{\sigma},\sigma\in\mathfrak{D}_{n}
and let
O(\lambda(f))
denote the orbit of
\lambda(f)
under the action of
\mathfrak{D}_{n}
. Then the map
g\mapsto\lambda(g)
is a bijection from
O(f)
to
O(\lambda(f))
and
\lambda\circ\mathcal{P}_{\sigma}=\sigma\circ\lambda
as functions from
O(f)\to O(\lambda(f))
. Moreover, if
g\in O(f)
, then for all
x\in\mathbb{R},k\in\{1,\dots,n\}
we have

\sum_{i=1}^{k}g_{i}(x)=f[(-\infty,\mathbf{m}^{k,g})\to(x^{k},1)],

where
\mathbf{m}^{k,g}\in\{1,\dots,n\}^{k}_{<}
is the minimal vector such that as multi-sets,

\{\lambda(g)_{1},\dots,\lambda(g)_{k}\}=\{\lambda(f)_{\mathbf{m}^{k,g}_{1}},\dots,\lambda(f)_{\mathbf{m}^{k,g}_{k}}\}.

2.

(Measure preservation) If
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
B\sim\mu_{\lambda}
, then
\mathcal{P}_{\sigma}B-\mathcal{P}_{\sigma}B(0)\sim\mu_{\sigma(\lambda)}
.

3.

(Isometry) The isometry (
7
) holds with
\mathcal{P}_{\sigma}f
in place of
W^{U}f
.

Theorem
2.2
can be interpreted as saying that given an environment
f
, we can build a family of boundary isometric environment
\mathcal{P}_{\sigma}f
which permute the slopes of the lines of
f
in different ways. We can access different information about last passage values across
f
by studying different elements in this family.
Theorem
2.2
is proven in the text as a combination of Corollary
4.5
, Lemma
4.6
, and Proposition
4.10
. The special case of Theorem
2.1
is given in Corollary
4.12
.

2.2
The second limit: RSK for functions
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\mathbb{N}}(\mathbb{R})

Next, we take a limit of RSK on
\mathcal{C}^{n}(\mathbb{R})
as
n\to\infty
. This is the goal of Section
5
. This limit naturally results in
Busemann functions
for Brownian LPP, foreshadowing the description of the maps in Theorem
1.6
. Let
B=(B_{i},i\in\mathbb{N})\in\mathcal{C}^{\mathbb{N}}(\mathbb{R})
be an environment of independent two-sided Brownian motions with no drift. For
\theta\in[0,\infty)^{k}_{\leq}
and
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
define the multi-path Busemann function

\mathcal{B}^{\theta}(\mathbf{x};B)=\lim_{t\to-\infty}B[(t,{\left\lfloor\theta|t|\right\rfloor}+k)\to(\mathbf{x},1)]-N_{\theta}(t)

(10)

Here the notation
{\left\lfloor\theta t\right\rfloor}
should be understood coordinatewise:
{\left\lfloor\theta t\right\rfloor}=({\left\lfloor\theta_{1}t\right\rfloor},\dots,{\left\lfloor\theta_{k}t\right\rfloor})
. We use similar coordinatewise notation throughout the paper.
We have added
+k
above to ensure that the Busemann function is well-defined even if
\theta=0^{k}
. The function
N_{\theta}(t)
is a normalization term needed in order to ensure that the limit (
10
) exists. There are a couple of different choices for this normalization term; we choose the normalization that retains the most information. Letting
\hat{\theta}\in[0,\infty)^{m}_{<}
be uniquely chosen so that
\theta=(\hat{\theta}_{1}^{\ell_{1}},\dots,\hat{\theta}_{m}^{\ell_{m}})
, set

N_{\theta}(t):=\sum_{i=1}^{m}B[(t,\lfloor\hat{\theta}_{i}^{\ell_{i}}|t|\rfloor+k)\to(0^{\ell_{i}},1)],

where the operation
\hat{\theta}_{i}^{\ell_{i}}\mapsto\lfloor\hat{\theta}_{i}^{\ell_{i}}|t|\rfloor+k
should again be understood coordinatewise. Busemann functions are closely linked to the study of
semi-infinite optimizers
. A semi-infinite optimizer in direction
\theta
ending at
(\mathbf{x},1)
is a
k
-tuple
\pi=(\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{k})
such that
\pi_{i}:(-\infty,x_{i}]\to\mathbb{N}
,
\pi(t)/|t|\to\theta
as
t\to-\infty
and such that
\pi|_{[y,x_{k}]}
is an optimizer ending at
(\mathbf{x},1)
for any
y<x_{1}
. A semi-infinite optimizer with one path is called a
semi-infinite geodesic
.

The single-path Busemann process and semi-infinite geodesics in Brownian LPP have been extensively studied in recent years. The state-of-the-art results are due to Seppäläinen and Sorensen
[
SS23b
]
, building on
[
ARAS20
,
SS23a
]
. They showed that there is a random countable set
\Theta\subset[0,\infty)
such that for
\theta\notin\Theta
, the limit (
10
) exists for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
and the process
(\theta,x)\mapsto\mathcal{B}^{\theta}(x;B)
is continuous in both
x
and
\theta
on
\mathbb{R}\times[0,\infty)\setminus\Theta
. Moreover, semi-infinite geodesics exist ending at all points and in all directions
\theta
, and for
\theta\notin\Theta
, all semi-infinite geodesics in direction
\theta
eventually coalesce. We use these results to help build an analogous toolkit for working with multi-path Busemann functions for Brownian LPP, see Section
5.1
for details.

Now let us return to the problem of defining a limit for the RSK correspondence on
\mathcal{C}^{n}(\mathbb{R})
as
n\to\infty
. Consider the Brownian environment
B
as above, and let
B^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}^{\mathbb{N}}(\mathbb{R})
be an independent Brownian environment, where each
B^{\prime}_{i}
has drift
a>0
. For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
W^{n}=\mathcal{P}_{\sigma_{n}}(B_{1},\dots,B_{n},B_{1}^{\prime},\dots,B_{n}^{\prime})
, where
\sigma_{n}
is chosen so that the slopes are reversed, i.e.
\lambda(W^{n})=(a^{n},0^{n})
. By Theorem
2.2
.2,
W^{n}-W^{n}(0)\sim\mu_{(a^{n},0^{n})}
. In particular,
W^{n}-W^{n}(0)
converges in law as
n\to\infty
to an environment
B^{a}\in\mathcal{C}^{\mathbb{N}}(\mathbb{R})
of infinitely many independent Brownian motions with slope
a
. It turns out that this convergence also happens almost surely, and that
B^{a}
is described purely in terms of Busemann functions for the unsloped environment
B
; the dependence on
B^{\prime}
disappears in the limit. We have now defined a collection of maps indexed by
a>0
that play the role of RSK on
\mathcal{C}^{\mathbb{N}}(\mathbb{R})
. It turns out they are invertible, measure-preserving, and naturally satisfy a
Busemann isometry
.

To make things more precise, define

\mathcal{W}^{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};B):=\mathcal{B}^{\lambda|\lambda|/2}(\mathbf{x};B),

which parametrizes Busemann functions by the asymptotic slope of the function
\mathbf{x}\mapsto\mathcal{W}^{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};B)
, rather than the Busemann direction. For
a\geq 0
we can alternately define
B^{a}\in\mathcal{C}^{\mathbb{N}}(\mathbb{R})
by the formula

\sum_{i=1}^{k}B^{a}_{i}(x)=\mathcal{W}^{a^{k}}(x^{k};B),\qquad k\in\mathbb{N}.

(11)

We also build environments for
a<0
. First, for an environment
f
, define
Rf(x)=f(-x)
, and for
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
, let
-\mathbf{x}=(-x_{k},\dots,-x_{1})\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
. For
\theta\in(-\infty,0]^{k}_{\leq},\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
, define

\mathcal{W}^{\theta}(\mathbf{x};B)=\mathcal{B}^{\theta|\theta|/2}(\mathbf{x};B):=\mathcal{B}^{-\theta|\theta|/2}(-\mathbf{x};RB),\qquad

(12)

See Figure
3
for an illustration of this definition when
\theta\in(-\infty,0)
. Using this definition, we can understand formula (
11
) for arbitrary
a\in\mathbb{R}
. The full suite of RSK transformations
B\mapsto B^{a},a\in\mathbb{R}
exhibits a group structure, and acts on Busemann functions via a simple parameter translation.

Theorem 2.3
.

Let
B=(B_{i},i\in\mathbb{N})
be an environment of independent two-sided Brownian motions with zero drift.
Then:

1.

(Invertibility and group structure) For any
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
, almost surely:

B^{0}=B,\qquad(B^{a})^{-a}=B,\qquad(B^{a})^{b}=B^{a+b}.

2.

(Measure preservation) For any
a\in\mathbb{R}
,
B^{a}
is a sequence of independent Brownian motions of drift
a
.

3.

(Busemann isometry) Fix
a\in\mathbb{R}
and
\lambda\in(-\infty,0]^{k}_{\leq}\cup[0,\infty)^{k}_{\leq}
. Suppose also that
\lambda+a\in(-\infty,0]^{k}_{\leq}\cup[0,\infty)^{k}_{\leq}
. Then a.s. for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
we have

\mathcal{W}^{\lambda+a}(\mathbf{x};B)=\mathcal{W}^{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};B^{a}).

Theorem
2.3
.3 also allows us to define the function
\mathcal{W}^{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};B)
when
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
but possibly has coordinates of both signs: we simply set
\mathcal{W}^{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};B)=\mathcal{W}^{\lambda-\lambda_{1}}(\mathbf{x};B^{-\lambda_{1}})
. Theorem
2.3
is proven in the text as Theorem
5.12
.

We can also take different limits of the RSK correspondence on
\mathcal{C}^{n}(\mathbb{R})
that result in new representations for the multi-path Busemann process for
B
. More precisely, let
\theta\in[0,\infty)^{k}_{\leq}
and let
B^{\prime}\sim\mu_{\theta}
. Let the transform
\mathcal{P}_{\sigma_{n}},\sigma_{n}\in\mathfrak{D}_{n+k}
be chosen so that
W^{n}:=\mathcal{P}_{\sigma_{n}}(B_{1},\dots,B_{n},B^{\prime})
satisfies
\lambda(W^{n})=(\theta,0^{n})
. Again, the maps
W^{n}-W^{n}(0)
have an almost sure limit
W
as
n\to\infty
which comes from the Busemann process for
B
. The next theorem describes this limit.

Theorem 2.4
.

Let
\theta\in[0,\infty)^{k}_{\leq}
, and define
W=(W_{1},\dots,W_{k}):\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
by the formula:

\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}W_{i}(x)=\mathcal{W}^{(\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{\ell})}(x^{\ell};B),\qquad 1\leq\ell\leq k.

Then
W-W(0)\sim\mu_{\theta}
. Moreover, consider any
\mathbf{n}\in\{1,\dots,k\}^{\ell}_{<}
for some
\ell\leq k
and suppose that
\mathbf{n}\leq\mathbf{m}
for any
\mathbf{m}\in\{1,\dots,k\}^{\ell}_{<}
with
\theta_{n_{i}}=\theta_{m_{i}}
for all
i
. Then almost surely, for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}_{\leq}
we have

\mathcal{B}^{(\theta_{n_{1}},\dots,\theta_{n_{\ell}})}(\mathbf{x};B)=W[(-\infty,\mathbf{n})\to(\mathbf{x},1)].

(13)

The identity (
13
) may look familiar to the reader in the special case when
\mathbf{n}
is a singleton. Indeed, as part of their comprehensive study of Brownian Busemann functions, Seppäläinen and Sorensen
[
SS23b
]
showed that given
\theta\in[0,\infty)^{k}_{<}
, there exists an environment
\tilde{W}\sim\mu_{\theta}
such that the following identity holds in distribution, as functions of
(x,i)\in\mathbb{R}\times\{1,\dots,k\}
.

\tilde{W}[(-\infty,i)\to(x,1)]-\tilde{W}[(-\infty,i)\to(0,1)]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{W}^{\theta_{i}}(x;B)

(14)

One upshot of Theorem
2.4
is that we realize this as an
almost sure identity
, naturally constructing
\tilde{W}
in terms of
B
.
A multi-path almost sure approach to the identity (
14
) was previously suggested in
[
Dau23b
, Remark 1.3.4]
and developed there in the context of the Airy sheet. Theorem
2.4
is proven in the text as Theorem
5.9
.

Figure 3
:
Semi-infinite geodesics in
B
, indexed by the direction
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
. To make sense of the direction when
\theta<0
, we look along down-right paths, or equivalently, paths in the reversed environment.

Figure 4
:
Semi-infinite geodesics in
\mathcal{L}
. The colouring of paths in Figures
3
and
4
is chosen so that the start points and directions match in the two pictures under the version of the RSK correspondence in Theorem
2.5
.

2.3
The final limit: RSK for the directed landscape

At this point, we have constructed analogues of the RSK correspondence on an infinite line environment on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{N}
indexed by
a\in\mathbb{R}
. We can make one final limiting transition. This transition can be viewed either by taking
a\to\pm\infty
in Theorem
2.3
, or equivalently by decreasing the spacing between lines to
0
, in a manner that takes Brownian LPP to the directed landscape. This is the goal of Section
6
.

Recall the main theorem of
[
DOV22
]
, cited above as Theorem
1.5
, which defines the directed landscape as the scaling limit of Brownian LPP. In Theorem
1.5
, the convergence in distribution is in the compact topology on
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow})
.
Just as with LPP, the directed landscape is best thought of as assigning distances to pairs of points
p=(x,s),q=(y,t)
. The value
\mathcal{L}(p;q)
is best thought of as a distance between two points
p
and
q
in the space-time plane, and indeed, it satisfies a reverse triangle inequality

\mathcal{L}(x,s;y,t)\geq\mathcal{L}(x,s;z,r)+\mathcal{L}(z,r;y,t),

(15)

just like LPP. Unlike a usual metric,
\mathcal{L}
is not symmetric, does not assign distances to every pair of points in the plane, and may take negative values.

We define the analogue of RSK for
\mathcal{L}
by modifying the Busemann definition (
11
) for the limiting context. To set things up properly we need to define path lengths, geodesics, and optimizers in the directed landscape.
For a continuous function
\pi:[s,t]\mapsto\mathbb{R}
, referred to as a
path
, let
\bar{\pi}(r)=(\pi(r),r)
. Following
[
DOV22
, Section 12]
, define the
length
of
\pi
by

\|\pi\|_{\mathcal{L}}=\inf_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\inf_{s=t_{0}<t_{1}<\ldots<t_{k}=t}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{L}(\bar{\pi}(t_{i-1});\bar{\pi}(t_{i}))\,.

(16)

Next, for
s<t
and
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
, define the
extended landscape value

\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t)=\max_{\pi=(\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{k})}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\|\pi_{i}\|_{\mathcal{L}},

(17)

where the maximum is over all
disjoint
k
-tuples
of paths
\pi=(\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{k})
with
\pi(r)\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{<}
for
r\in(s,t)
, and such that
\pi(s)=\mathbf{x},\pi(t)=\mathbf{y}
. In
[
DZ21
]
, the authors show that the maximum in (
17
) is almost surely achieved for all
s<t
and
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
. As before, we call a maximizer a
disjoint optimizer
, or
geodesic
if
k=1
. Moreover, the extended landscape is continuous, and is the limit of multi-path Brownian LPP. Using this structure, we can define (multi-path) Busemann functions in
\mathcal{L}
as in (
10
). For
\theta,\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
, let

\mathfrak{B}^{\theta}(\mathbf{x};\mathcal{L}):=\lim_{t\to-\infty}\mathcal{L}(\theta|t|,t;\mathbf{x},0)-\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathcal{L}(\hat{\theta}^{\ell_{i}}|t|,t;0^{\ell_{i}},0).

(18)

Putting aside the question of Busemann existence for one moment, by analogy with (
11
), for every
a\in\mathbb{R}
we can define an RSK map for
\mathcal{L}
. For
a\in\mathbb{R}
, define
B^{\mathcal{L},a}(\cdot)\in\mathcal{C}^{\mathbb{N}}(\mathbb{R})
by:

\sum_{i=1}^{k}B^{\mathcal{L},a}_{i}(x)=\mathfrak{B}^{(a/2)^{k}}(x;\mathcal{L}).

(19)

We can now ask for the usual properties of these maps: invertibility, measure-preservation, isometry. The next theorem gives analogues of these properties here.

Theorem 2.5
.

Let
\mathcal{L}
denote the directed landscape, and let
\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\uparrow}=\{(x,s;y,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}:s<t\leq 0\}
. For every
a\in\mathbb{R}
define an environment
B^{\mathcal{L},a}\in\mathcal{C}^{\mathbb{N}}(\mathbb{R})
as in (
19
) from
\mathcal{L}|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\uparrow}}
.
Then:

1.

(Measure preservation) For any
a\in\mathbb{R}
,
B^{\mathcal{L},a}
is a sequence of independent Brownian motions of drift
a
. Moreover, the joint law of
B^{\mathcal{L},a},a\in C
is the same as the joint law of the environments
B^{a},a\in C
in Theorem
2.3
for any countable set
C\subset\mathbb{R}
(we restrict to countable
C
to avoid topological issues). In particular, almost surely
B^{\mathcal{L},a+b}=(B^{\mathcal{L},a})^{b}
for any fixed
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
.

2.

(Busemann isometry) For any
a\in\mathbb{R}
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
, a.s. for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
we have

\mathcal{W}^{\lambda+a}(\mathbf{x};B^{\mathcal{L},a})=\mathfrak{B}^{(\lambda+a)/2}(\mathbf{x};\mathcal{L}).

3.

(Invertibility) If we let the environment
\mathcal{L}^{a}|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\uparrow}}
be defined as in Theorem
1.5
where
B^{a}=B^{\mathcal{L},a}
, then

\mathcal{L}^{a}|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\uparrow}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\to}}\mathcal{L}|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\uparrow}}

as
a\to-\infty
, where the convergence in probability is in the compact topology on functions from
\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\uparrow}\to\mathbb{R}
. Since any two of the environments
B^{\mathcal{L},a},B^{\mathcal{L},b}
are a.s. measurable functions of each other by part
1
, this implies that for every
a_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
there is a measurable map
f:\mathcal{C}^{\mathbb{N}}(\mathbb{R})\to\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\uparrow})
such that
\mathcal{L}|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\uparrow}}=f(B^{\mathcal{L},a_{0}})
a.s.

Theorem
2.5
contains Theorem
1.6
, and is proven in the text as part of the stronger Theorem
6.8
.
To understand where Theorem
2.5
comes from, we should examine what happens to the Busemann functions for
B^{a}
under the scaling in Theorem
1.5
. If we momentarily assume that we can exchange the order of the
t\to-\infty
limit in (
10
) and the
a\to-\infty
limit in Theorem
1.5
, we can use the coupling of the environments
B^{a}
in Theorem
2.3
to see that:

\begin{split}\mathfrak{B}^{\theta}(\mathbf{x};\mathcal{L})&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\lim_{a\to-\infty}\mathcal{B}^{a^{3}/(2a+8\theta)}(\mathbf{x};B^{a})=\lim_{a\to-\infty}\mathcal{W}^{|a|\sqrt{1/(1+4\theta/a)}}(\mathbf{x};B^{a})\\
&=\lim_{a\to-\infty}\mathcal{W}^{|a|\sqrt{1/(1+4\theta/a)}+a}(\mathbf{x};B)=\mathcal{W}^{2\theta}(\mathbf{x};B).\end{split}

(20)

Putting this together gives a candidate for the law of the extended Busemann process
\mathfrak{B}^{\theta}(\mathbf{x};\mathcal{L})
, which is verified by Theorem
2.5
.1,2. Comparing Figures
3
and
4
illustrates this part of the theorem. The law we have identified is consistent with known results about the single-path Busemann process in the directed landscape
[
RV21
,
Bus21
,
BSS24
]
. In
[
Bus21
]
, the process
\mathfrak{B}^{\theta}(x,\mathcal{L})
for
\theta,x\in\mathbb{R}
was termed the stationary horizon, so naturally, we call the extended process
(\theta,\mathbf{x})\mapsto\mathfrak{B}^{\theta}(\mathbf{x},\mathcal{L})
for arbitrary
(\theta,\mathbf{x})\in\bigcup_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
the
extended stationary horizon
.

Now, given Theorem
2.5
.1,2, the coupling of the environments
\mathcal{L}^{a}
through the environments
B^{\mathcal{L},a}
gives a sequence of maps converging to
\mathcal{L}
in law, and whose multi-path Busemann functions converge in probability. One may optimistically expect that in such a coupling, we also have convergence in probability of the directed landscape on the whole lower half-plane. Indeed, this is the case, and is verified by Theorem
2.5
.3, using the truss-slit framework described in Section
1.4
. See Sections
6.3
,
6.4
,
6.5
for details.

3
Preliminaries

In this section, we gather a few basic results about LPP and the directed landscape. For basic definitions, we refer the reader back to the introduction.

3.1
Basics of last passage percolation

Recall the definition of LPP, multi-path LPP, geodesics, and semi-infinite optimizers in Section
2.1
. We use the following basic facts about these objects throughout the paper. We state all results here for
k
-path optimizers and multi-path last passage values. When
k=1
, these results specialize to the simpler setting of geodesics and last passage values. Throughout Section
3.1
, we fix
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathbb{R})
. All results will also apply for
f\in\mathcal{C}^{I}(\mathbb{R})
for integer intervals
I
, when the relevant objects are well-defined. For
\mathbf{p}=(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{n}),\mathbf{q}=(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{m})\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}\times\mathbb{Z}^{k}_{\leq}
, we call
(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q})
an
endpoint pair
if there is at least one disjoint
k
-tuple from
\mathbf{p}
to
\mathbf{q}
.

Lemma 3.1

(Optimizer existence: Lemma 2.2,
[
DZ21
]
)
.

Let
(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q})
be an endpoint pair. Then there exists an optimizer
\pi^{L}
from
\mathbf{p}
to
\mathbf{q}
such that
\pi^{L}\leq\pi
for any optimizer
\pi
from
\mathbf{p}
to
\mathbf{q}
. We call
\pi^{L}
the
leftmost
optimizer. Similarly, there exists a
rightmost
optimizer
\pi^{R}
from
\mathbf{p}
to
\mathbf{q}
such that
\pi^{R}\geq\pi
for any optimizer
\pi
from
\mathbf{p}
to
\mathbf{q}
.

Lemma 3.2

(Optimizer monotonicity: Lemma 2.3,
[
DZ21
]
)
.

Let
(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q})
and
(\mathbf{p}^{\prime},\mathbf{q}^{\prime})
be two endpoint pairs of sizes
k
. Suppose that:

1.

\mathbf{p}=(\mathbf{x},n)
and
\mathbf{p}^{\prime}=(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},n)
for some
\mathbf{x}\leq\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
, or else
\mathbf{p}=(x,\mathbf{n})
and
\mathbf{p}^{\prime}=(x,\mathbf{n}^{\prime})
for some
\mathbf{n}\leq\mathbf{n}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}_{\leq}
.

2.

\mathbf{q}=(\mathbf{y},n)
and
\mathbf{q}^{\prime}=(\mathbf{y}^{\prime},n)
for some
\mathbf{y}\leq\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
, or else
\mathbf{q}=(y,\mathbf{m})
and
\mathbf{q}^{\prime}=(y,\mathbf{m}^{\prime})
for some
\mathbf{m}\leq\mathbf{m}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}_{\leq}
.

Then letting
\pi
be the rightmost optimizer from
\mathbf{p}
to
\mathbf{q}
, and
\pi^{\prime}
be the rightmost optimizer from
\mathbf{p}^{\prime}
to
\mathbf{q}^{\prime}
we have that
\pi\leq\pi^{\prime}
.

Technically,
[
DZ21
, Lemma 2.2]
only applies when
\mathbf{p}=(\mathbf{x},n),\mathbf{p}^{\prime}=(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},n)
and
\mathbf{q}=(\mathbf{y},n),\mathbf{q}^{\prime}=(\mathbf{y}^{\prime},n)
. However, the proof goes through verbatim is the slightly more general setting above.

Lemma 3.3

(Metric composition law)
.

Let
(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q})=(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{n};\mathbf{y},\mathbf{m})
be an endpoint pair of size
k
and let
\ell\in\{m_{k}+1,\dots,n_{1}\}
. Then

f[\mathbf{p}\to\mathbf{q}]=\max_{\mathbf{z}}f[\mathbf{p}\to(\mathbf{z},\ell)]+f[(\mathbf{z},\ell-1)\to\mathbf{q}],

where the maximum is taken over
\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
such that both
(\mathbf{p};\mathbf{z},\ell)
and
(\mathbf{z},\ell-1;\mathbf{q})
are endpoint pairs. Similarly, if
z\in[x_{k},y_{1}]
, then

f[\mathbf{p}\to\mathbf{q}]=\max_{\mathbf{l}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}_{<}}f[\mathbf{p}\to(z,\mathbf{l})]+f[(z,\mathbf{l})\to\mathbf{q}],

where the maximum is taken over
\mathbf{l}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}_{<}
such that both
(\mathbf{p};z,\mathbf{l})
and
(z,\mathbf{l};\mathbf{q})
are endpoint pairs.

Lemma 3.4

(Quadrangle inequality, Lemma 2.4,
[
DZ21
]
)
.

Let
(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q})=(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{n};\mathbf{y},\mathbf{m})
,
(\mathbf{p}^{\prime},\mathbf{q}^{\prime})=(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{n}^{\prime};\mathbf{y}^{\prime},\mathbf{m}^{\prime})
be endpoint pairs satisfying the conditions of Lemma
3.2
. Suppose that
(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}^{\prime})
and
(\mathbf{p}^{\prime},\mathbf{q})
are also endpoint pairs. Then:

f[\mathbf{p}\to\mathbf{q}^{\prime}]+f[\mathbf{p}^{\prime}\to\mathbf{q}]\leq f[\mathbf{p}\to\mathbf{q}]+f[\mathbf{p}^{\prime}\to\mathbf{q}^{\prime}]

with equality if there exists a point
z\in[x_{k}^{\prime},y_{1}]
and optimizers
\pi
from
\mathbf{p}
to
\mathbf{q}
,
\pi^{\prime}
from
\mathbf{p}^{\prime}
to
\mathbf{q}^{\prime}
such that
\pi(z)=\pi^{\prime}(z)
.

The ‘equality if’ claim in Lemma
3.4
is not contained in
[
DZ21
]
, but it is easy to see. In this case, the paths
\pi|_{(-\infty,z)}\oplus\pi^{\prime}|_{[z,\infty)}
and
\pi^{\prime}|_{(-\infty,z)}\oplus\pi|_{[z,\infty)}
(here
\oplus
denotes paths concatenation), are disjoint
k
-tuples from
\mathbf{p}
to
\mathbf{q}^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{p}^{\prime}
to
\mathbf{q}
whose lengths sum to
f[\mathbf{p}\to\mathbf{q}]+f[\mathbf{p}^{\prime}\to\mathbf{q}^{\prime}]
, proving the reverse inequality in Lemma
3.4
. We end with a straightforward statement regarding last passage across lines and common shifts of the environment, whose proof we leave to the reader.

Lemma 3.5

(Last passage commutes with shifts)
.

Let
g:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be any continuous function, and let
f+g\in\mathcal{C}^{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathbb{R})
be given by
(f_{i}+g,i\in\mathbb{Z})
. Then for any endpoint pairs
\mathbf{p}=(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{n}),\mathbf{q}=(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{m})\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}\times\mathbb{Z}^{k}_{\leq}
, we have:

(f+g)[\mathbf{p}\to\mathbf{q}]=f[\mathbf{p}\to\mathbf{q}]+\sum_{i=1}^{k}[g(y_{i})-g(x_{i})].

We will typically use Lemma
3.5
when
g
is a constant or linear function. We will typically refer to these five results without reference, as they should be viewed as part of the basic language for working with geodesics and optimizers.

3.2
Shape and continuity bounds for Brownian LPP

We will need two complementary limit shape theorems for Brownian LPP. For the first proposition, for
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
x,\alpha,b,w>0
we define

\mathcal{N}_{b,w}(n,x,a)=\sqrt{8nx}+\sqrt{x}n^{-1/6}(a+b\log^{2/3}(n^{1/3}|\log(x/w)|+1)).

Note that for any
\alpha>0
, we have

\mathcal{N}_{b,\alpha w}(n,\alpha x,a)=\sqrt{\alpha}\mathcal{N}_{b,w}(n,x,a).

(21)

Proposition 3.6

(Proposition 4.3,
[
DV21a
]
)
.

There exist positive constants
b,c
and
d
such that for all
w,a>0
and
n\geq 1
, the probability that

B[(-x,n)\to(0,1)]\leq\mathcal{N}_{b,w}(n,x,a),\;\;\forall x\in(0,\infty)

is greater than or equal to
1-ce^{-da^{3/2}}
.

The bound in Proposition
3.6
is chosen to minimize the error term at
x=w
. Note that in
[
DV21a
]
, Proposition
3.6
is stated when
w=1
. The general case follows by Brownian scaling. We will typically use Proposition
3.6
when
w=n/\theta
for some
\theta>0
, in which case the mean term
\sqrt{8nx}\sim n\sqrt{8/\theta}
when
x\sim w
and the error term is
\sqrt{xn^{-1/6}}=O(n^{1/3})
, as is expected for KPZ models. Note that Proposition
3.6
also provides a uniform upper bound on multi-path last passage values, by virtue of the bound

B[(\mathbf{x},n)\to(\mathbf{y},1)]\leq\sum_{i=1}^{k}B[(x_{i},n)\to(y_{i},1)].

A corresponding lower bound also holds. We only state this at the level of the one-point bound.

Proposition 3.7

(Lemma A.4,
[
DZ21
]
)
.

For every
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there exist positive constants
c_{k},d_{k},k\in\mathbb{N}
such that the following holds. For all
m>0
and all endpoint pairs
(\mathbf{x},n),(\mathbf{y},1)
with
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
we have

\mathbb{P}\left(|B[(\mathbf{x},n)\to(\mathbf{y},1)]-\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sqrt{8n(x_{i}-y_{i})}|\geq m\sqrt{x}n^{-1/6}\right)\leq c_{k}e^{-d_{k}m^{3/2}}.

Note that in the statement of Lemma A.4,
[
DV21a
]
, there are restrictions on
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}
. However, these restrictions are not used in the proof of that lemma. A typical application of Propositions
3.6
and
3.7
will be to bound the location of the argmax in the metric composition law from Brownian LPP. This is why we need a uniform upper bound but only a pointwise lower bound.

3.3
The directed landscape

In this final preliminary section, we collect basic results about the directed landscape. We start with the axiomatic description of
\mathcal{L}
in terms of the marginal
\mathcal{S}(x,y)=\mathcal{L}(x,0;y,1)
, known as the
Airy sheet
. We have the following uniqueness theorem, see Definition 10.1 and Theorem 10.9 of
[
DOV22
]
.

Theorem 3.8
.

The directed landscape
\mathcal{L}:\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}\to\mathbb{R}
is the unique random continuous function satisfying:

1.

(Airy sheet marginals) For any
t\in\mathbb{R}
and
s>0
we have

\mathcal{\mathcal{L}}(x,t;y,t+s^{3})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}s\mathcal{S}(x/s^{2},y/s^{2})

jointly in all
x,y
. That is, the increment over time interval
[t,t+s^{3})
is an
Airy sheet of scale
s
.

2.

(Independent increments) For disjoint time intervals
\{[t_{i},s_{i}]:i\in\{1,\dots k\}\}
, the random functions

\{\mathcal{L}(\cdot,t_{i};\cdot,s_{i}):i\in\{1,\dots,k\}\}

are independent.

3.

(Metric composition law) Almost surely, for any
r<s<t
and
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
we have that

\mathcal{L}(x,r;y,t)=\max_{z\in\mathbb{R}}[\mathcal{L}(x,r;z,s)+\mathcal{L}(z,s;y,t)].

Note that Definition 10.1 in
[
DOV22
]
states the independent increment property for disjoint open intervals, rather than closed intervals. The two are equivalent by continuity. The directed landscape has invariance properties which we use throughout the paper.

Lemma 3.9

(Lemma 10.2,
[
DOV22
]
)
.

We have the following equalities in distribution as random functions in
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow})
. Here
r,c\in\mathbb{R}
, and
q>0
.

1.

(Time stationarity)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(x,t;y,t+s)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{L}(x,t+r;y,t+s+r).

2.

(Spatial stationarity)

\mathcal{L}(x,t;y,t+s)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{L}(x+c,t;y+c,t+s).

3.

(Flip symmetry)

\mathcal{L}(x,t;y,t+s)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{L}(-y,-s-t;-x,-t).

4.

(Shear stationarity)

\mathcal{L}(x,t;y,t+s)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{L}(x+ct,t;y+ct+sc,t+s)+s^{-1}[(x-y)^{2}-(x-y-sc)^{2}].

5.

(
1:2:3
rescaling)

\mathcal{L}(x,t;y,t+s)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}q\mathcal{L}(q^{-2}x,q^{-3}t;q^{-2}y,q^{-3}(t+s)).

As discussed in the introduction (see (
16
) and surrounding discussion), we can define path length and geodesics in the directed landscape. We record one strong convergence lemma for geodesics that will be used in Section
6.1
.

Lemma 3.10
.

For two
\mathcal{L}
-geodesics
\pi:[s,t]\to\mathbb{R},\gamma:[s^{\prime},t^{\prime}]\to\mathbb{R}
, define the overlap
O(\pi,\gamma)
to be the closure of the set
\{r\in(s,t)\cap(s^{\prime},t^{\prime}):\pi(r)=\gamma(r)\}
. Also let
\Gamma(S)
denote the set of geodesics with endpoints in a set
S\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
.
Then the following claims hold almost surely:

1.

(Theorem 1,
[
Bha24
]
or Lemma 3.3.2,
[
Dau23a
]
) For any
\mathcal{L}
-geodesics
\pi,\gamma
,
O(\pi,\gamma)
is always a closed interval.

2.

(Part of Proposition 3.5,
[
Dau23a
]
) For two geodesics,
\gamma:I\to\mathbb{R},\pi:I^{\prime}\to\mathbb{R}
, define

d_{o}(\gamma,\pi)=\lambda(I)+\lambda(I^{\prime})-2\lambda(O(\pi,\gamma)),

For any compact set
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
, this is a metric on
\Gamma(K)
that turns
\Gamma(K)
into a compact Polish space.

Lemma
3.10
.2 ensures that any pointwise limit of a sequence of geodesics is also a geodesic, and moreover, is also a limit in the stronger sense of overlap. The overlap structure of landscape geodesics allows us to treat them more like their discrete counterparts in LPP.

We move on to bounds on the extended directed landscape, see (
17
) for the definition. We start with a version of Theorem
1.5
for the full extended landscape. For this theorem, recall the scaling
(\mathbf{x},s)_{a}=(\mathbf{x}-as/4,{\left\lfloor sa^{3}/8\right\rfloor})
. In the introduction, we used this notation only for singletons
\mathbf{x}
. Here we extend its use to vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
. We also let
\mathfrak{X}_{\uparrow}
be the space of all points
(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t)
, where
s<t\in\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}
lie in the same space
\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

Theorem 3.11

(Theorem 1.5/1.6,
[
DZ21
]
)
.

For every
a\in\mathbb{R}
, let
B^{a}=(B^{a}_{i}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R},i\in\mathbb{Z})
be an environment of independent two-sided Brownian motions of common drift
a
, and for a point
(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t)\in\mathfrak{X}_{\uparrow}
with
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
, define

\mathcal{L}^{a}(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t)=B^{a}[(\mathbf{x},s)_{a}\to(\mathbf{y},t)_{a}]-\frac{ka^{2}}{4}(t-s),

when the right-hand side exists, and simply set
\mathcal{L}^{a}(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t)=-\infty
otherwise.
Then as
a\to-\infty
,

\mathcal{L}^{a}(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t),

where the underlying topology is compact convergence of functions on
\mathfrak{X}_{\uparrow}
.

Theorem
3.11
is stated with drift-free Brownian motions in
[
DZ21
]
. The translation between the two theorems is immediate since last passage commutes with common shifts of the environment (Lemma
3.5
).

Analogues of Lemmas
3.1
,
3.2
,
3.3
, and
3.4
hold in the directed landscape, and we record here the results we need in that context.

Lemma 3.12

(Optimizer existence in
\mathcal{L}
: Theorem 1.7,
[
DZ21
]
)
.

Almost surely, for every choice of
(\mathbf{x},s,\mathbf{y},t)

there is a disjoint
k
-tuple
\pi=(\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{k})
with

\sum_{i=1}^{k}\|\pi\|_{\mathcal{L}}=\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t).

That is, the maximum (
17
) is attained for every
\mathbf{x},s,\mathbf{y},t
. For any fixed
(\mathbf{x},s,\mathbf{y},t)
, almost surely this
disjoint optimizer
is unique.

Unlike in the semi-discrete setting, Lemma
3.1
is actually quite a difficult result. Its proof takes up a large part of the paper
[
DZ21
]
. In the
k=1
case of geodesics, the result is easier, and was proven in
[
DOV22
, Theorem 1.7, Lemma 13.2]
, where the existence of leftmost and rightmost geodesics is also shown. Because Lemma
3.12
does not give the existence of leftmost and rightmost optimizers, the analogue of optimizer monotonicity for
\mathcal{L}
is slightly more subtle.

Lemma 3.13

(Optimizer monotonicity in
\mathcal{L}
: see Lemma 7.7,
[
DZ21
]
)
.

Almost surely the following holds in
\mathcal{L}
.
Let
\mathbf{x}\leq\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
,
\mathbf{y}\leq\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
,
s<t
, and let
\pi,\pi^{\prime}
be optimizers in
\mathcal{L}
from
(\mathbf{x},s)
to
(\mathbf{y},t)
and
(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},s)
to
(\mathbf{y}^{\prime},t)
, respectively. Then if either
\pi
or
\pi^{\prime}
is the
unique
optimizer between its endpoints, we have
\pi\leq\pi^{\prime}
.

Observe that in the setting of Lemma
3.2
, we can also conclude that
\pi\leq\pi^{\prime}
if there exists
\mathbf{x}^{\prime\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime\prime}
with
\mathbf{x}\leq\mathbf{x}^{\prime\prime}\leq\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{y}\leq\mathbf{y}^{\prime\prime}\leq\mathbf{y}^{\prime}
such that there is a unique optimizer from
(\mathbf{x}^{\prime\prime},s)
to
(\mathbf{y}^{\prime\prime},t)
. Such
\mathbf{x}^{\prime\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime\prime}
will exist almost surely for all
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime}
for which
x_{i}<x_{i}^{\prime},y_{i}<y_{i}^{\prime}
for all
i
.

Lemma 3.14

(Metric composition law, Proposition 6.9,
[
DZ21
]
)
.

Almost surely, for every
r<s<t
and
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
we have

\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{x},r;\mathbf{y},t)=\max_{z\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{<}}\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{x},r;\mathbf{z},s)+\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{z},s;\mathbf{y},t).

Lemma 3.15

(Quadrangle inequality, Lemma 5.7,
[
DZ21
]
)
.

Almost surely, for all
\mathbf{x}\leq\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{y}\leq\mathbf{y}^{\prime}
in
\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
and
s<t
, we have

\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y}^{\prime},t)+\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},s;\mathbf{y},t)\leq\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t)+\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},s;\mathbf{y}^{\prime},t).

We end with a shape bound on the extended directed landscape.

Lemma 3.16

(Lemma 6.7,
[
DZ21
]
)
.

For any
\eta>0
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there is a random constant
R>1
, such that for any
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
and
s<t
, we have

\left|\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t)+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\frac{(x_{i}-y_{i})^{2}}{t-s}\right|<RG(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},s,t)^{\eta}(t-s)^{1/3}

where

G(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},s,t)=\left(1+\frac{\|\mathbf{x}\|_{1}+\|\mathbf{y}\|_{1}}{(t-s)^{2/3}}\right)\left(1+\frac{|s|}{t-s}\right)(1+|\log(t-s)|).

Also
\mathbb{P}(R>a)<ce^{-da}
for any
a>0
, where
c,d>0
are constants depending on
k,\eta
.

4
The biHecke monoid and RSK on
\mathcal{C}^{n}(\mathbb{R})

In this section we build up the theory of Pitman transforms and the biHecke monoid. One aspect of this theory is a version of the RSK correspondence for functions
f\in\mathcal{C}^{n}(\mathbb{R})
. However, the theory as a whole offers a much richer picture.

4.1
An abstract theory of sorting

Let
S_{n}
denote the symmetric group with
n
elements, and let
\pi_{i}=(i,i+1)
denote the adjacent transposition in
S_{n}
swapping
i,i+1
. The adjacent transpositions
\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{n-1}
generate the symmetric group, together with the relations

\displaystyle\pi_{i}\pi_{i+1}\pi_{i}

\displaystyle=\pi_{i+1}\pi_{i}\pi_{i+1},\quad

\displaystyle i=1,\dots,n-2\qquad

(Braid relation)

(22)

\displaystyle\pi_{i}\pi_{j}

\displaystyle=\pi_{j}\pi_{i},\quad

\displaystyle|i-j|\geq 2\qquad

(Commutation relation)

(23)

\displaystyle\pi_{i}^{2}

\displaystyle=\text{id},\quad

\displaystyle i=1,\dots,n-1\qquad

(Involution relation)

(24)

Now, let

G(S_{n})

be the monoid of all functions
f:S_{n}\to S_{n}
(with the operation of function composition). For
i=1,\dots,n-1
define the
adjacent sorting operator

\tau_{i}\in G(S_{n})
by:

\displaystyle\tau_{i}(\chi)=\begin{cases}\chi,\qquad&\chi^{-1}(i)>\chi^{-1}(i+1),\\
\pi_{i}\chi,\qquad&\chi^{-1}(i)<\chi^{-1}(i+1).\end{cases}

Define the
0
-Hecke monoid

\mathfrak{M}_{n}
as the submonoid of
G(S_{n})
generated by the elements
\tau_{i},i=1,\dots,n-1
, see
[
Nor79
]
.
The following description of the operators
\tau_{i}
may also be enlightening. Recall that the inversion number of a permutation
\pi
is given by:

\operatorname{Inv}(\pi)=\#\{(i,j)\in\{1,\dots,n\}^{2}:i<j,\pi(i)>\pi(j)\}.

Then the operator
\tau_{i}
takes the permutation
\chi
and applies the adjacent transposition
\pi_{i}
if and only if doing so increases the inversion number. The effect of this is that composition by the element
\tau_{i}
pushes
\chi
further away from the identity permutation and closer to the reverse permutation
n\cdots 1
. From this point of view, we can think of repeated applications of
\tau_{i}
as sorting into reverse order.

Abstractly the
0
-Hecke monoid can be given by a set of relations that is quite similar to the relations above for the symmetric group. Indeed, the
\tau_{i}
still satisfy the braid and commutation relations (
22
) and (
23
), but rather than satisfying the involution relation (
24
), they satisfy an idempotent relation:

\displaystyle\tau_{i}^{2}

\displaystyle=\tau_{i},\quad

\displaystyle i=1,\dots,n-1\qquad

(Idempotent relation)

(25)

Because the relations for the
\tau_{i}
are so similar to the relations for the adjacent transpositions
\pi_{i}
, the theory of the
0
-Hecke monoid is closely related to the theory of the symmetric group itself. In fact, this theory is almost equivalent to the study of reduced decompositions of elements of the symmetric group, and the map
\pi\mapsto\pi(\text{id})
defines a bijection from
\mathfrak{M}_{n}\to S_{n}
that commutes with both the braid and commutation relations.

The RSK correspondence across lines can be viewed as arising from an action of
\mathfrak{M}_{n}
. This perspective was taken up in
[
BBO05
]
. In our setting, we will need to consider a related action of a larger monoid in
G(S_{n})
.
Define the
reverse adjacent sorting operator

\bar{\tau}_{i}\in G(S_{n})
by

\displaystyle\bar{\tau}_{i}(\chi)=\begin{cases}\chi,\qquad&\chi^{-1}(i)<\chi^{-1}(i+1),\\
\pi_{i}\chi,\qquad&\chi^{-1}(i)>\chi^{-1}(i+1).\end{cases}

The operators
\bar{\tau}_{i}
sort permutations towards the identity, rather than towards the reverse permutation. Let the
biHecke monoid

\mathfrak{D}_{n}
be the submonoid of
G(S_{n})
generated by the elements
\tau_{i},\bar{\tau}_{i},i=1,\dots,n-1
, see
[
HST13
]
. Unlike with
\mathfrak{M}_{n}
, it does not seem straightforward to describe the biHecke monoid in terms of a set of relations or to relate it directly to the symmetric group. Because of this, we will need the following abstract proposition in order to define actions of
\mathfrak{D}_{n}
.

Proposition 4.1
.

Let
D
be a partially ordered set and suppose that we have maps
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{i}:D^{n}\to D^{n}
,
\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{i}:D^{n}\to D^{n}
,
i=1,\ldots n-1
satisfying the following conditions.

(i)

The maps
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{i}
satisfy the braid, commutation, and idempotent relations (
22
), (
23
), (
25
) as do the maps
\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{i}
. Moreover, we have the mixed commutation relation
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{i}\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{j}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{j}{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{i}
whenever
|i-j|\geq 2
.

(ii)

{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{i}d=d
if
d_{i}\not>d_{i+1}
and
\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{i}d=d
if
d_{i}\not<d_{i+1}
.

(iii)

For
d\in D^{n}
, let
\Xi_{d}
be the set of elements
\xi\in S_{n}
such that for
i,j\in\{1,\dots,n\}
we have

d_{i}<d_{j}\qquad\implies\qquad\xi^{-1}(i)<\xi^{-1}(j).

Then for any
d\in D^{n}
and
i=1,\dots,n-1
we have

\Xi_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{i}d}=\tau_{i}\Xi_{d}
and
\Xi_{\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{i}d}=\bar{\tau}_{i}\Xi_{d}
.

(iv)

{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{i}\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{i}d=d
if
d_{i}\not<d_{i+1}
and
\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{i}{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{i}d=d
if
d_{i}\not>d_{i+1}
.

Then:

(I)

For a word
\mathbf{w}
in
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{i},\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{i}
, let
w
be the corresponding word in
\tau_{i},\bar{\tau}_{i}
in
\mathfrak{D}_{n}
. For any
d\in D^{n}
,

\Xi_{\mathbf{w}d}=w\Xi_{d}.

(II)

Consider
d\in D^{n}
, words
\mathbf{w},\mathbf{w}^{\prime}
in
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{i},\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{i}
and let
w,w^{\prime}
be the corresponding words in
\tau_{i},\bar{\tau}_{i}
in
\mathfrak{D}_{n}
. Suppose that there exists
\xi\in\Xi_{d}
such that
w\xi=w^{\prime}\xi
. Then
\mathbf{w}d=\mathbf{w}^{\prime}d
. In particular, if
w,w^{\prime}
evaluate to the same word in
\mathfrak{D}_{n}
, then
\mathbf{w},\mathbf{w}^{\prime}
define the same map from
D^{n}\to D^{n}
.

(III)

The map
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{i}\mapsto\tau_{i}
,
\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{i}\mapsto\bar{\tau}_{i}
extends to a monoid homomorphism between the monoid
T
generated by the maps
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{i},\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{i},

i=1,\ldots,n-1
and the biHecke monoid
\mathfrak{D}_{n}
. If there is an element
d\in D^{n}
with
d_{1}<d_{2}<\dots<d_{n}
then this is a monoid isomorphism.

Throughout the proof (and in claims (I, II) above) , we use the convention that for a word
\mathbf{w}=\mathbf{w}_{k}\cdots\mathbf{w}_{1}
where
\mathbf{w}_{i}\in\{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{i},\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{i}:i=1,\dots,n-1\}
, we write
w=w_{k}\cdots w_{1}
for the word in
\mathfrak{D}_{n}
given by taking
\mathbf{w}
and replacing all instances of
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{i},\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{i}
with
\tau_{i},\bar{\tau}_{i}
. Similarly, we write
\tilde{w}=\tilde{w}_{k}\cdots\tilde{w}_{1}
for the word in
S_{n}
given by mapping both
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{i},\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{i}
to the adjacent transposition
\pi_{i}=(i,i+1)
.

Proof.

Part (I).
By induction, it suffices to check (I) for
\mathbf{w}={\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{i}
and
\mathbf{w}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{i}
, in which case it is the content of (iii).

Part (II).
Let
\xi\in\Xi_{d}
be such that
w\xi=w^{\prime}\xi
. Let us call the letter
w_{\ell}
in the word
w_{k}\cdots w_{1}

ineffective
for
d
if
w_{\ell}\cdots w_{1}\xi=w_{\ell-1}\cdots w_{1}\xi
. We call a word
w
effective for
d
if it has no ineffective letters. By part (I), we have that

w_{\ell-1}\cdots w_{1}\xi\in\Xi_{\mathbf{w}_{\ell-1}\cdots\mathbf{w}_{1}d}
. Therefore by (ii), we have that
\mathbf{w}_{\ell}\cdots\mathbf{w}_{1}d=\mathbf{w}_{\ell-1}\cdots\mathbf{w}_{1}d
. Therefore, we may drop all ineffective letters from
w,w^{\prime}
along with the corresponding letters from
\mathbf{w},\mathbf{w}^{\prime}
without changing
w\xi,w^{\prime}\xi,\mathbf{w}d,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}d
. In other words, to prove part (II) it suffices to show that

w\xi=w^{\prime}\xi\qquad\implies\qquad\mathbf{w}d=\mathbf{w}^{\prime}d

(26)

whenever
w,w^{\prime}
have no ineffective letters.

Now, suppose that the left equation in (
26
) holds for two words
w,w^{\prime}
which are effective for
d
. The effectiveness of
w,w^{\prime}
implies that every letter in
w,w^{\prime}
acts as an adjacent transposition in the composition
w\xi,w^{\prime}\xi
and so

w\xi=\tilde{w}\xi,\qquad w^{\prime}\xi(d)=\tilde{w}^{\prime}\xi.

This implies that
\tilde{w}\xi=\tilde{w}^{\prime}\xi
, and so
\tilde{w},\tilde{w}^{\prime}
represent the same element of the symmetric group. Therefore there is a sequence of words
\tilde{w}=u^{1},\dots,\tilde{w}^{\prime}=u^{k}
in the alphabet
\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{n-1}
such that for all
i=1,\dots,k
,
\tilde{w}_{i}
and
u^{i},u^{i+1}
differ from each other by one of the relations (
22
), (
23
), (
24
).

Next, given each of the words
u^{i}
in the
\pi_{i}
, there is a unique way to change each letter
\pi_{i}
to either
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{i}
or
\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{i}
so that every letter in the resulting word
w^{i}
is effective. Moreover, this change is consistent in the sense that the assignment of bars agrees on consecutive words
w^{i},w^{i-1}
everywhere except where the relation is used.
This procedure also gives rise to a sequence of words
\mathbf{w}=\mathbf{w}^{1},\dots,\mathbf{w}^{k}=\mathbf{w}^{\prime}
in
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{i},\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{i}
.

To complete the proof of (II), we just need to show that
\mathbf{w}^{i}d=\mathbf{w}^{i-1}d
for all
i=2,\dots,k
. First suppose that
u^{i-1},u^{i}
differ by a commutation relation (
23
) so that
u^{i-1}=\tilde{a}\pi_{\ell}\pi_{j}\tilde{b},u^{i}=\tilde{a}\pi_{j}\pi_{\ell}\tilde{b}
. Then there is a unique choice of
f(\pi_{\ell})\in\{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{\ell},\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{\ell}\}
and
f(\pi_{j})\in\{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{j},\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{j}\}
such that

\mathbf{w}^{i-1}=\mathbf{a}f(\pi_{\ell})f(\pi_{j})\mathbf{b},\qquad\mathbf{w}^{i}=\mathbf{a}f(\pi_{j})f(\pi_{\ell})\mathbf{b}.

By the commutation relation in (i), we have that
\mathbf{w}^{i-1}=\mathbf{w}^{i}
. Next, suppose that
u^{i-1},u^{i}
differ by an involution relation (
24
) so that
u^{i-1}=\tilde{a}\pi_{j}^{2}\tilde{b},u^{i}=\tilde{a}\tilde{b}
(or vice versa, with the
\pi_{j}^{2}
on
u^{i}
). Then
\mathbf{w}^{i}=\mathbf{a}\mathbf{b}
and either

\mathbf{w}^{i-1}=\mathbf{a}{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{j}\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{j}\mathbf{b}\qquad\text{ or }\qquad\mathbf{w}^{i-1}=\mathbf{a}{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{j}\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{j}\mathbf{b}.

Without loss of generality, assume that
\mathbf{w}^{i-1}=\mathbf{a}{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{j}\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{j}\mathbf{b}
. First, since the word
w^{i}
is effective for
d
we have that
\bar{\tau}_{j}b\xi\neq b\xi
and so
[b\xi]^{-1}(j)>[b\xi]^{-1}(j+1).
Now by part i,
b\xi\in\Xi_{\mathbf{b}d}
, and so
\mathbf{b}d_{j}\not<\mathbf{b}d_{j+1}
.
Therefore by (iv),
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{j}\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{j}
acts as the identity in
\mathbf{w}^{i-1}d
, and so
\mathbf{w}^{i-1}d=\mathbf{w}^{i}d
.

Finally, suppose that
u^{i-1},u^{i}
differ by a braid relation (
22
), so that
u^{i-1}=\tilde{a}\pi_{j}\pi_{j+1}\pi_{j}\tilde{b}
and
u^{i}=\tilde{a}\pi_{j+1}\pi_{j}\pi_{j+1}\tilde{b}
(or vice versa). Without loss of generality, we may assume
j=1
.
Here we divide into cases based on the relative order of
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}
, where
\alpha_{i}:=[b\xi]^{-1}(i)
, as the relative order of the
\alpha_{i}
determines the barring on the operators
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{2}
in
\mathbf{w}^{i},\mathbf{w}^{i-1}
.

Case 1:

\alpha_{1}<\alpha_{2}<\alpha_{3}
or
\alpha_{1}>\alpha_{2}>\alpha_{3}
.   In the first of these cases,
\mathbf{w}^{i-1}=\mathbf{a}{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{1}{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{2}{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{1}\mathbf{b}
and
\mathbf{w}^{i}=\mathbf{a}{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{2}{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{1}{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{2}\mathbf{b}
and in the second of these cases,
\mathbf{w}^{i-1}=\mathbf{a}\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{1}\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{2}\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{1}\mathbf{b}
and
\mathbf{w}^{i}=\mathbf{a}\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{2}\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{1}\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{2}\mathbf{b}
. In either case,
\mathbf{w}^{i-1}=\mathbf{w}^{i}
by the braid relation in (i).

Case 2:

\alpha_{3}<\alpha_{1}<\alpha_{2}
or
\alpha_{3}>\alpha_{1}>\alpha_{2}
.   We only deal with the first of these options, since as in Case 1, the second option is symmetric after swapping bars and non-bars. In this case,
\mathbf{w}^{i-1}=\mathbf{a}\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{1}\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{2}{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{1}\mathbf{b}
and
\mathbf{w}^{i}=\mathbf{a}{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{2}\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{1}\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{2}\mathbf{b}
. Letting
e=\mathbf{b}d
, we have that
b\xi\in\Xi_{e}
, and so

e_{3}\not>e_{1},\qquad e_{3}\not>e_{2},\qquad e_{1}\not>e_{2}.

Now, we have that

{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{2}\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{1}\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{2}e={\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{2}\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{1}\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{2}\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{1}{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{1}e={\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{2}\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{2}\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{1}\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{2}{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{1}e.

Here the first equality follows from (iv). The second equality is a braid relation for the
\mathbf{b}\tau_{i}
. Setting
f=\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{1}\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{2}{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{1}e
, by (I) we have that
\bar{\tau}_{1}\bar{\tau}_{2}\tau_{1}b\xi\in\Xi_{f}
and so the ordering on the
\alpha_{i}
guarantees that
f_{2}\not>f_{3}
. Therefore
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{2}\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{2}
acts as the identity on
f
by (iv), and so
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{2}\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{1}\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{2}e=\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{1}\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{2}{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{1}e
and hence
\mathbf{w}^{i-1}d=\mathbf{w}^{i}d
, as desired.

Case 2’:

\alpha_{1}<\alpha_{3}<\alpha_{2}
or
\alpha_{1}>\alpha_{3}>\alpha_{2}
.   Again, we only deal with the first option. In this case,
\mathbf{w}^{i-1}=\mathbf{a}{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{1}\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{2}\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{1}\mathbf{b}
and
\mathbf{w}^{i}=\mathbf{a}\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{2}\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{1}{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{2}\mathbf{b}
, and the same proof as in Case 2 will work with the roles of
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{1}
,
\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{1}
and
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{2}
,
\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{2}
reversed. This completes the proof of (II).

Part (III).
The fact that the map
w\mapsto\mathbf{w}
is a monoid homomorphism follows from (II). To see that it is an isomorphism when there exists
d\in D^{n}
be such that
d_{1}<d_{2}<\dots<d_{n}
, consider two distinct elements
w,w^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{D}_{n}
and a permutation
\chi
with
w\chi\neq w^{\prime}\chi
. Let
w^{\prime\prime}\in\mathfrak{D}_{n}
be an element with
w^{\prime\prime}\text{id}=\chi
, and let
d\in D^{n}
be such that
d_{1}<d_{2}<\dots<d_{n}
. Then
\Xi_{\mathbf{w}^{\prime}\mathbf{w}^{\prime\prime}d}=w^{\prime}w^{\prime\prime}\Xi_{d}=w^{\prime}\chi
, and
\Xi_{\mathbf{w}\mathbf{w}^{\prime\prime}d}=ww^{\prime\prime}\Xi_{d}=w\chi
. Since
w\chi\neq w^{\prime}\chi
this implies that
\mathbf{w}\neq\mathbf{w}^{\prime}
.
∎

Example 4.2
.

The following is essentially the simplest example of an application of Proposition
4.1
. Consider
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and for
i=1,\dots,k-1
define

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{i}(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\mathbf{x},\qquad&x_{i}\geq x_{i+1},\\
(x_{1},\dots,x_{i-1},x_{i+1},x_{i},x_{i+2},\dots,x_{n}),\qquad&x_{i}<x_{i+1},\end{cases}

\displaystyle\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{i}(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\mathbf{x},\qquad&x_{i}\leq x_{i+1},\\
(x_{1},\dots,x_{i-1},x_{i+1},x_{i},x_{i+2},\dots,x_{n}),\qquad&x_{i}>x_{i+1},\end{cases}

It is straightforward to check that these maps satisfy the conditions of Proposition
4.1
, and hence extend to an action of
\mathfrak{D}_{n}
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. We call this action the
basic action
of
\mathfrak{D}_{n}
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Example
4.2
shows that the biHecke monoid can be used to sorts lists with possibly equal elements.

4.2
\mathfrak{D}_{n}
-actions and Pitman operators

We next define a
\mathfrak{D}_{n}
