-action on the space
\mathcal{C}^{n}(\mathbb{R})
of continuous functions
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
with an asymptotic slope. First, define the
Pitman operator

\mathcal{P}:\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R})\to\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R})
by following rules. If
\lambda_{1}(f)\geq\lambda_{2}(f)
, then set
\mathcal{P}f=f
. If
\lambda_{1}(f)<\lambda_{2}(f)
, then define

\begin{split}\mathcal{P}f_{1}(x)&=f[(-\infty,2)\to(x,1)]=f_{1}(x)+\sup_{z\leq x}[f_{2}(z)-f_{1}(z)],\\
\qquad\mathcal{P}f_{2}(x)&=f_{1}(x)+f_{2}(x)-\mathcal{P}f_{1}(x)=f_{2}(x)-\sup_{z\leq x}[f_{2}(z)-f_{1}(z)].\end{split}

(27)

In the above expression and in the remainder of the paper, we write
\mathcal{P}f_{i}:=(\mathcal{P}f)_{i}
to streamline notation.
Next, define the
co-Pitman operator

\bar{\mathcal{P}}:\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R})\to\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R})
by letting
\mathcal{P}f=f
if
\lambda_{1}(f)\leq\lambda_{2}(f)
, and setting

\begin{split}\bar{\mathcal{P}}f_{1}(x)&=f_{1}(x)+\sup_{z\geq x}[f_{2}(z)-f_{1}(z)],\\
\qquad\bar{\mathcal{P}}f_{2}(x)&=f_{2}(x)-\sup_{z\geq x}[f_{2}(z)-f_{1}(z)]\end{split}

(28)

if
\lambda_{1}(f)>\lambda_{2}(f)
. The co-Pitman operator can be given by conjugating the Pitman operator. Indeed, if we let
Rf(x)=f(-x)
denote the reflection map, then

\bar{\mathcal{P}}=R\mathcal{P}R.

Similarly, if we let
\hat{R}f_{1}(x)=-f_{2}(-x)
and
\hat{R}f_{2}(x)=-f_{1}(-x)
denote the 180-degree rotation of the environment, then
\bar{\mathcal{P}}=\hat{R}\mathcal{P}\hat{R}.

Next, for
f\in\mathcal{C}^{n}(\mathbb{R})
and
i=1,\dots,n-2
define

\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{i}}f=(f_{1},\dots,f_{i-1},\mathcal{P}(f_{i},f_{i+1}),f_{i+2},\dots,f_{n}),

and similarly set
\mathcal{P}_{\bar{\tau}_{i}}=R\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{i}}R
. Our goal in the remainder of this section is to extend the Pitman operators
\mathcal{P},\bar{\mathcal{P}}
to actions of the biHecke monoid on
\mathcal{C}^{n}(\mathbb{R})
. To do so, we check the conditions of Proposition
4.1
. We first record an
isometric
property for Pitman operators. This isometric property is one of the key reasons that Pitman operators are so useful in the study of last passage percolation.

Following
[
DNV22
]
, we say that two environments
f,g\in\mathcal{C}^{n}(\mathbb{R})
are
boundary isometric
and write
f\sim g
if

f[(\mathbf{x},n)\to(\mathbf{y},1)]=g[(\mathbf{x},n)\to(\mathbf{y},1)]

(29)

for any vectors
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq},k\in\mathbb{N}
. We say that a map
F:\mathcal{C}^{n}(\mathbb{R})\to\mathcal{C}^{n}(\mathbb{R})
is a (boundary) isometry if
Ff\sim f
for all
f\in\mathcal{C}^{n}(\mathbb{R})
.

Lemma 4.3
.

For all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
i\in\{1,\dots,n-1\}
, the operators
\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{i}},\mathcal{P}_{\bar{\tau}_{i}}
are boundary isometries.

Proof.

We will just check that
\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{i}}
is an isometry. The claim for
\mathcal{P}_{\bar{\tau}_{i}}
follows from the fact that
X
is an isometry if and only if
\hat{R}X\hat{R}
is an isometry for any map
X
. If
\lambda_{i}(f)\geq\lambda_{i+1}(f)
, then
\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{i}}f=f
and the claim is immediate. Now suppose
\lambda_{i}(f)<\lambda_{i+1}(f)
. We check that (
29
) holds for a fixed
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}
. Let
T=\argmax_{z\leq x_{1}}f_{i+1}(z)-f_{i}(z)
, and let
f^{\prime}=f-f(T)
. Observing that
f\sim f^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{i}}f\sim\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{i}}f-f(T)=\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{i}}f^{\prime}
, it suffices to show that (
29
) holds for
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}
with
f,g
replaced by
f^{\prime},\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{i}}f^{\prime}
. In this case,
f^{\prime}(T)=0
and by the definition of
T
, for
x_{1}\leq z
we have that

\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{i}}f_{i}^{\prime}(z)=f^{\prime}[(T,2)\to(z,1)],\qquad\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{i}}f_{i+1}^{\prime}+\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{i}}f_{i}^{\prime}=f_{i+1}+f_{i}.

For an operator defined using the left-hand sides above on continuous functions
f^{\prime}:[T,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
f^{\prime}(T)=0
, boundary isometry is proven as Lemma 4.3 in
[
DOV22
]
.
∎

We are now ready to prove the basic algebraic relations for Pitman operators.

Lemma 4.4
.

The Pitman operators
\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{i}},\mathcal{P}_{\bar{\tau}_{i}}
have the following properties.

i.

Recall that
\lambda:\mathcal{C}^{n}(\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
denotes the slope map, and let
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{i},\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{i}
denote the generators for the basic
\mathfrak{D}_{n}
-action on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
introduced in Example
4.2
.
Then for
f\in\mathcal{C}^{n}(\mathbb{R})
and
i=1,\dots,n-1
we have
\lambda\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{i}}f={\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{i}\lambda f
and
\lambda\mathcal{P}_{\bar{\tau}_{i}}f=\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{i}\lambda f
.

ii.

The maps
\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{i}}
satisfy the braid, commutation, and idempotent relations (
22
), (
23
), (
25
) as do the maps
\mathcal{P}_{\bar{\tau}_{i}}
. Moreover, we have the mixed commutation relation
\mathcal{P}_{\bar{\tau}_{i}}\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{j}}=\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{j}}\mathcal{P}_{\bar{\tau}_{i}}
when
|i-j|\geq 2
.

iii.

\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{i}}\mathcal{P}_{\bar{\tau}_{i}}f=f
if
\lambda_{i}(f)\leq\lambda_{i+1}(f)
and
\mathcal{P}_{\bar{\tau}_{i}}\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{i}}f=f
if
\lambda_{i}(f)\geq\lambda_{i+1}(f)
.

Proof.

Part i.
Here we just need to observe that if
\lambda_{1}(f)<\lambda_{2}(f)
then
\lambda(\mathcal{P}f)=(\lambda_{2}(f),\lambda_{1}(f))
.

Part ii.
The commutation relation (
23
) and the mixed commutation relation are immediate. The idempotent relation (
25
) follows from part i, which implies that
\lambda_{1}(\mathcal{P}f)\geq\lambda_{2}(\mathcal{P}f)
for any
f\in\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R})
, and hence
\mathcal{P}
acts as the identity on its image. It suffices to check braid relation when
n=3
, and by symmetry, only the unbarred identity

\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{1}}\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{2}}\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{1}}f=\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{2}}\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{1}}\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{2}}f.

(30)

We divide into cases, depending on the order of the slope
\lambda(f)
.

Case 1:
\lambda_{1}(f)<\lambda_{2}(f)<\lambda_{3}(f)
.
Letting
g,h
denote the left- and right-hand sides of (
30
). Observing that Pitman operators
\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{i}}
preserve the sum
f_{1}+f_{2}+f_{3}
, it suffices to show that
g_{1}=h_{1}
and
g_{3}=h_{3}
. We will only prove the first of these equalities as the second follows from a symmetric argument. We claim that

h_{1}=(\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{1}}\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{2}}f)_{1}=g_{1}.

(31)

The first equality in (
31
) is immediate since
\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{2}}
does not affect line
1
. For the second equality, using that
\lambda_{1}(f)\vee\lambda_{2}(f)<\lambda_{3}(f)
we have that

(\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{1}}\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{2}}f)_{1}(x)=f_{1}(x)+\sup_{z_{2}\leq z_{1}\leq x}[f_{2}(z_{1})-f_{1}(z_{1})]+[f_{3}(z_{2})-f_{2}(z_{2})].

(32)

In other words, we get the top line
(\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{1}}\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{2}}f)_{1}
by reflecting
f_{2}
off of
f_{3}
, and then reflecting
f_{1}
off of the result. Next, we can rewrite the right-hand side of (
32
) as

\sup_{z_{2}\leq x}f_{3}(z_{2})+f[(z_{2},2)\to(x,1)].

By Lemma
4.3
, this equals

\sup_{z_{2}\leq x}f_{3}(z_{2})+\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{1}}f[(z_{2},2)\to(x,1)]=\sup_{z_{2}\leq x}\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{1}}f_{3}(z_{2})+\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{1}}f[(z_{2},2)\to(x,1)].

Now, by part i, the inequality
\lambda_{1}(f)\vee\lambda_{2}(f)<\lambda_{3}(f)
is preserved by the map
\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{1}}
. Hence the equality (
32
) also holds with
\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{1}}f
in place of
f
. Putting this together with the previous three displays we get that
(\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{1}}\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{2}}f)_{1}=(\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{1}}\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{2}}\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{1}}f)_{1}
, giving the second equality in (
31
).

Case 2:
\lambda_{i}(f)\geq\lambda_{i+1}(f)
for some
i\in\{1,2\}
.
In this case, using the slope-interchange property in part i and working through different cases, in the composition
\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{1}}\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{2}}\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{1}}f
we have that

•

The rightmost
\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{1}}
-operator acts as the identity if
i=1
;

•

The leftmost
\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{1}}
-operator acts as the identity if
i=2
.

Similarly, in the composition
\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{2}}\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{1}}\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{2}}f
we have that

•

The rightmost
\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{2}}
-operator acts as the identity if
i=2
;

•

The leftmost
\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{2}}
-operator acts as the identity if
i=1
.

Therefore if
i=1
then both sides of (
30
) are equal to
\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{1}}\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{2}}f
, and if
i=2
then both sides of (
30
) are equal to
\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{2}}\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{1}}f
.

Part iii.
This is also shown in the appendix of
[
SS23a
]
, though the language used there is different. We only check the first identity as the second follows by symmetry. If
\lambda_{i}(f)=\lambda_{i+1}(f)
, then the identity holds since both
\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{i}}
and
\mathcal{P}_{\bar{\tau}_{i}}
act as the identity. Now assume
\lambda_{i}(f)<\lambda_{i+1}(f)
. Since the sum
f_{i}+f_{i+1}
is preserved by both
\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{i}}
and
\mathcal{P}_{\bar{\tau}_{i}}
it suffices to check that
\mathcal{P}_{\bar{\tau}_{i}}\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{i}}f_{i}=f_{i}
. Define

Sf(x)=\sup_{z\leq x}f_{i+1}(x)-f_{i}(x)

so that

\mathcal{P}_{\bar{\tau}_{i}}\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{i}}f_{i}(x)=f_{i}(x)+Sf(x)+\sup_{z\geq x}[f_{i+1}(z)-f_{i}(z)-2Sf(z)].

Noting that
Sf\geq f_{i+1}-f_{i}
and that
Sf
is non-decreasing we can see that

\mathcal{P}_{\bar{\tau}_{i}}\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{i}}f_{1}(x)\leq f_{1}(x)+Sf(x)+\sup_{z\geq x}[-Sf(z)]=f_{1}(x).

On the other hand, since
f_{1},f_{2}
are continuous and the difference
f_{2}(z)-f_{1}(z)\to\infty
with
z
, there must exist
z_{0}\geq x
where
f_{2}(z_{0})-f_{1}(z_{0})=Sf(z_{0})=Sf(x).
Therefore
\mathcal{P}_{\bar{\tau}_{i}}\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{i}}f_{1}(x)\geq f_{1}(x)
, yielding the result.
∎

Given Lemma
4.4
, we can use Proposition
4.1
to extend the definition of Pitman operators to the whole monoid
\mathfrak{D}_{n}
. Indeed, for
\sigma\in\mathfrak{D}_{n}
, let
\xi_{k}\cdots\xi_{1}=\sigma
where each
\xi_{i}\in\{\tau_{i},\bar{\tau}_{i}:i=1,\dots,n-1\}
and define

\mathcal{P}_{\sigma}=\mathcal{P}_{\xi_{k}}\cdots\mathcal{P}_{\xi_{1}}.

Corollary 4.5
.

The map
\sigma\mapsto\mathcal{P}_{\sigma}:\mathcal{C}^{n}(\mathbb{R})\to\mathcal{C}^{n}(\mathbb{R})
is a monoid isomorphism of
\mathfrak{D}_{n}
. Moreover, letting
\lambda:\mathcal{C}^{n}(\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
denote the slope map, for any
\sigma\in\mathfrak{D}_{n}
we have

\lambda\circ\mathcal{P}_{\sigma}=\sigma\circ\lambda

where on the right-hand side of this equation
\sigma
acts on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
through the basic action.

Proof.

To prove that the map
\sigma\mapsto\mathcal{P}_{\sigma}
is unambiguously defined and yields a monoid isomorphism we check the conditions of Proposition
4.1
where
D=\mathcal{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R})
is given the partial order induced by the slope map
\lambda
. Property (i) in Proposition
4.1
is guaranteed by Lemma
4.4
.ii, property (ii) is guaranteed by the definition, property (iii) is guaranteed by Lemma
4.4
.i, and property (iv) follows from Lemma
4.4
.iii. The ‘Moreover’ claim then follows by Lemma
4.4
.i again.
∎

The Pitman transforms described in this section have more structure than the abstract monoid actions in Proposition
4.1
. In particular, we can describe orbits of elements in
\mathcal{C}^{n}(\mathbb{R})
using this structure.

Lemma 4.6
.

Consider the action
\sigma\mapsto\mathcal{P}_{\sigma}:\mathcal{C}^{n}(\mathbb{R})\to\mathcal{C}^{n}(\mathbb{R})
, and for
f\in\mathcal{C}^{n}(\mathbb{R})
let
O(f)=\{\mathcal{P}_{\sigma}f:\sigma\in\mathfrak{D}_{n}\}
denote its orbit. Let
O(\lambda(f))
be the orbit of
\lambda(f)
under the basic action of
\mathfrak{D}_{n}
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Then:

i.

For any
g\in O(f)
there exists
\sigma\in\mathfrak{D}_{n}
such that
\mathcal{P}_{\sigma}g=f
and so
O(f)=O(g)
.

ii.

The slope map
g\mapsto\lambda(g)
is a bijection from
O(f)
to
O(\lambda(f))
.

Proof.

For part i, if
g\in O(f)
then
g=\mathcal{P}_{\sigma}f
for some word
\sigma=\pi_{k}\cdots\pi_{1}
where
\pi_{j}\in\{\tau_{i},\bar{\tau}_{i}:i=1,\dots,n-1\}
. Then from the definition of
\mathcal{P}
and Lemma
4.4
.iii there exists a word
\sigma^{\prime}=\pi_{1}^{\prime}\cdots\pi_{k}^{\prime}
where each
\pi_{j}^{\prime}
equals either
\pi_{j}
or its barred/unbarred version, such that
\mathcal{P}_{\sigma^{\prime}\sigma}f=\mathcal{P}_{\sigma^{\prime}}g=f
.

For part ii, by Corollary
4.5
the map
\lambda:O(f)\to O(\lambda(f))
is onto. Now suppose that
g_{1},g_{2}\in O(f)
are such that
\lambda(g_{1})=\lambda(g_{2})
. Using the notation
\Xi_{g_{1}}
from Proposition
4.1
, let
\xi\in\Xi_{g_{1}}
. We will aim to find
\sigma\in\mathfrak{D}_{n}
with
\sigma\xi=\xi
and
\mathcal{P}_{\sigma}g_{1}=g_{2}
. If we can find such a
\sigma
, then by Proposition
4.1
(II), we have that
g_{2}=\mathcal{P}_{\sigma}g_{1}=\mathcal{P}_{\text{id}}g_{1}=g_{1}
, as desired. First, since
\lambda:O(f)\to O(\lambda(f))
is onto we can find
h\in O(f)=O(g_{1})
with
\lambda(h)_{1}\geq\dots\geq\lambda(h)_{k}
. Let
\sigma_{1}\in\mathfrak{D}_{n}
be such that
\mathcal{P}_{\sigma_{1}}g_{1}=h
. Since
O(h)=O(f)
by part i, we can then find
\sigma_{2}
such that
\mathcal{P}_{\sigma_{2}\sigma_{1}}g_{1}=\mathcal{P}_{\sigma_{2}}h=g_{2}
. We can write
\sigma_{1}=\hat{\tau}_{i_{k}}\cdots\hat{\tau}_{i_{1}}
and
\sigma_{2}=\hat{\tau}_{j_{\ell}}\cdots\hat{\tau}_{j_{1}}
where
\hat{\tau}_{i}\in\{\tau_{i},\bar{\tau}_{i}\}
for all
i
.
We may also assume that
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}
are effective, in the sense that for any
a\leq k
or
b\leq\ell
we have

\mathcal{P}_{\hat{\tau}_{i_{a}}\cdots\hat{\tau}_{i_{1}}}g_{1}\neq\mathcal{P}_{\hat{\tau}_{i_{a-1}}\cdots\hat{\tau}_{i_{1}}}g_{1},\qquad\mathcal{P}_{\hat{\tau}_{j_{b}}\cdots\hat{\tau}_{j_{1}}}h\neq\mathcal{P}_{\hat{\tau}_{j_{b-1}}\cdots\hat{\tau}_{j_{1}}}h.

Now, let
\tilde{\sigma}_{1}=\pi_{i_{k}}\cdots\pi_{i_{1}}
and
\tilde{\sigma}_{2}=\pi_{j_{\ell}}\cdots\pi_{j_{1}}
be the corresponding products of adjacent transpositions in
S_{n}
. Effectiveness of
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}
implies that
\tilde{\sigma}_{2}\tilde{\sigma}_{1}\xi=\sigma_{2}\sigma_{1}\xi
. Next, let
\Pi(h)
be the set of adjacent transpositions
\pi_{i}
such that
\lambda(h)_{i}=\lambda(h)_{i+1}
, and let
H(h)\subset S_{n}
be the subgroup generated by
\Pi(h)
. Consider
\tilde{\kappa}\in H(h)
, and let
\pi_{m_{k}}\cdots\pi_{m_{1}}
be a reduced word for
\tilde{\kappa}
, written in the alphabet
\Pi(h)
. There is a unique way to map the
\pi_{m_{i}}
to
\{\tau_{m_{i}},\bar{\tau}_{m_{i}}\}
such that the resulting element
\kappa\in\mathfrak{D}_{n}
is effective on
\sigma_{1}\xi
. Hence
\tilde{\kappa}\tilde{\sigma}_{1}\xi=\kappa\sigma_{1}\xi
. On the other hand, from the definition of the Pitman transform applied to lines of equal slope,
\mathcal{P}_{\kappa\sigma_{1}}g_{1}=\mathcal{P}_{\kappa}h=h
for all
\kappa\in H(h)
, so by the effectiveness of
\sigma_{2}
on
h
we have that
\tilde{\sigma}_{2}\kappa\sigma_{1}\xi=\sigma_{2}\kappa\sigma_{1}\xi
. We also have that
\mathcal{P}_{\sigma_{2}\kappa\sigma_{1}}g_{1}=g_{2}
and hence
\sigma_{2}\kappa\sigma_{1}\xi=\tilde{\sigma}_{2}\tilde{\kappa}\tilde{\sigma}_{1}\xi\in\Xi_{g_{2}}=\Xi_{g_{1}}
. Finally, the map

\tilde{\kappa}\mapsto\sigma_{2}\kappa\sigma_{1}\xi=\tilde{\sigma}_{2}\tilde{\kappa}\tilde{\sigma}_{1}\xi

from
H(h)\to\Xi_{g_{1}}
is a bijection. Indeed, this map is one-to-one by the invertibility of
\tilde{\sigma}_{2},\tilde{\sigma}_{2}
and it is easy to see that
|H(h)|=|\Xi_{g_{1}}|
(these are conjugate subgroups). Hence there must be some
\kappa
with
\sigma_{2}\kappa\sigma_{1}\xi=\xi
. Setting
\sigma=\sigma_{2}\kappa\sigma_{1}
then gives the desired sorting element.
∎

Corollary 4.7
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{C}^{n}(\mathbb{R})
, and let
g\in O(f)
, where
O(f)
is the
\mathcal{P}
-orbit of
f
. Let
I
be a set of the form
\{1,\ldots,k\}
or
\{k,\ldots,n\}
, and suppose that

\lambda(f)_{j}=\lambda(g)_{j}
for all
j\in I
. Then
f_{j}=g_{j}
for all
j\in I
.

Proof.

We can find
\sigma\in\mathfrak{D}^{n}
with
\sigma\lambda(f)=\lambda(g)
, where
\sigma
acts through the basic action and can be written as a product of adjacent transpositions that do not use coordinates of
I
. By Lemma
4.6
,
\mathcal{P}_{\sigma}f=g
, and since
\mathcal{P}_{\sigma}
only affects coordinates in
I^{c}
,
f_{j}=g_{j}
for all
j\in I
.
∎

4.3
Sorting and last passage percolation

The goal of this section is to represent the
\mathfrak{D}_{n}
-action from Corollary
4.5
in terms of last passage values. First consider
f\in\mathcal{C}^{n}(\mathbb{R})
with
\lambda(f)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}_{\leq}
, and recall the definition of last passage from
-\infty
in (
8
). The limit in that definition exists when
\mathbf{m}\in\{1,\dots,n\}^{k}_{<}
satisfies the following property:

\text{If $m_{i}$ is a coordinate of $\mathbf{m}$ but $m_{i}-1$ is not, then $\lambda_{m_{i-1}}<\lambda_{m_{i}}$}.

(33)

Moving forward, we will say
\mathbf{m}
satisfies (
33
) with respect to
\lambda
when the slope vector is not clear context. We call a
k
-tuple of paths
\pi=(\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{k})
, where each
\pi_{j}:(-\infty,x_{j}]\to\{1,\dots,n\}
is a nonincreasing cadlag path satisfying
\pi_{j}(x_{j})=1
and
\lim_{t\to-\infty}\pi_{j}(t)=m_{j}
a
disjoint optimizer
from
(-\infty,\mathbf{m})
to
(\mathbf{x},1)
if
\pi|_{[t,x_{k}]}
is a disjoint optimizer for all
t<x_{1}
. The condition (
33
) guarantees that disjoint optimizers from
(-\infty,\mathbf{m})
to
(\mathbf{x},1)
exist and that they are pointwise limits of disjoint optimizers from
(t,\mathbf{m})
to
(\mathbf{x},1)
as
t\to-\infty
.

For
f\in\mathcal{C}^{n}(\mathbb{R})
, we can describe elements of its
\mathcal{P}
-orbit
O(f)
using last passage percolation. We start with a few examples before moving towards a general theory.

Example 4.8

(LPP to the top)
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{C}^{n}(\mathbb{R})
and suppose that
\lambda(f)_{k}<\lambda(f)_{j}
for all
j<k
. Then

f[(-\infty,k)\to(x,1)]=\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{1}\cdots\tau_{k-1}}f_{1}(x).

(34)

This is almost immediate from the definition and the fact that the Pitman transform
\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{i}}
switches the slopes of
f_{i},f_{i+1}
when
\lambda(f)_{i}<\lambda(f)_{i+1}
.

Example 4.9

(Zigzag last passage percolation)
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{C}^{n}(\mathbb{R})
, suppose that
\lambda(f)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}_{<}
. Let
g\in O(f)
, and let
\xi\in S_{n}
be such that

\lambda(g)_{\xi^{-1}(1)}<\cdots<\lambda(g)_{\xi^{-1}(n)}.

Then for every
j\in\{1,\dots,n\}
,

f[(-\infty,j)\to(x,1)]=\mathcal{P}_{\sigma_{j}}g_{1}(x),\qquad\sigma_{j}=\sigma_{j,1}\cdots\sigma_{j,\xi^{-1}(j)-1},\!\!\qquad\sigma_{j,i}=\begin{cases}\tau_{i},\quad&j<\xi_{g}(i)\\
\bar{\tau}_{i},&j>\xi_{g}(i)\end{cases}

Indeed, by the previous example we have that
f[(-\infty,j)\to(x,1)]=\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{1}\cdots\tau_{j-1}}f_{1}(x)
, and moreover
\lambda(\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{1}\cdots\tau_{j-1}}f)_{1}=\lambda(f)_{j}
. Similarly, by construction
\lambda(\mathcal{P}_{\sigma_{j}}g)_{1}=\lambda(f)_{j}
. Therefore by Corollary
4.7
we have that
\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{1}\cdots\tau_{j-1}}f_{1}=\mathcal{P}_{\sigma_{j}}g_{1}
. We can rewrite
\mathcal{P}_{\sigma_{j}}g_{1}
more explicitly as a kind of
zig-zag last passage percolation
. Let
k=\xi^{-1}(j)
. Then

\mathcal{P}_{\sigma_{j}}g_{1}(x)=g_{1}(x)+\sup_{\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}}\left(\sum_{i=2}^{k}g_{i}(y_{i})-g_{i-1}(y_{i})\right),

where the supremum is over all vectors
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
with
y_{1}=x
and satisfying the inequalities
y_{i}\leq y_{i-1}
or
y_{i}\leq y_{i-1}
for all
2\leq i\leq k
depending on whether
\lambda(g)_{k}>\lambda(g)_{i-1}
or
\lambda(g)_{k}<\lambda(g)_{i-1}
. Remarkably, the Brownian Burke theorem (see Section
5
) implies that we can construct models of zig-zag last passage percolation across independent Brownian motions whose stationary measures are themselves Brownian motions!

Both of the previous examples are special cases of the following general proposition.

Proposition 4.10

(Orbit elements as last passage values)
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{C}^{n}(\mathbb{R})
with
\lambda(f)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}_{\leq}
, let
g\in O(f)
, let
k\in\{1,\dots,n\}
, and let
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
. Recall that
\Xi_{g}\subset S_{n}
is the set of permutations satisfying

\lambda(g)_{\xi_{g}^{-1}(1)}\leq\cdots\leq\lambda(g)_{\xi_{g}^{-1}(n)}.

(35)

There exists a unique vector
\mathbf{m}\in\{1,\dots,n\}^{k}_{<}
satisfying (
33
) for
\lambda(f)
such that
\mathbf{m}=\xi\{1,\dots,k\}
for some
\xi\in\Xi_{g}
(here thinking of
\mathbf{m}
as a subset). We have

g[(-\infty,(1,\dots,k))\to(\mathbf{x},1)]=f[(-\infty,\mathbf{m})\to(\mathbf{x},1)].\qquad

(36)

In particular, if
x=x_{1}=\dots=x_{n}
then

g_{1}(x)+\dots+g_{k}(x)=f[(-\infty,\mathbf{m})\to(x^{k},1)].

The proof of Proposition
4.10
is an induction based on the following lemma.

Lemma 4.11
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{C}^{n}(\mathbb{R})
. Define
\sigma=\tau_{n-1}\cdots\tau_{1}
. If
\lambda(f)_{1}<\lambda(f)_{i}
for all
i>1
, then for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}_{\leq}
:

f[(-\infty,(2,\ldots,n))\to(\mathbf{x},1)]=\mathcal{P}_{\sigma}f[(-\infty,(1,\ldots,n-1))\to(\mathbf{x},1)].

(37)

In particular, if
x_{1}=\dots=x_{n-1}=x
then:

f[(-\infty,(2,\ldots,n))\to(x^{n-1},1)]=\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\mathcal{P}_{\sigma}f_{i}(x).

(38)

Proof.

We actually prove the restricted case (
38
) first and then use this to prove the general version (
37
).
To shorten notation, we write
[i,j]:=(i,i+1,\dots,j)\in\mathbb{R}^{j-i+1}
through the proof. First, for
t\leq x
we have

f[(t,[1,n])\to(x^{n},1)]=\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{i}(x)-f_{i}(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{P}_{\sigma}f_{i}(x)-f_{i}(t).

(39)

The first equality is by definition, and the second equality uses that Pitman transforms preserve the sum of all lines. Moreover, it is easy to check from the definition that

\displaystyle f[(t,

\displaystyle[2,n])\to(x^{n},1)]

\displaystyle=f[(t,[1,n])\to(x^{n},1)]+f_{1}(t)-f_{n}(x)+\max_{t\leq y_{1}\leq\dots\leq y_{n-1}\leq x}\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}f_{i+1}(y_{i})-f_{i}(y_{i}).

Now, since
\lambda(f)_{1}<\lambda(f)_{i}
for all
i>1
, the final term above equals

\max_{y_{1}\leq\dots\leq y_{n-1}\leq x}\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}f_{i+1}(y_{i})-f_{i}(y_{i}).

for all small enough
t
, and this minimum is attained. Therefore by (
39
), to verify (
38
) we just need to show that

\mathcal{P}_{\sigma}f_{n}(x)=f_{n}(x)-\max_{y_{1}\leq\dots\leq y_{n-1}\leq x}\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}f_{i+1}(y_{i})-f_{i}(y_{i}).

(40)

As in Example
4.8
, this is essentially immediate from the definition (
27
) and an induction since
\lambda(f)_{1}<\lambda(f)_{i}
for all
i>1
.

We move to the general case. Fix
t\leq x_{1}
. By Lemma
4.3
we have that

f[(t,[2,n])\to(\mathbf{x},1)]=\mathcal{P}_{\sigma}f[(t,[2,n])\to(\mathbf{x},1)].

Now, since
\lambda(f)_{1}<\lambda(f)_{i}
for all
i\geq 1
, then there exists
t_{0}
such that the function

t\mapsto f[(t,[2,n])\to(\mathbf{x},1)]+\sum_{i=2}^{n}f_{i}(t)

is constant for
t\leq t_{0}
, and hence equals the left-hand side of (
37
).
On the other hand, since
\lambda(f)_{1}<\lambda(f)_{i}
for
i>1
we have
\lambda(\mathcal{P}_{\sigma}f)_{n}=\lambda(f)_{1}<\lambda(\mathcal{P}_{\sigma}f)_{i}=\lambda(f)_{i+1}
for
i<n
. Therefore for all
t
small enough we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\sigma}

\displaystyle f[(t,[2,n])\to(x,1)]+\sum_{i=2}^{n}\mathcal{P}_{\sigma}f_{i}(t)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}_{\sigma}f[(x_{1},[1,n-1])\to(\mathbf{x},1)]+\sup_{y_{1}\leq\dots\leq y_{n-1}\leq x_{1}}\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\mathcal{P}_{\sigma}f_{i+1}(y_{i})-\mathcal{P}_{\sigma}f_{i}(y_{i})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}_{\sigma}f[(-\infty,[1,n-1])\to(\mathbf{x},1)]+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\mathcal{P}_{\sigma}f_{i}(x_{1})

\displaystyle\;\;\;+\sup_{y_{1}\leq\dots\leq y_{n-1}\leq x_{1}}\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\mathcal{P}_{\sigma}f_{i+1}(y_{i})-\mathcal{P}_{\sigma}f_{i}(y_{i})

Call the latter two terms on the right-hand side above
G(x_{1})
.
Combining all of the above displays gives that for all small enough
t
we have the equality

f[(-\infty,[2,n])\to(\mathbf{x},1)]=\mathcal{P}_{\sigma}f[(-\infty,[1,n-1])\to(\mathbf{x},1)]+G(x_{1})+\sum_{i=2}^{n}f_{i}(t)-\mathcal{P}_{\sigma}f_{i}(t).

Therefore

\displaystyle f[(-\infty,[2,n])\to(\mathbf{x},1)]-f[(-\infty,[2,n])\to(x^{n-1},1)]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\sigma}f[(-\infty,[1,n-1])\to(\mathbf{x},1)]-\mathcal{P}_{\sigma}f[(-\infty,[1,n-1])\to(x^{n-1},1)].

On the other hand, (
38
) ensures that the second terms on both the right- and left-hand sides above are equal, and hence so are the first terms, yielding (
37
).
∎

Proof of Proposition
4.10
.

First, we can construct
\mathbf{m}
by recursively constructing
\xi\in\Xi_{g}
. Indeed, for every
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
, we always let
\xi(i)
be the minimal index
j\in\{1,\dots,n\}\setminus\{\xi(1),\dots,\xi(i-1)\}
with
\lambda(f)_{j}=\lambda(g)_{i}
. Since
\lambda(g)
is a permutation of
\lambda(f)
, this process results in a permutation
\xi
. Moreover, the use of minimal indices in the construction implies that resulting vector
\mathbf{m}
satisfies (
33
) for
\lambda(f)
. Uniqueness of
\mathbf{m}
follows since
\lambda(f)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}_{\leq}
.

To prove (
36
), first assume that
m_{k}>k
. Let
\ell\in\{1,\dots,m_{k}-1\}
be the maximal index with
\ell\notin\{m_{1},\dots,m_{k-1}\}
. Let
\mathbf{m}_{2}=(\ell+1,\ell+2,\ldots,m_{k})
so we can write
\mathbf{m}=(\mathbf{m}_{1},\mathbf{m}_{2})
. Since
\mathbf{m}
satisfies (
33
), both
\mathbf{m}_{1},\mathbf{m}_{2}
also satisfy (
33
) and so we may define last passage from
(-\infty,\mathbf{m}_{1}),(-\infty,\mathbf{m}_{2})
. Next, for
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{k}-\ell}_{\leq}
, define

\displaystyle G(f,\mathbf{y},\mathbf{m}_{1}):=\lim_{t\to-\infty}f[(t^{|\mathbf{m}_{1}|},\mathbf{y};\mathbf{m}_{1},(\ell-1)^{m_{k}-\ell})\to(\mathbf{x},1)]+\sum_{i\in\mathbf{m}_{1}}f_{i}(t).

Using this kind of hybrid last passage value, we can write down the following metric composition law:

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle[(-\infty,\mathbf{m})\to(\mathbf{x},1)]=\sup_{\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{m-\ell}_{\leq}}f[(-\infty,\mathbf{m}_{2})\to(\mathbf{y},\ell)]+G(f,\mathbf{y},\mathbf{m}_{1}).

Now, the condition (
33
) implies that
\lambda(f)_{\ell}<\lambda(f)_{j}
for all
j>\ell
. Therefore by Lemma
4.11
we may write the above as

\displaystyle\sup_{\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{m-\ell}_{\leq}}f^{\prime}

\displaystyle[(-\infty,\mathbf{m}_{2}^{\prime})\to(\mathbf{y},\ell)]+G(f^{\prime},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{m}_{1})=f^{\prime}[(-\infty,\mathbf{m}^{\prime})\to(\mathbf{x},1)],

where
f^{\prime}={\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{\ell}\cdots{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{m-1}f
,
\mathbf{m}_{2}^{\prime}=\{\ell,\ldots,m_{k}-1\}
and
\mathbf{m}^{\prime}=(\mathbf{m}_{1},\mathbf{m}_{2}^{\prime})
. Note that
G(f^{\prime},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{m}_{1})
involves lines
1,\ldots,\ell-1
only, which are equal in
f
and
f^{\prime}
. Now, observe that our construction gives that
\lambda(f^{\prime})_{m_{i}^{\prime}}=\lambda(f)_{m_{i}}
for all
i
, and that in the new environment
f^{\prime}
, the vector
\mathbf{m}^{\prime}
satisfies (
33
) for
\lambda(f^{\prime})
. Therefore if
m_{k}-1\neq k
we can repeat the above argument with
\mathbf{m}^{\prime},f^{\prime}
in place of
\mathbf{m},f
to get
\mathbf{m}^{\prime\prime},f^{\prime\prime}
with
m^{\prime\prime}_{k}=m_{k}-2
,
\lambda(f^{\prime\prime})_{m_{i}^{\prime\prime}}=\lambda(f)_{m_{i}}
for all
i
, and
\mathbf{m}^{\prime\prime}
satisfying (
33
) for
\lambda(f^{\prime\prime})
. Continuing in this way, we end at an environment
h
satisfying

f[(-\infty,\mathbf{m})\to(\mathbf{x},1)]=h[(-\infty,(1,\dots,k))\to(\mathbf{x},1)]=h[(x_{1}^{k},k)\to(\mathbf{x},1)]+\sum_{i=1}^{k}h_{i}(x_{1})

and satisfying
\lambda(h)_{i}=\lambda(f)_{m_{i}}
for all
i
. If
m_{k}=m
, we can avoid the above argument entirely and simply let
h=f
.

Now, there is a sorting operator
\sigma\in\mathfrak{D}_{n}
which can be written as a product of
\tau_{i},\bar{\tau}_{i},i\leq k-1
such that
\sigma\lambda(g)_{j}=\lambda(h)_{j}
for all
j\leq k
. Therefore by Corollary
4.7
we have that
\mathcal{P}_{\sigma}g_{j}=h_{j}
for all
j\leq k
, and so

\displaystyle h[(x_{1}^{k},k)\to(\mathbf{x},1)]+\sum_{i=1}^{k}h_{i}(x_{1})

\displaystyle=g[(x_{1}^{k},k)\to(\mathbf{x},1)]+\sum_{i=1}^{k}g_{i}(x_{1})

\displaystyle=g[(-\infty,(1,\dots,k))\to(\mathbf{x},1)].

Here the first equality uses that Pitman transforms preserve sums and are boundary isometries (Lemma
4.3
). Putting this together with the previous display yields the result.
∎

The following corollary of Proposition
4.10
gives a clean formula for inverting the full sort, where we completely reverse the order of the slopes. This corollary can be viewed as describing the RSK correspondence in this setting.

Let
\mathcal{C}^{n}_{\leq}(\mathbb{R}),\mathcal{C}^{n}_{\geq}(\mathbb{R})
be the subsets of
\mathcal{C}^{n}(\mathbb{R})
such that if
f\in\mathcal{C}^{n}_{\leq}(\mathbb{R})
then
\lambda(f)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}_{\leq}
and if
f\in\mathcal{C}^{n}_{\geq}(\mathbb{R})
then
\lambda(f)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}_{\geq}
. By Lemma
4.6
, for
f\in\mathcal{C}^{n}_{\leq}(\mathbb{R})
there is a unique element
\mathcal{P}_{\operatorname{RSK}}f
in the
\mathcal{P}
-orbit of
f
such that
\mathcal{P}_{\operatorname{RSK}}f\in\mathcal{C}^{n}_{\geq}(\mathbb{R})
and given
g\in\mathcal{C}^{n}_{\geq}(\mathbb{R})
, there is a unique element
\mathcal{Q}_{\operatorname{RSK}}g
in its
\mathcal{P}
-orbit contained in
\mathcal{C}^{n}_{\geq}(\mathbb{R})
. Lemma
4.6
.1 guarantees that these maps are inverses, so we have defined a bijection

\mathcal{P}_{\operatorname{RSK}}:\mathcal{C}^{n}_{\leq}(\mathbb{R})\to\mathcal{C}^{n}_{\geq}(\mathbb{R})

with inverse
\mathcal{Q}_{\operatorname{RSK}}
. The next corollary gives a simple global description of these maps without appealing to iterated Pitman transforms. This is the analogue of Greene’s theorem in the present setting.

Corollary 4.12
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{C}^{n}_{\leq}(\mathbb{R})
, let
g=\mathcal{P}_{\operatorname{RSK}}f
, and set
Rg(x)=g(-x)
. For every
k\in\{1,\dots,n\}
let
\mathbf{n}_{k}\in\{1,\dots,n\}^{k}_{<}
denote the unique maximal vector (in the coordinatewise order) satisfying (
33
) for
\lambda(f)
. Similarly, let
\mathbf{m}_{k}\in\{1,\dots,n\}^{k}_{<}
denote the unique maximal vector (in the coordinatewise order) satisfying (
33
) for
-\lambda(f)=(-\lambda(f)_{k},\dots,-\lambda(f)_{1})
. Then:

\displaystyle f[(-\infty,\mathbf{n}_{k})\to(x^{k},1)]

\displaystyle=g_{1}(x)+\dots+g_{k}(x),\qquad\text{ and }

\displaystyle Rg[(-\infty,\mathbf{m}_{k})\to(-x^{k},1)]

\displaystyle=f_{1}(x)+\dots+f_{k}(x).

Proof.

The recovery formulas for
g
from
f
are both special cases of Proposition
4.10
. To recover
f
from
g
, first let
\sigma
be such that
\mathcal{P}_{\sigma}g=f
. Now, from the formulas
\mathcal{P}_{\bar{\tau}_{i}}=R\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{i}}R
and the identity
R^{2}=\text{id}
, we have that
Rf=\mathcal{P}_{\bar{\sigma}}Rg
, where
\bar{\sigma}
is the given by taking the word
\sigma
and mapping
\tau_{i}\mapsto\bar{\tau}_{i}
and
\bar{\tau}_{i}\mapsto\tau_{i}
everywhere. Moreover,
Rg\in\mathcal{C}^{n}_{\leq}(\mathbb{R})
so we can apply Proposition
4.10
to recover
Rf
from
Rg
. Applying
R
to
Rf
then yields the formula above.
∎

A different approach to the bijection in Corollary
4.12
was developed in Sorensen’s Ph.D. thesis using queuing maps rather than multi-path last passage, e.g. see Section 2.3.3 and Lemma 2.3.18 in
[
Sor23
]
. Another perspective on Corollary
4.12
is in terms of the Schützenberger involution. Indeed, we can write
\mathcal{Q}_{\operatorname{RSK}}=R\mathcal{P}_{\operatorname{RSK}}R
, where
Rf(x)=f(-x)
. From this point of view, the fact that
\mathcal{P}_{\operatorname{RSK}}
inverts
\mathcal{Q}_{\operatorname{RSK}}
on
\mathcal{C}^{n}_{\leq}(\mathbb{R})
says that
\mathcal{P}_{\operatorname{RSK}}R
is an involution when restricted to
\mathcal{C}^{n}_{\geq}(\mathbb{R})
. If we consider
\mathcal{C}^{n}_{\geq}(\mathbb{R})\subset\mathcal{C}^{n}(\mathbb{R})
as the analogue of the set of Young tableaux, we can understand
\mathcal{P}_{\operatorname{RSK}}R
as the Schützenberger involution in the present setting. See
[
Ful97
, Appendix A]
for discussion of the classical Schützenberger involution and
[
BOZ21
]
for a comprehensive modern account of the connection between the Schützenberger involution, last passage percolation, and directed polymer partition functions.

4.4
Burke theorems

Pitman transforms behave well with Brownian inputs. At the level of the two-line Pitman transform, this is the well-known
Brownian Burke property
, which has been previously used in the study of LPP in
[
OY01
,
SS23a
,
SS23b
]
. For this theorem and throughout the remainder of the paper, for a vector
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
we let
\mu_{\lambda}
denote the law of
t\mapsto B(t)+\lambda t
, where
B\in\mathcal{C}^{n}_{0}(\mathbb{R})
is
k
-tuple of independent, two-sided Brownian motions of variance
2
.

Theorem 4.13

(Brownian Burke property)
.

Let
B=(B_{1},B_{2})\sim\mu_{(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})}
for some
\lambda_{2}>\lambda_{1}
. Then

\mathcal{P}B-\mathcal{P}B(0)\sim\mu_{(\lambda_{2},\lambda_{1})}
.

Proof.

This is part of
[
OY01
, Theorem 4]
. We translate the language in order to assist the reader navigating between that paper and ours. It suffices to prove the theorem when
\lambda_{1}=0
, since the Pitman transform
\mathcal{P}
commutes with a common linear shift of both functions (Lemma
3.5
).

In the language of that paper, O’Connell and Yor show the following. Let
B^{\prime},C:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be two-sided, independent standard Brownian motions, and let
m>0
. Define functions
q,e,d:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
as follows:

\displaystyle q(t)

\displaystyle=B_{t}^{\prime}+C_{t}-mt+\sup_{s\leq t}(ms-C_{s}-B_{s}),

\displaystyle d(t)

\displaystyle=B_{t}^{\prime}+q(0)-q(t),

\displaystyle e(t)

\displaystyle=C_{t}+q(0)-q(t).

Theorem 4 in
[
OY01
]
states
d,e
are independent standard Brownian motions and that
(d,e)|_{(-\infty,t]}
is independent of
q|_{[t,\infty)}
for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
. We can rewrite their result in terms of Pitman transforms. Define
B_{1}(t)=\sqrt{2}B_{t}^{\prime},B_{2}(t)=\sqrt{2}(mt-C_{t})
and observe that if
m=\lambda_{2}/\sqrt{2}
, then
B=(B_{1},B_{2})\sim\mu_{(0,\lambda_{2})}
. Then

\displaystyle\sqrt{2}q(t)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}B_{1}(t)-B_{2}(t),

\displaystyle\qquad\sqrt{2}d(t)

\displaystyle=B_{1}(t)+B_{2}(t)+\mathcal{P}B_{1}(0)-\mathcal{P}B_{1}(t)=\mathcal{P}B_{2}(t)-\mathcal{P}B_{2}(0).

\displaystyle\sqrt{2}(mt-e(t))

\displaystyle=B_{2}(t)+\mathcal{P}B_{1}(t)-B_{2}(t)-\mathcal{P}B_{1}(0)=\mathcal{P}B_{1}(t)-\mathcal{P}B_{1}(0)

The claim that
d,e
are independent standard Brownian motions gives that
(\sqrt{2}(mt-e),\sqrt{2}d)=\mathcal{P}B-\mathcal{P}B(0)\sim\mu_{(\lambda_{2},0)}
.
∎

The following corollary extends the Brownian Burke property to the biHecke monoid. For this corollary and in the remainder of the paper, we write
f^{0}:=f-f(0)
for
f\in\mathcal{C}^{I}(\mathbb{R})
, and write
\mathcal{P}^{0}_{\sigma}f=(\mathcal{P}_{\sigma}f)^{0}
.

Corollary 4.14
.

Let
B\sim\mu_{\lambda}
for some vector
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Then for any
\sigma\in\mathfrak{D}_{n}
we have

\mathcal{P}_{\sigma}^{0}B\sim\mu_{\sigma\lambda},

(41)

where
\sigma
acts on
\lambda
by the basic action.

Proof.

First, if
\sigma=\tau_{i}
for some
i=1,\dots,n-1
and
\lambda_{i}<\lambda_{i+1}
, then (
41
) holds by Theorem
4.13
. If
\lambda_{i}\geq\lambda_{i+1}
, then (
41
) holds since
\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{i}}B=B
and
\tau_{i}\lambda=\lambda
. The case of general
\sigma
follows by induction.
∎

We end this section with a simple consequence of Corollary
4.14
and Proposition
4.10
. This corollary can be viewed as implying the existence of stationary measures for Brownian LPP.

Corollary 4.15
.

Consider vectors
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\eta\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
and suppose that
\lambda_{i}>\eta_{j}
for all
i\in\{1,\dots,n\},j\in\{1,\dots,m\}
. Let
W=(B^{\prime},B)\sim\mu_{(\eta,\lambda)}
. Then for
\mathbf{n}\in\{1,\dots,n\}^{k}_{<},\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
, we have

\displaystyle W[(-\infty,\mathbf{n}+m)\to(\mathbf{x},1)]-W[(-\infty,\mathbf{n}+m)\to(0^{k},1)]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}B[(-\infty,\mathbf{n})\to(\mathbf{x},1)],

where here
\mathbf{n}+m=(n_{1}+m,\dots,n_{k}+m)
, and the equality in distribution is joint in all
\mathbf{n},\mathbf{x}
.

Proof.

Let
\sigma\in\mathfrak{D}_{n+m}
be such that
\sigma(\nu,\lambda)=(\lambda,\nu)
under the basic
\mathbb{R}
-action. Then by Proposition
4.10
, we have

W[(-\infty,\mathbf{n}+m)\to(\mathbf{x},1)]=\mathcal{P}_{\sigma}W[(-\infty,\mathbf{n})\to(\mathbf{x},1)]

for all
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{n}
for which either side is defined. On the other hand
\mathcal{P}_{\sigma}^{0}W\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}(B,B^{\prime})
by Corollary
4.14
, yielding the result.
∎

5
RSK on
\mathcal{C}^{\mathbb{N}}(\mathbb{R})
: From Pitman to Busemann

In this section we study limits of Pitman transforms on
\mathcal{C}^{n}(\mathbb{R})
as
n\to\infty
. As discussed in the introduction, this is natural when we work with a line environment of independent Brownian motions. The resulting limits are expressed in terms of Busemann functions. Because of this, in taking this limit we will simultaneously build up a theory of multi-path Busemann functions in Brownian LPP. Throughout this section, we let
B=(B_{i}:i\in\mathbb{Z})
be an environment of independent
2
-sided Brownian motions. Also, recall from Section
2.2
the definition of multi-path Busemann functions and semi-infinite geodesics and optimizers.

5.1
Multi-path Busemann functions

To study multi-path Busemann functions, we first need to understand single-path Busemann functions. The theory in this setting was built by Seppäläinen and Sorensen
[
SS23a
,
SS23b
]
, and we use their results as a starting point.

Theorem 5.1
.

In the environment
B
, define the centered Busemann function ending at
(x,n)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}
in direction
\theta\in[0,\infty)
by

\mathcal{B}^{\theta}(x,n)=\lim_{t\to-\infty}B[(t,{\left\lfloor\theta t+n\right\rfloor})\to(x,n)]-B[(t,{\left\lfloor\theta t+n\right\rfloor})\to(0,1)].

(42)

Then there exists a random countable set
\Theta\subset(0,\infty)
such that the following claims hold on an almost sure set
\Omega
:

1.

(Busemann existence,
[
SS23b
, Theorem 2.5(i)]
,
[
SS23a
, Theorem 3.5(viii)]
). For any
(x,n)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}
and
\theta\in(0,\infty)\setminus\Theta
the above limit exists and is equal to

\lim_{t\to-\infty}B[(t,\pi(t))\to(x,n)]-B[(t,\pi(t))\to(0,1)]

for any function
\pi:(-\infty,x\wedge 0]\to\mathbb{R}
such that
\pi(t)/|t|\to\theta
as
t\to-\infty
. When
\theta=0,n=1
, the above limit exists and
\mathcal{B}^{\theta}(x,1)=B_{1}(x)
.

2.

(Metric composition,
[
SS23a
, Theorem 3.5(vi)]
). We have the following metric composition law for Busemann functions. For any
n>m\in\mathbb{Z}
,
x\in\mathbb{R}
, and direction
\theta\in(0,\infty)\setminus\Theta
we have

\mathcal{B}^{\theta}(x,m)=\max_{z\leq x}\mathcal{B}^{\theta}(z,n)+B[(z,n-1)\to(x,1)].

3.

(Geodesic existence,
[
SS23a
, Theorem 3.1(i), Theorem 4.3(ii)]
). There exist semi-infinite geodesics ending at every point
(x,n)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}
in every direction
\theta\in(0,\infty)
. Moreover, for every
\theta\in(0,\infty)
,
(x,n)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}
there exist
leftmost
and
rightmost
geodesics
\pi^{L},\pi^{R}
from
\theta
to
(x,n)
in the sense that for every geodesic
\tau
in direction
\theta
ending at
(x,n)
we have
\pi^{L}\leq\tau\leq\pi^{R}
.

4.

(Geodesic monotonicity,
[
SS23a
, Theorem 4.3(iii)]
). For
\theta_{1}\leq\theta_{2},n\in\mathbb{N}
and
x_{1}\leq x_{1}
, if
\pi^{L}_{i},i=1,2
is the leftmost geodesic from
\theta_{i}
to
(x_{i},n)
, then
\pi^{L}_{1}\leq\pi^{L}_{2}
. An identical statement holds for rightmost geodesics.

5.

(Geodesic coalescence,
[
SS23b
, Theorem 2.8(i)]
). For
\theta\in(0,\infty)\setminus\Theta
, any two semi-infinite geodesics
\pi,\tau
in direction
\theta
eventually coalesce, i.e.
\pi(t)=\tau(t)
for all small enough
t
. Moreover, there is a unique semi-infinite geodesic in direction
\theta
and ending at
(x,n)
for every
x\in\mathbb{Q},n\in\mathbb{Z}
.

6.

(
0
-directed geodesics,
[
SS23a
, Theorem 4.3(iv, vi)]
). For every
(x,n)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}
the unique semi-infinite geodesic in direction
0
ending at
(x,n)
is the constant path
\pi:(-\infty,x]\to\mathbb{R},\pi\equiv n
.

Moreover, by
[
SS23b
, Theorem 2.5]
,
\mathbb{P}(\theta\in\Theta)=0
for all
\theta>0
. Therefore we may assume
\mathbb{Q}\cap\Theta=\emptyset
on
\Omega
.

We will write
\mathcal{B}^{\theta}(x,n;\mathcal{B})
in place of
\mathcal{B}^{\theta}(x,)
if the environment is not clear from context and let
\mathcal{B}^{\theta}(x):=\mathcal{B}^{\theta}(x,1)
. Moving forward, we will write
\mathcal{I}:=[0,\infty)\setminus\Theta
.

Remark 5.2
.

In the above theorem, we have aimed to follow the notation of
[
SS23a
,
SS23b
]
but have made a few changes in order to better integrate it with our present setup. Our Busemann functions follow semi-infinite paths starting at
t=-\infty
, whereas the semi-infinite paths in
[
SS23a
,
SS23b
]
end at
t=\infty
instead. We also use Brownian motions of variance
2
instead of
1
. A more important difference is that our Busemann function in direction
\theta
is their Busemann function in direction
1/\theta
. This choice is more natural for our eventual study of RSK in the directed landscape. Finally, the versions of Theorem
5.1
.2 and Theorem
5.1
.5 stated in
[
SS23b
]
give queuing representations equivalent to our descriptions.

We now extend the definition of Busemann functions to multiple
distinct
directions. Later we will use a continuity argument to allow for repeated directions. For this definition, for
\theta\in\mathcal{I}^{k}_{\leq}
, write
\pi^{\theta}=(\pi^{\theta_{1}},\dots,\pi^{\theta_{k}})
, where
\pi^{\theta_{i}}
is the unique geodesic in direction
\theta_{i}
ending at
(0,1)
.

Proposition 5.3
.

For
\theta\in\mathcal{I}^{k}_{<},\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
, define

\mathcal{B}^{\theta}(\mathbf{x})=\lim_{t\to-\infty}B[(t,{\left\lfloor\theta|t|+k\right\rfloor})\to(\mathbf{x},1)]-\sum_{i=1}^{k}B[(t,{\left\lfloor\theta_{i}|t|+k\right\rfloor})\to(0,1)].

(43)

Then the following claims hold on an almost sure set
\Omega^{\prime}
.

1.

The limit (
43
) exists for all
k\in\mathbb{N},\theta\in\mathcal{I}^{k}_{<},\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
. Moreover, for any
k
-tuple of semi-infinite paths
\pi=(\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{k})
in direction
\theta
, we have

\mathcal{B}^{\theta}(\mathbf{x})=\lim_{t\to-\infty}B[(t,\pi(t))\to(\mathbf{x},1)]-\sum_{i=1}^{k}B[(t,\pi_{i}(t))\to(0,1)].

(44)

2.

There exists leftmost and rightmost semi-infinite optimizers in every direction
\theta\in[0,\infty)^{k}_{\leq}
ending at every point
(\mathbf{x},1),\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
. Moreover, if we let
\tau^{\mathbf{x},\theta}
denote the leftmost semi-infinite optimizer in direction
\theta
ending at
(\mathbf{x},1)
, then
\tau^{\mathbf{x},\theta}\leq\tau^{\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\theta^{\prime}}
whenever
\mathbf{x}\leq\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\theta\leq\theta^{\prime}
. Monotonicity similarly holds for rightmost optimizers.

3.

Any semi-infinite optimizer
\tau
in a direction
\theta\in\mathcal{I}^{k}_{<}
eventually coalesces with the
k
-tuple of paths
\pi^{\theta}
in the sense that
\tau|_{(-\infty,t]}=\pi^{\theta}|_{(-\infty,t]}
for small enough
t
.

To prove Proposition
5.3
we require two simple lemmas. For the first lemma, we let
\pi^{\theta,y}
denote the rightmost geodesic in direction
\theta
, ending at
(y,1)
.

Lemma 5.4
.

The following event holds a.s. For every
k\in\mathbb{N},a>0
and every compact box
K=\prod_{i=1}^{k}[\theta_{i}^{-},\theta_{i}^{+}]\subset\mathbb{R}^{k}_{<}
there exist vectors
\mathbf{y},\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{Q}^{k}_{\leq}
with
\mathbf{y}\leq-a^{k}\leq a^{k}\leq\mathbf{z}
such that for any
\theta\in K
, the
k
-tuples of semi-infinite rightmost geodesics
\pi^{\theta,\mathbf{y}}:=(\pi^{\theta,y_{1}},\dots,\pi^{\theta,y_{k}})
and
\pi^{\theta,\mathbf{z}}:=(\pi^{\theta,z_{1}},\dots,\pi^{\theta,z_{k}})
are both disjoint
k
-tuples. In particular, they are both disjoint optimizers.

Proof.

By induction it is enough to prove the result when
k=2
. Moreover, by countable additivity it is enough to prove that the result for every fixed
\theta_{i}^{-},\theta_{i}^{+}\in\mathbb{Q}
a.s. and it is enough to prove the existence of
\mathbf{y}
since the existence of
\mathbf{z}
follows by symmetry. Finally, by monotonicity of geodesics (Theorem
5.1
.3),
\pi^{\theta,\mathbf{y}}
is a disjoint
k
-tuple for all
\theta\in K
if and only if
\pi^{(\theta_{1}^{+},\theta_{2}^{-}),\mathbf{y}}
is. We will prove this latter statement.

For every fixed
\theta\geq 0
, the process
x\mapsto\pi^{\theta,x}(x+\cdot)
is stationary, since the same holds for the background environment
B-B(x)
. Therefore for any pair
\theta_{1}<\theta_{2}\in[0,\infty)
, any
w\in\mathbb{R}
, and any function
f:(-\infty,w]\to\mathbb{R}
with
f(t)/|t|\to\theta_{2}
as
t\to\infty
a.s. there exists a random integer
Y<w
such that
\pi^{\theta_{1},Y}(u)<f(u)
for
u\leq Y
. Setting
f=\pi^{\theta_{2}^{-},-a}
and
\mathbf{y}=(Y,-a)
, the lemma follows.
∎

Lemma 5.5
.

The following holds almost surely. For every
k\geq 1,\theta\in\mathcal{I}^{k}_{<}
and
a>0
, there exists
\epsilon>0
,
\mathbf{y}\leq-a^{k}<a^{k}\leq\mathbf{z}
, and
T<y_{1}
such that whenever
\|\theta_{1}-\theta\|_{\infty}<\epsilon,\|\theta_{2}-\theta\|_{\infty}<\epsilon
, we have:

•

For any
i=1,\dots,k
, any geodesics from
\theta_{1,i}
to
y_{i}
and
\theta_{2,i}
to
z_{i}
are equal at time
T
.

•

Any geodesics from
\theta_{1,i}
to
y_{i},i=1,\dots,k
are mutually disjoint. Similarly, any geodesics from
\theta_{2,i}
to
z_{i}
,
i=1,\dots,k
, are mutually disjoint.

For the proof of the lemma, it will be convenient to use the
Hausdorff topology
on finite or semi-infinite paths. We say that a sequence of (nonincreasing cadlag) paths
\pi_{n}:[a_{n},b_{n}]\to\mathbb{N}
converges in the Hausdorff topology to a limiting path
\pi:[a,b]\to\mathbb{R}
for
a<b
if the graphs
\mathfrak{g}\pi_{n}:=\{(r,\pi(r)):r\in[a_{n},b_{n}]\}
converge in the Hausdorff topology on closed sets to the graph
\mathfrak{g}\pi
. We define Hausdorff convergence on semi-infinite paths by asking for Hausdorff convergence when restricted to every compact interval.
Path length is continuous and the disjointness required in the definition of disjoint
k
-tuples is a closed property in this topology, making it convenient to work with when studying LPP. See
[
DNV22
, Section 2.3]
for more discussion.

Proof.

First, fix a compact set
K=\prod_{i=1}^{k}[\theta_{i}^{-},\theta_{i}^{+}]\subset\mathbb{R}^{k}_{<}
containing
\theta
in its interior, and choose
\epsilon_{0}>0
so that if
\|\theta^{\prime}-\theta\|_{\infty}<\epsilon_{0}
then
\theta^{\prime}\in K
. Applying Lemma
5.4
with
K
and
a>0
as in the statement of the lemma implies that the second bullet point above holds as long as we choose
\epsilon<\epsilon_{0}
. It remains to prove that we can choose
\epsilon
small enough so that the first bullet holds. For this, fix a coordinate
\theta_{i}
. Consider sequences
\pi_{i,n}^{\pm},n\in\mathbb{N}
of rightmost geodesics (for
\pi_{i,n}^{+}
) in direction
\theta_{i}\pm 1/n
ending at
(y_{i},1)
(for
\pi_{i,n}^{-}
) and
(z_{i},1)
(for
\pi_{i,n}^{+}
). By monotonicity of geodesics, we have that
\pi_{i,1}^{-}\leq\pi_{i,2}^{-}\leq\cdots\leq\pi_{i,2}^{+}\leq\pi_{i,1}^{+}
. This implies that both of the sequence
\pi_{i,n}^{\pm},n\in\mathbb{N}
converge in the Hausdorff topology, and that the limits
\pi^{+},\pi^{-}
must be geodesics in direction
\theta
ending at
(y_{i},1)
. In particular, by Theorem
5.1
.5, these geodesics must be equal on some interval
(-\infty,t_{i}]
. Hausdorff convergence of
\pi_{i,n}^{\pm}
then implies that for any
t<t_{i}
we have
\pi_{i,n}^{-}|_{[t,t_{i}]}=\pi_{i,n}^{+}|_{[t,t_{i}]}
for all large enough
n
. Choosing
T<\min_{i}(t_{i})
, we get that for large enough
n
, we have
\pi_{i,n}^{-}(T)=\pi_{i,n}^{+}(T)
for all
i=1,\dots,k
. Applying monotonicity of geodesics then yields the first bullet point.
∎

Proof of Proposition
5.3
.

Let
\Omega^{\prime}
be the intersection of the almost sure events in Theorem
5.1
and Lemma
5.5
, and let
\pi
be as in part
1
of the proposition. Fix
a>0
, and let
\mathbf{y},\mathbf{z},\epsilon,T
be as in Lemma
5.5
. Then for
t<T
, if
\theta^{1}<\theta<\theta^{2}
satisfy
\|\theta^{i}-\theta\|_{\infty}<\epsilon
then the optimizers
(t,\pi^{\theta^{1}}(t))
to
(\mathbf{y},1)
and from
(t,\pi^{\theta^{2}}(t))
to
(\mathbf{z},1)
consist of
k
geodesics, and all these geodesics agree at the common time
T
. Therefore for all large enough
t
, by monotonicity of optimizers, all optimizers from
(t,\pi(t))
to a point
\mathbf{x}
with
\mathbf{y}\leq\mathbf{x}\leq\mathbf{z}
agree at time
t
, and so;

B[(t,\pi(t))\to(\mathbf{x},1)]=B[(t,\pi(t))\to(T,\pi^{\theta}(T))]+B[(T,\pi^{\theta}(T))\to(\mathbf{x},1)],

and similarly, for all
i=1,\dots,k
we have

B[(t,\pi_{i}(t))\to(x_{i},1)]=B[(t,\pi_{i}(t))\to(T,\pi^{\theta}_{i}(T))]+B[(T,\pi^{\theta}_{i}(T))\to(\mathbf{x},1)],

To complete the proof of part
1
, we claim that for large enough
t
we have:

\sum_{i=1}^{k}B[(t,\pi_{i}(t))\to(T,\pi^{\theta}_{i}(T))]=B[(t,\pi(t))\to(T,\pi^{\theta}(T))].

Indeed, by the construction in Lemma
5.5
, for any
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
, the geodesics from
(\pi^{\theta_{2}}_{i}(t),t)
to
(y_{i},1)
and from
(\pi^{\theta_{1}}_{i+1}(t),t)
to
(z_{i+1},1)
are disjoint. These geodesics go through the points
(T,\pi^{\theta}_{i}(T)),(T,\pi^{\theta}_{i+1}(T))
. Therefore by monotonicity of geodesics, for any
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
the two geodesics from
(t,\pi_{i}(t))
to
(T,\pi^{\theta}_{i}(T))
and from
(t,\pi_{i+1}(t))
to
(T,\pi^{\theta}_{i+1}(T))
are also disjoint as long as
t
is large enough so that

\pi_{i}(t)\leq\pi^{\theta_{2}}_{i}(t)<\pi^{\theta_{1}}_{i+1}\leq\pi_{i+1}(t).

Therefore for large enough
t
, the
k
geodesics from
(t,\pi(t))
to
(T,\pi^{\theta}(T))
are all disjoint, yielding the above equality and proving part
1
.

We move on to existence of optimizers. We first consider the case when
\theta\in\mathcal{I}^{k}_{<}
. Let all notation be as in the proof of part
1
. In this case, if
\mathbf{y}\leq\mathbf{z}
are as in part
1
, then the
k
-tuples of semi-infinite geodesics to
(\mathbf{y},1)
and
(\mathbf{z},1)
are (unique) optimizers, and these agree on the interval
(-\infty,T]
with
\pi^{\theta}
. By monotonicity of optimizers, for
\mathbf{y}\leq\mathbf{x}\leq\mathbf{z}
, if we then concatenate
\pi^{\theta}|_{(-\infty,T]}
to an optimizer from
(T,\pi(T))
to
(\mathbf{x},1)
, this must be a semi-infinite optimizer in direction
\theta
to
(\mathbf{x},1)
, and all optimizers must be of this form. Existence of rightmost and leftmost optimizers for
\theta\in\mathcal{I}^{k}_{<}
then follows from existence of rightmost and leftmost optimizers in the finite setting.

Now let
\theta\in[0,\infty)^{k}_{\leq},\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
, and let
\theta^{n}\in\mathcal{I}^{k}_{<}
be such that
\theta^{n}\downarrow\theta
. Let
\pi^{n}
be an optimizer to
(\mathbf{x},1)
in direction
\theta^{n}
. Then by monotonicity of optimizers, we have
\pi^{1}\geq\pi^{2}\geq\cdots
and so
\pi^{n}
has a limit
\pi
in the Hausdorff topology satisfying

\limsup_{t\to\infty}\frac{\pi(t)}{|t|}\leq\lim_{n\to\infty}\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{\pi^{n}(t)}{|t|}=\theta.

Since being an optimizer is a closed property in the Hausdorff topology,
\pi
is also an optimizer. We check that
\pi
has direction
\theta
. Let
\theta=(0^{\ell},\theta_{\ell+1},\dots,\theta_{k})
, where
\theta_{\ell+1}>0
. Let
\eta=(0^{\ell},\eta_{\ell+1}<\dots<\eta_{k})
be such that
\eta_{i}\in\mathcal{I}
for all
i
and
\eta\leq\theta
. As in the proof of part
1
, we can find
y_{i}\leq x_{i},i=\ell+1,\dots,k
with
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Q}^{k}_{\leq}
such that all the leftmost geodesics
\tau^{\eta}_{i}
in direction
\eta_{i}
to
(y_{i},1),i=\ell+1,\dots,k
are disjoint. Therefore we can find
y_{\ell}<y_{\ell+1}
so that defining
\tau^{\eta}_{i}:(-\infty,y_{\ell}]\to\mathbb{N}
by
\tau^{\eta}_{i}(x)=i
for all
i
,
\tau^{\eta}
is a disjoint optimizer in direction
\eta
. Monotonicity of optimizers then guarantees that
\tau^{\eta}\leq\pi^{n}
for all
n
and so
\tau^{\eta}\leq\pi
. Hence

\eta=\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{\tau^{\eta}(t)}{|t|}\leq\liminf_{t\to\infty}\frac{\pi(t)}{|t|},

so combining the two displays and letting
\eta\uparrow\pi
gives that
\pi
is an optimizer in direction
\theta
. This completes the proof of existence of an optimizer for general
\theta
.

To argue that there must exist rightmost and leftmost optimizers for general
\theta
, let
\rho,\rho^{\prime}
be two optimizers in direction
\theta
, ending at
(\mathbf{x},1)
. By a standard argument (see the proof of Lemma 2.2,
[
DZ21
]
), the pointwise minima and maxima
\rho\wedge\rho^{\prime}
and
\rho\vee\rho^{\prime}
are also both optimizers, to the left and right of
\rho,\rho^{\prime}
. Moreover, if
\rho_{n}
is a sequence of semi-infinite disjoint optimizers with
\rho_{1}\geq\rho_{2}\geq\cdots
then
\rho_{n}
has a Hausdorff limit
\rho
, which is itself an optimizer to
(\mathbf{x},1)
in direction
\theta
. Indeed, the direction is preserved since all the
\rho_{i}
must be bounded between
\tau^{\eta},\eta\leq\theta
and
\pi^{n},n\in\mathbb{N}
for all
n
, and the optimizer property is preserved since path length is continuous and disjointness is a closed property in the Hausdorff topology. These two facts allow us to apply Zorn’s lemma to the set of optimizers to
(\mathbf{x},1)
in direction
\theta
, yielding a leftmost optimizer (and similarly a rightmost one).
∎

Our next goal is to extend the process
\mathcal{B}^{\theta}(\mathbf{x})
defined in Proposition
5.3
to general
\theta
. We do this by continuity; later we will argue that doing so produces Busemann functions. To facilitate the proof, we need two short lemmas.

Lemma 5.6

(Quadrangle inequality)
.

For
\mathbf{x}\leq\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}_{\geq}
and
\theta\leq\theta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}^{m}_{<}
we have

\mathcal{B}^{\theta}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})+\mathcal{B}^{\theta^{\prime}}(\mathbf{x})\leq\mathcal{B}^{\theta}(\mathbf{x})+\mathcal{B}^{\theta^{\prime}}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})

with equality if there are semi-infinite optimizers
\pi,\pi^{\prime}
in directions
\theta,\theta^{\prime}
ending at
(\mathbf{x},1),(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},1)
such that
\pi(T)=\pi^{\prime}(T)
for some
T\leq x_{1}
.

Proof.

The quadrangle inequality is immediate from appealing to Proposition
5.3
.1 with
\pi=\pi^{\theta}
for direction
\theta
and
\pi=\pi^{\theta^{\prime}}
for direction
\theta^{\prime}
, and then using the usual quadrangle inequality (Lemma
3.4
).
∎

Lemma 5.7
.

The following holds almost surely. For every
k\geq 1,\theta\in\mathcal{I}^{k}_{<}
and
a>0
, there exists
\epsilon>0
, such that if
\|\theta^{\prime}-\theta\|_{\infty}<\epsilon
with
\theta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}^{k}_{<}
we have
\mathcal{B}^{\theta}(\mathbf{x})-\mathcal{B}^{\theta}(0^{k})=\mathcal{B}^{\theta^{\prime}}(\mathbf{x})-\mathcal{B}^{\theta}(0^{k})
whenever
\mathbf{x}\in[-a,a]^{k}_{\leq}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{y},\mathbf{z},\epsilon
be as in Lemma
5.5
. Then by the ‘equality if’ claim in Lemma
3.4
, for
\theta_{1}\leq\theta_{2}
such that
\|\theta-\theta_{j}\|_{\infty}<\epsilon,j=1,2
we have

\mathcal{B}^{\theta_{1}}(\mathbf{y})+\mathcal{B}^{\theta_{2}}(\mathbf{z})=\mathcal{B}^{\theta_{1}}(\mathbf{z})+\mathcal{B}^{\theta_{2}}(\mathbf{y}).

The quadrangle inequality in Lemma
3.4
forces this equality to hold when we replace
\mathbf{y},\mathbf{z}
with any points in
[-a,a]^{k}
, yielding the result.
∎

We will move to the case of repeated endpoints by first extending the process by continuity.
When we extend to the case of repeated endpoints, we cannot normalize our Busemann functions by subtracting off
k
single-path last passage values as in (
43
). Rather, we need a normalization that treats repeated endpoints together. First, define the
over-normalized Busemann function

\hat{\mathcal{B}}^{\theta}(\mathbf{x})=\lim_{\theta^{\prime}\downarrow\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}^{k}_{<}}\mathcal{B}^{\theta^{\prime}}(\mathbf{x})-\mathcal{B}^{\theta^{\prime}}(0^{k}).

(45)

This Busemann function exists by a simple monotonicity argument, but loses some information about the process. We also define a minimally normalized Busemann function
\mathcal{B}
which has a more involved definition. First, for
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
, let
\Pi(\theta)=(I_{1}(\theta),\dots,I_{m(\theta)}(\theta))
be the partition of
\{1,\dots,k\}
such that
\theta_{i}=\theta_{j}
if and only if
i,j
are in the same part of
\Pi(\theta)
.
Also, for
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
and
I=\{i_{1},\dots,i_{\ell}\}\subset\{1,\dots,k\}
, write
\mathbf{x}^{I}=(x_{i_{1}},\dots,x_{i_{\ell}})\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}_{\leq}
. We use the same notation when
I=(i_{1},\dots,i_{\ell})
is a vector in
\{1,\dots,k\}^{\ell}_{<}
. Then for
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
and
\theta\in[0,\infty)^{k}_{\leq}
we can define the
Busemann function

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}^{\theta}(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=\lim_{\theta^{\prime}\downarrow\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}^{k}_{<}}\mathcal{B}^{\theta^{\prime}}(\mathbf{x})-\sum_{j=1}^{m(\theta)}\mathcal{B}^{\theta^{\prime I_{j}(\theta)}}(0^{|I_{j}(\theta)|})

(46)

The strategy for proving that (
46
) is well-defined will be to first work with the over-normalized function
\hat{\mathcal{B}}
, and then compare normalizations. To do this comparison, we will need the following definition. Consider
\theta\in[0,\infty)^{k}_{\leq}
. We say that
\mathbf{x}\in\operatorname{DJ}(\theta)
if there exists
\epsilon>0
such that if
|\theta^{\prime}-\theta|\leq\epsilon
and
\pi^{\theta^{\prime},i}
is the leftmost optimizer ending at
(\mathbf{x}^{I_{i}(\theta)},1)
in direction
\theta^{\prime I_{i}(\theta)}
, then
(\pi^{\theta^{\prime},1},\dots,\pi^{\theta^{\prime},m(\theta)})
is a (semi-infinite) disjoint
k
-tuple. We can argue exactly as in Lemma
5.4
that almost surely, for all
\theta\in[0,\infty)^{k}_{\leq}
the set
\operatorname{DJ}(\theta)
is non-empty.

Proposition 5.8
.

Almost surely, the limits (
45
) and (
46
) exist for every
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
and
\theta\in[0,\infty)^{k}_{\leq}
. Moreover, for every
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
and every partition
\Pi
of
\{1,\dots,k\}
,
\theta\mapsto\hat{\mathcal{B}}^{\theta}(\mathbf{x})
is a right-continuous function on
[0,\infty)^{k}_{\leq}
.

The two Busemann functions are compatible in the following sense. Let
\Pi(\theta)=(I_{1},\dots,I_{\ell})
. Then almost surely, for any
\theta\in[0,\infty)^{k}_{\leq}
and any
\mathbf{z}\in\operatorname{DJ}(\theta)
we have

\mathcal{B}^{\theta}(\mathbf{x})=\hat{\mathcal{B}}^{\theta}(\mathbf{x})-\hat{\mathcal{B}}^{\theta}(\mathbf{z})+\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}\hat{\mathcal{B}}^{\theta^{I_{j}}}(\mathbf{z}^{I_{j}}).

(47)

Proof.

For
\theta\in[0,\infty)^{k}_{\leq}
and
\mathbf{x}\leq\mathbf{y}
, define

\hat{\mathcal{B}}^{\theta}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\lim_{\theta^{\prime}\downarrow\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}^{k}_{<}}\mathcal{B}^{\theta^{\prime}}(\mathbf{y})-\mathcal{B}^{\theta^{\prime}}(\mathbf{x}).

(48)

By Lemma
5.6
, the function
\theta\mapsto\mathcal{B}^{\theta}(\mathbf{x})-\mathcal{B}^{\theta}(\mathbf{y})
is nondecreasing in
\theta
, so the limit in (
48
) exists in
\mathbb{R}\cup\{-\infty\}
. To see that it cannot be
-\infty
, observe that by a quadrangle inequality (Lemma
3.4
), we have the lower bound

B[(-\infty,(1,\dots,k))\to(\mathbf{y},1)]-B[(\infty,(1,\dots,k))\to(\mathbf{x},1)]\leq B^{\theta}(\mathbf{y})-\mathcal{B}^{\theta}(\mathbf{x})

for all
\theta\in\mathcal{I}^{k}_{<}
.
To prove right continuity of
\hat{\mathcal{B}}^{\theta}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
in
\theta
, given a sequence
\theta_{m}\downarrow\theta
, pick
\theta^{\prime}_{m}\in[\theta_{m},\theta_{m}+1/m]\cap\mathcal{I}^{k}_{<}
so that
|\mathcal{B}^{\theta_{m}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{y})-\mathcal{B}^{\theta_{m}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{x})-\hat{\mathcal{B}}^{\theta_{m}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})|\leq 1/m
. By (
48
),

\mathcal{B}^{\theta^{\prime}_{m}}(\mathbf{y})-\mathcal{B}^{\theta^{\prime}_{m}}(\mathbf{x})\to\hat{\mathcal{B}}^{\theta}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}),
and so
\hat{\mathcal{B}}^{\theta_{m}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\to\hat{\mathcal{B}}^{\theta}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
. Finally, for
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
picking
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
with
\mathbf{y}\leq\mathbf{x}
and
\mathbf{y}\leq 0^{k}
, we see that

\hat{\mathcal{B}}^{\theta}(\mathbf{x})=\hat{\mathcal{B}}^{\theta}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x})-\hat{\mathcal{B}}^{\theta}(\mathbf{y},0^{k}),

which implies the existence of the limit (
45
).

We move to (
47
) and the existence of the limit (
46
). For
\mathbf{z}\in\operatorname{DJ}(\theta)
and
\theta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}^{k}_{<}
sufficiently close to
\theta
, we have that
\mathcal{B}^{\theta^{\prime}}(\mathbf{z})=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\mathcal{B}^{\theta^{\prime I_{i}}}(z_{i}^{\ell_{i}})
. Therefore

\displaystyle\lim_{\theta^{\prime}\downarrow\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}^{k}_{<}}\mathcal{B}^{\theta^{\prime}}(0^{k})

\displaystyle-\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathcal{B}^{\theta^{\prime I_{i}}}(0^{|I_{i}|})

\displaystyle=\lim_{\theta^{\prime}\downarrow\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}^{k}_{<}}\mathcal{B}^{\theta^{\prime}}(0^{k})-\mathcal{B}^{\theta^{\prime}}(\mathbf{z})+\lim_{\theta^{\prime}\downarrow\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}^{k}_{<}}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\mathcal{B}^{\theta^{\prime I_{i}}}(z_{i}^{\ell_{i}})-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\mathcal{B}^{\theta^{\prime I_{i}}}(0^{|I_{i}|})\right)

\displaystyle=\hat{\mathcal{B}}^{\theta}(\mathbf{x})-\hat{\mathcal{B}}^{\theta}(\mathbf{z})+\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}\hat{\mathcal{B}}^{\theta^{I_{j}}}(\mathbf{z}^{I_{j}}).

This yields both (
47
) and (
46
).
∎

For fixed
\theta
,
\mathcal{B}^{\theta}
can alternately be defined in terms of Busemann functions in the original Brownian environment. In a weak sense, this is immediate from the quadrangle inequality. However, to prove a stronger result it will be convenient to have a full characterization of the law of
\mathcal{B}^{\theta}
. This is the goal of the next subsection.

5.2
The law of the multi-path Busemann process

Next, we find the law of the process
\mathcal{B}^{\theta}
constructed in the previous section. The identification of the full law will follow from taking a limit of Pitman maps on
\mathcal{C}^{n}(\mathbb{R})
.

Theorem 5.9
.

Consider
\theta\in[0,\infty)^{n}_{\leq}
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Define
W\in\mathcal{C}^{n}(\mathbb{R})
by:

\sum_{i=1}^{k}W_{i}(x)=\mathcal{B}^{\theta^{\{1,\ldots,k\}}}(x^{i})

(49)

for
x\in\mathbb{R},k\in\{1,\dots,n\}
.
Then:

1.

W^{0}:=W-W(0)\in\mathcal{C}^{n}_{0}(\mathbb{R})
is a sequence of independent two-sided Brownian motions of drift
\sqrt{2\theta_{i}}
.

2.

Suppose that
\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}_{<}
is such that
\mathbf{n}\leq\mathbf{m}
for any
\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}_{<}
with
\theta_{n_{i}}=\theta_{m_{i}}
(equivalently,
\mathbf{n}
satisfies (
33
) for
\theta
). Then almost surely, for
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
, we have

\mathcal{B}^{\theta^{\mathbf{n}}}(\mathbf{x})=W[(-\infty,\mathbf{n})\to(\mathbf{x},1)].

3.

For every
k\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\{\ell_{k},\dots,k\}
be the largest interval with
\theta_{\ell_{k}}=\theta_{k}
. We can recover
W
from its centered version
W^{0}
via the formula

W_{\ell_{k}}(0)+\dots W_{k}(0)=-W^{0}[(-\infty,(\ell_{k},\dots,k))\to(0^{k-\ell_{k}+1},1)].

(50)

The
n=1
case of Theorem
5.9
is part of
[
SS23b
, Theorem 3.7]
. We will use this as an input for proving Theorem
5.9
. As mentioned in the introduction (Equation (
14
)), that theorem also contains a distributional identity for the joint law of the single-path Busemann process that Theorem
5.9
realizes almost surely. Theorem
5.9
is a more detailed version of Theorem
2.4
.

Proof.

First assume that all of the
\theta_{i}
are distinct and nonzero.

Let
W^{0}:=(W_{1}^{0},\dots,W_{n}^{0})\sim\mu_{\sqrt{2\theta}}
, be chosen independently of the environment
B
. For
m=0,1,\dots
, consider the environment
W^{m}:=(B_{1},\dots,B_{m},W^{0})
, and consider the function

\mathcal{K}_{m}(\mathbf{n},\mathbf{x}):=W^{m}[(-\infty,\mathbf{n}+m)\to(\mathbf{x},1)]-W^{m}[(-\infty,\mathbf{n}+m)\to(0^{|\mathbf{n}|},1)].

For every
m
, Corollary
4.15
implies that
\mathcal{K}_{m}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{K}_{0}
. Now consider the metric composition law for
W^{m}
:

W^{m}[(-\infty,\mathbf{n}+m)\to(\mathbf{x},1)]:=\max_{\mathbf{z}\leq\mathbf{x}}W^{0}[(-\infty,\mathbf{n})\to(\mathbf{z},1)]+B[(\mathbf{z},m)\to(\mathbf{x},1)].

(51)

The shape theorems for Brownian last passage percolation (Propositions
3.6
and
3.7
) imply that for any fixed
\mathbf{n}\in\{1,\dots,n\}^{k}_{<},\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
, the argmax in this metric composition law is contained in the box

A(m,\theta):=\prod_{i=1}^{k}[-m/\theta_{i}-m^{3/4},-m/\theta_{i}+m^{3/4}]

with probability tending to
1
as
m\to\infty
. Note also that the location of this argmax is monotone in
\mathbf{x}
by monotonicity of optimizers, so for any fixed
a\in\mathbb{R}
, with probability tending to
1
, for all
\mathbf{x}\in[-a,a]^{k}_{\leq}
, this argmax is contained in
A(m,\theta)
,
and so (
51
) holds with
A(m,\theta)
in place of
\mathbb{R}
. Therefore if for
\mathbf{z}\in A(m,\theta)
, all of the functions

B[(\mathbf{z},m)\to(\mathbf{x},1)]-B[(\mathbf{z},m)\to(0^{|\mathbf{n}|},1)],\qquad\mathbf{x}\in[-a,a]^{k}_{\leq}

above are equal, then all of these functions equal
\mathcal{K}_{m}(\mathbf{n},\mathbf{x})
. Equality of all these functions holds with probability tending to
1
as
m\to\infty
by Lemma
5.7
, and moreover, equals
\hat{\mathcal{B}}^{\theta^{\mathbf{n}}}(\mathbf{x};B)=\mathcal{B}^{\theta^{\mathbf{n}}}(\mathbf{x};B)-\mathcal{B}^{\theta}(0^{k};B)
with probability tending to
1
. Therefore for any compact set
K
in
\mathbf{n},\mathbf{x}
, with probability tending to
1
we have that

\mathcal{K}_{m}(\mathbf{n},\mathbf{x})=\hat{\mathcal{B}}^{\theta^{\mathbf{n}}}(\mathbf{x};B)

for
\mathbf{x}\in[-a,a]^{k}_{\leq}
, and hence in distribution we have the equality

\hat{\mathcal{B}}^{\theta^{\mathbf{n}}}(\mathbf{x})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}W^{0}[(-\infty,\mathbf{n})\to(\mathbf{x},1)]-W^{0}[(-\infty,\mathbf{n})\to(0^{k},1)],

(52)

jointly in all
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{n}
, where
W^{0}\sim\mu_{\sqrt{2\theta}}
. Before moving on to using (
52
) to establish the theorem, let us extend to the case of general
\theta
.

First, observe that the right-hand side of (
52
) is continuous in law in the uniform-on-compact topology on functions of
(\mathbf{n},\mathbf{x})
with respect to the drift vector
\sqrt{2\theta}
, even when we allow repeated elements in
\theta
. Next, let
\theta^{n}=\theta+(1/m,2/m,\dots,n/m)
so that
\theta^{m}\downarrow\theta
. By the right-continuity in Proposition
5.8
we have that

\hat{\mathcal{B}}^{\theta^{m,\mathbf{n}}}(\mathbf{x})\to\hat{\mathcal{B}}^{\theta^{\mathbf{n}}}(\mathbf{x})

for fixed
\mathbf{n},\mathbf{x}
. Therefore by the case when the
\theta_{i}
are distinct and non-zero, (
52
) holds at the level of finite-dimensional distribution. To show that it holds as continuous functions of
(\mathbf{n},\mathbf{x})
, we just need to check that
\hat{\mathcal{B}}^{\theta}
is continuous.

Let
g_{n}=\hat{\mathcal{B}}^{\theta^{n}}-\hat{\mathcal{B}}^{\theta}
. Then
g_{n}\downarrow 0
pointwise, and for
\mathbf{x}\leq\mathbf{y}
, the increment
g_{n}(\mathbf{y})-g_{n}(\mathbf{x})\geq 0
by the quadrangle inequality. Therefore for any
a>0,k\in\mathbb{N}
,

\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in[-a,a]^{k}_{\leq}}|g_{n}(\mathbf{x})|\leq\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in[-a,a]^{k}_{\leq}}|g_{n}(\mathbf{x})-g_{n}(-a^{k})|+|g_{n}(-a^{k})|=|g_{n}(a^{k})-g_{n}(-a^{k})|+|g_{n}(-a^{k})|,

where the final equality uses the quadrangle inequality. Hence
g_{n}\to 0
uniformly on compacts, and so
\hat{\mathcal{B}}^{\theta}
is continuous, yielding (
52
).

Now, consider a coupling of
W^{0},B
where (
52
) holds. Define an environment
W
from
W^{0}
by the formula (
50
).
We claim that Theorem
5.9
.2 then holds. This immediately implies (
49
), and hence the entire theorem. To see why Theorem
5.9
.2 holds, fix a vector
\mathbf{n}
as in that part of the theorem, and first suppose that
\theta^{\mathbf{n}}
consists only of repeated elements. Since last passage commutes with constant shifts of the environment, (
52
) and the definition of
W
implies that

\begin{split}\mathcal{B}^{\theta^{\mathbf{n}}}(\mathbf{x})=\hat{\mathcal{B}}^{\theta^{\mathbf{n}}}(\mathbf{x})&=W^{0}[(-\infty,\mathbf{n})\to(\mathbf{x},1)]-W^{0}[(-\infty,\mathbf{n})\to(0^{k},1)]\\
&=W[(-\infty,\mathbf{n})\to(\mathbf{x},1)].\end{split}

(53)

Now suppose that we can write
\theta^{\mathbf{n}}=(\lambda_{1}^{\ell_{1}},\dots,\lambda_{j}^{\ell_{j}})
for some
\lambda\in[0,\infty)^{j}_{<}
. By (
52
) we have that for some
a\in\mathbb{R}
,

\mathcal{B}^{\theta^{\mathbf{n}}}(\mathbf{x})=W[(-\infty,\mathbf{n})\to(\mathbf{x},1)]+a

simultaneously for all
x
. On the other hand, arguing as in Lemma
5.4
, we can find
\mathbf{y}=(y_{1}^{\ell_{1}},\dots,y_{j}^{\ell_{j}})
by sufficiently spacing out the coordinates
y_{1}<\dots<y_{j}
for which

\mathcal{B}^{\theta^{\mathbf{n}}}(\mathbf{y})=\sum_{i=1}^{j}\mathcal{B}^{\lambda_{i}^{\ell_{i}}}(y_{i}^{\ell_{i}}),\qquad W[(-\infty,\mathbf{n})\to(\mathbf{y},1)]=\sum_{i=1}^{j}W[(-\infty,\mathbf{n}^{i})\to(y_{i}^{\ell_{i}},1)],

where
\mathbf{n}^{i}
are the coordinates of
n
corresponding to the first
\ell_{i}
coordinates of
\theta
that equal
\lambda_{i}
. The right-hand sides above are equal by the repeated-element equality (
53
), yielding the result.
∎

One consequence of Theorem
5.9
is the almost sure continuity of the multi-path Busemann process at a fixed
\theta
. This allows us to relate the functions
\hat{\mathcal{B}}^{\theta},\mathcal{B}^{\theta}
to true Busemann functions in the original Brownian environment.

Lemma 5.10
.

Let
\theta\in[0,\infty)^{k}_{\leq}
, and let
\Pi(\theta)=(I_{1},\dots,I_{m})
. For
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
define

\bar{\mathcal{B}}^{\theta}(\mathbf{x})=\lim_{t\to-\infty}B[(t,{\left\lceil\theta t\right\rceil}+k)\to(\mathbf{x},1)]-\sum_{i=1}^{m}B[(t,{\left\lceil\theta^{I_{j}}t\right\rceil}+k)\to(0^{|I_{j}|},1)].

(54)

Then for any fixed
\theta\in[0,\infty)^{k}_{\leq}
, almost surely
\bar{\mathcal{B}}^{\theta}=\mathcal{B}^{\theta}
for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
and

\bar{\mathcal{B}}^{\theta}(\mathbf{x})=\lim_{t\to-\infty}B[(t,\pi(t))\to(\mathbf{x},1)]-\sum_{i=1}^{m}B[(t,\pi^{I_{j}}(t))\to(0^{|I_{j}|},1)]

for any disjoint
k
-tuple
\pi
ending at
(\mathbf{x},1)
in direction
\theta
.

The process
\bar{\mathcal{B}}^{\theta}
is more geometrically motivated than the original process
\mathcal{B}^{\theta}
. We can think of the latter process as the right-continuous extension of
\bar{\mathcal{B}}^{\theta},\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
to all choices of
\theta
.

Proof.

Let
a<b<a^{\prime}<b^{\prime}
, and consider any disjoint
k
-tuple
\pi
ending at
(\mathbf{x},1)
in direction
\theta
. For
\mathbf{x}\in[a,b]^{k}_{\leq},\mathbf{y}\in[a^{\prime},b^{\prime}]^{k}_{\leq}
set

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{B}}^{\theta+}_{\pi}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})

\displaystyle=\limsup_{t\to-\infty}B[(t,\pi(t))\to(\mathbf{y},1)]-B[(t,\pi(t))\to(\mathbf{x},1)]

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{B}}^{\theta-}_{\pi}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})

\displaystyle=\liminf_{t\to-\infty}B[(t,\pi(t))\to(\mathbf{y},1)]-B[(t,\pi(t))\to(\mathbf{x},1)].

We first show that on
[a,b]^{k}_{\leq}\times[a^{\prime},b^{\prime}]^{k}_{\leq}
, almost surely we have
\bar{\mathcal{B}}^{\theta+}_{\pi}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\bar{\mathcal{B}}^{\theta-}_{\pi}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\hat{\mathcal{B}}^{\theta}(\mathbf{y})-\hat{\mathcal{B}}^{\theta}(\mathbf{x})
. We first deal with the case when
\theta_{1}\neq 0
. Let
\theta_{n}^{-},\theta_{n}^{+}\in\mathcal{I}^{k}_{<}
be such that

\theta_{n,i}^{-}<\theta_{i}<\theta_{n,i}^{+},\qquad\theta_{n}^{-}\to\theta,\theta_{n}^{+}\to\theta\quad\text{ as }n\to\infty.

(55)

Then by the quadrangle inequality, on
[a,b]^{k}_{\leq}\times[a^{\prime},b^{\prime}]^{k}_{\leq}
we have

\hat{\mathcal{B}}^{\theta^{-}_{n}}(\mathbf{y})-\hat{\mathcal{B}}^{\theta^{-}_{n}}(\mathbf{x})\leq\bar{\mathcal{B}}^{\theta\pm}_{\pi}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\leq\hat{\mathcal{B}}^{\theta^{+}_{n}}(\mathbf{y})-\hat{\mathcal{B}}^{\theta^{+}_{n}}(\mathbf{x}).

On the other hand, as continuous functions on
[a,b]^{k}_{\leq}\times[a^{\prime},b^{\prime}]^{k}_{\leq}
, both the left- and right-hand sides above converge in distribution to the same object:
\hat{\mathcal{B}}^{\theta}(\mathbf{y})-\hat{\mathcal{B}}^{\theta}(\mathbf{x})
. This follows from the characterization of the distribution of
\mathcal{B}^{\theta}
in Theorem
5.9
. Hence, almost surely we have
\bar{\mathcal{B}}^{\theta+}_{\pi}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\bar{\mathcal{B}}^{\theta-}_{\pi}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\hat{\mathcal{B}}^{\theta}(\mathbf{y})-\hat{\mathcal{B}}^{\theta}(\mathbf{x})
on
[a,b]^{k}_{\leq}\times[a^{\prime},b^{\prime}]^{k}_{\leq}
, as desired.

In the case when
\theta_{1}=0
we cannot construct
\theta^{-}_{n}
in
[0,\infty)^{k}_{<}
the same way, so a minor modification is needed. Instead, let
\lambda
be such that
\theta=(0^{\ell},\lambda)
for some
\ell\leq k,\lambda\in(0,\infty)^{k-\ell}_{\leq}
. Let
\lambda^{-}_{n}\in\mathcal{I}^{k}_{<}
be such that
\lambda^{-}_{n,i}<\lambda_{i}
for all
i
and
\lambda^{-}_{n}\to\lambda
as
n\to\infty
. Then define

F_{n}(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}B_{i}(x_{1})+\max_{\mathbf{z}\in[x_{1},x_{k}]^{k-\ell}_{\leq}}\mathcal{B}^{\lambda^{-}_{n}}(\mathbf{z},\ell+1)+B[(x_{1}^{\ell},\mathbf{z})\to(\mathbf{x},1)].

Then
F_{n}(\mathbf{y})-F_{n}(\mathbf{x})
defines a lower bound on
\mathcal{B}^{\theta\pm}_{\pi}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
by the quadrangle inequality. Moreover, in law as a function of
\mathbf{z}
we have that

\mathcal{B}^{\lambda^{-}_{n}}(\mathbf{z},\ell+1)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}W^{n}[(-\infty,(\ell+1,\dots,k))\to(\mathbf{z},\ell+1)],

where
(W^{n})^{0}\sim\mu_{\sqrt{2\lambda^{-}_{n}}}
. This uses Theorem
5.9
. Therefore as a function of
\mathbf{x}
,

F_{n}(\mathbf{x})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}(B_{1},\dots,B_{k},W^{n})[(-\infty,(1,\dots,k))\to(\mathbf{x},1)].

Therefore by Theorem
5.9
, as
n\to\infty
, the law of
F_{n}(\mathbf{y})-F_{n}(\mathbf{x})
converges to the law of
\hat{\mathcal{B}}^{\theta}(\mathbf{y})-\hat{\mathcal{B}}^{\theta}(\mathbf{x})
, which gives the desired result.

Next, let
\mathbf{z}\in\operatorname{DJ}(\theta)
and let
\pi
be a disjoint
k
-tuple to
(\mathbf{z},1)
in direction
\theta
. Then almost surely the following holds for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
:

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{B}}^{\theta}(\mathbf{x})-\hat{\mathcal{B}}^{\theta}(\mathbf{z})+\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}\hat{\mathcal{B}}^{\theta^{I_{j}}}(\mathbf{z}^{I_{j}})=\lim_{t\to-\infty}B[(t,\pi(t))\to(\mathbf{x},1)]-B[(t,\pi(t))\to(\mathbf{z},1)]

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}B[(t,\pi^{I_{j}}(t))\to(\mathbf{z}^{I_{j}},1)]+\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}B[(t,\pi^{I_{j}}(t))\to(0^{|I_{j}|},1)].

The two middle terms on the right-hand side above cancel out since
\mathbf{z}\in\operatorname{DJ}(\theta)
, and so the whole expression equals
\bar{\mathcal{B}}^{\theta}(\mathbf{x})
. On the other hand, it also equals
\mathcal{B}^{\theta}(\mathbf{x})
by Proposition
5.8
.
∎

Remark 5.11
.

Lemma
5.10
guarantees the existence of the Busemann function
\bar{\mathcal{B}}^{\theta}
in every fixed direction almost surely, which shows that the set of directions where the Busemann function is not defined has Lebesgue measure
0
. In fact, the size of this set should be at most countable, just as in Theorem
5.1
. An analogue of the coalescence result in Theorem
5.1
should also hold on the set where the Busemann function exists. We do not pursue these directions here. Finally, when
\theta_{1}>0
, for
m\in\mathbb{Z}
we can define the Busemann function in direction
\theta
ending at
(\mathbf{x},m)
by

\bar{\mathcal{B}}^{\theta}(\mathbf{x},m)=\lim_{t\to-\infty}B[(t,{\left\lceil\theta t\right\rceil})\to(\mathbf{x},1)]-\sum_{i=1}^{m}B[(t,{\left\lceil\theta^{I_{j}}t\right\rceil})\to(0^{|I_{j}|},1)].

Lemma
5.10
implies that for fixed
\theta
, this limit exists almost surely for all
\mathbf{x},m
, and we can replace
{\left\lceil\theta\;\cdot\right\rceil}
with any disjoint
k
-tuple in direction
\theta
ending at
(\mathbf{x},m)
. With this definition we have the following metric composition law (the analogue of Theorem
5.1
.2): for fixed
\theta\in(0,\infty)^{k}_{\leq}
, almost surely for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq},m>n
:

\bar{\mathcal{B}}^{\theta}(\mathbf{x},n)=\sup_{\mathbf{z}\leq\mathbf{x}}\bar{\mathcal{B}}^{\theta}(\mathbf{z},m)+B[(\mathbf{z},m+1)\to(\mathbf{x},n)].

where the supremum is over all
\mathbf{z}
such that the last passage value above is well-defined.

5.3
The Busemann shear

In this section, we use Busemann functions and Theorem
5.9
to construct versions of the RSK correspondence for functions
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\mathbb{N}}(\mathbb{R})
, Theorem
2.3
. We first recall a few definitions and restate that theorem.

For an environment
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\mathbb{N}}(\mathbb{R})
, let
Rf(x)=f(-x)
, and for
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
, let
-\mathbf{x}=(-x_{k},\dots,-x_{1})\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
. For an environment of Brownian motions
B=(B_{i},i\in\mathbb{Z})
, we extend the definition of the Busemann process
\mathcal{B}^{\theta}(\mathbf{x};B)
to
\theta\in(-\infty,0]^{k}_{\leq}
and
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
as follows:

\mathcal{B}^{\theta}(\mathbf{x};B)=\mathcal{B}^{-\theta}(-\mathbf{x};RB).

(56)

A quick comparison of the definitions guarantees that when
\theta=(0,\dots,0)
, the unique point in
(-\infty,0]^{k}_{\leq}\cap[0,\infty)^{k}_{\leq}
, our two definitions match up. Recall also the notation

\mathcal{W}^{\lambda}(\mathbf{x})=\mathcal{W}^{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};B)=\mathcal{B}^{\lambda|\lambda|/2}(\mathbf{x},B),

which indexes Brownian Busemann functions by their slope (as
\mathbf{x}\to\infty
), rather than by the Busemann direction in the original Brownian environment.

Theorem 5.12
.

Let
B=(B_{i},i\in\mathbb{N})\in\mathcal{C}^{\mathbb{N}}_{0}(\mathbb{R})
be an environment of independent two-sided Brownian motions. For
a\in\mathbb{R}
, define the environment
B^{a}\in\mathcal{C}^{\mathbb{N}}(\mathbb{R})
by the rule that for
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
we have

\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}B^{a}_{i}(x)=\mathcal{W}^{a^{\ell}}(x^{\ell};B).

Then:

1.

Almost surely,
B^{0}=B
.

2.

For any
a\in\mathbb{R}
,
B^{a}
is a sequence of independent Brownian motions with drift
a
.

3.

Fix
a\in\mathbb{R}
and
\lambda\in(-\infty,0]^{k}_{\leq}\cup[0,\infty)^{k}_{\leq}
. Suppose also that
\lambda+a:=(\lambda_{1}+a,\dots,\lambda_{k}+a)\in(-\infty,0]^{k}_{\leq}\cup[0,\infty)^{k}_{\leq}
. Then a.s. for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
we have that

\mathcal{W}^{\lambda+a}(\mathbf{x};B)=\mathcal{W}^{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};B^{a}).

(57)

4.

For any
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
, almost surely we have that:

(B^{a})^{-a}=B,\qquad(B^{a})^{b}=B^{a+b}.

(58)

In particular, if for
a\in\mathbb{R}
we define
a:\mathcal{C}^{\mathbb{N}}(\mathbb{R})\to\mathcal{C}^{\mathbb{N}}(\mathbb{R})
by letting
a(f)=f^{a}(x)-ax
, then for any countable subgroup
G\subset\mathbb{R}
, this defines a measure-preserving
G
-action on a
\mu^{\mathbb{N}}
-almost sure subset of
\mathcal{C}^{\mathbb{N}}(\mathbb{R})
.

Remark 5.13
.

Theorem
5.12
allows us to naturally extend the definition
\mathcal{W}^{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};B)
to arbitrary
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
. Indeed, for
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
, define

\mathcal{W}^{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};B):=\mathcal{W}^{\lambda-\lambda_{1}}(\mathbf{x};B^{\lambda_{1}}).

This definition is consistent with our original definitions when either
\lambda\in[0,\infty)^{k}_{\leq}
or
\lambda\in(-\infty,0]^{k}_{\leq}
by Theorem
5.12
.3. Moreover, since last passage percolation commutes with a common affine shifts applied to all lines, this extension has the remarkable invariance property that for any
a\in\mathbb{R}
we have

\mathcal{W}^{\lambda+a}(\mathbf{x})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{W}^{\lambda}(\mathbf{x})+a\mathbf{x},

where the equality in distribution is joint in all
\lambda,\mathbf{x}
