. This invariance corresponds to shear invariance in the directed landscape. We call the full process

(\lambda,\mathbf{x})\mapsto\mathcal{W}^{\lambda}(\mathbf{x}),\qquad(\lambda,\mathbf{x})\in\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}

the
extended stationary horizon
.

Proof of Theorem
5.12
.

Part
1
is immediate from the definition of
\bar{\mathcal{B}}
in Lemma
5.10
, and the fact that almost surely,
\bar{\mathcal{B}}^{0^{\ell}}=\mathcal{W}^{0^{\ell}}
for all
\ell
by that lemma. Part
2
is a special case of Theorem
5.9
when
a>0
. When
a<0
, it follows from Theorem
5.9
and the formula (
56
). The first part of part
4
is immediate from Part
3
. The ‘In particular’ claim additionally uses that last passage commutes with a common shift of all functions (Lemma
3.5
). The reason for restricting to a countable subgroup of
\mathbb{R}
is because the almost sure set where (
58
) holds depends on the choice of
a,b
.

We move to part
3
. First, for any
\lambda\in[0,\infty)^{k}_{\leq},a\in\mathbb{R}
by Theorem
5.9
.2 as functions of
\mathbf{x}
we have that

\mathcal{W}^{\lambda}(\mathbf{x},B^{a})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}W[(-\infty,(1,\dots,k))\to(\mathbf{x},1)],

where
W^{0}\sim\mu_{\lambda+a}
and
W
is constructed from
W^{0}
via (
50
).
The right-hand side above is continuous in
\mathbf{x}
almost surely, and hence so is the left. A similar argument holds when
\lambda\in(-\infty,0]^{k}_{\leq}
by (
56
). Therefore it is enough to prove (
57
) almost surely for any fixed
\mathbf{x}
. We break into cases.

Case 1:
\lambda\in[0,\infty)^{k}_{\leq},a>0
.
Consider the vector
\theta^{m}\in[0,\infty)^{m+k}_{\leq}
given by
\theta^{m}=(a^{m},\lambda+a)
. Define the environment
W^{m}\in\mathcal{C}^{m+k}(\mathbb{R})
by letting

\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}W^{m}_{i}(x)=\mathcal{W}^{\theta^{\{1,\ldots,\ell\}}}(x^{\ell}).

for
x\in\mathbb{R},\ell\leq m+k
. Then
W^{m}_{i}=B^{a}_{i}
for
i\leq m
and by Theorem
5.9
we have:

\mathcal{W}^{\lambda+a}(\mathbf{x};B)=W^{m}[(-\infty,(m+1,\dots,m+k))\to(\mathbf{x},1)].

(59)

Now, let
\tilde{W}^{m}\in\mathcal{C}^{m+k}_{0}(\mathbb{R})
be the environment with
W^{m}_{i}=B^{a}_{i}
for
i\leq m
and with
W^{m}_{m+i},1\leq i\leq k
given by the identity:

\sum_{i=1}^{j}W^{m}_{m+i}(x)=\bar{\mathcal{B}}^{\lambda}(x^{j},m+1;B^{a}),\qquad 1\leq j\leq k.

Here we use notation as in Remark
5.11
.
Then by Theorem
5.9
and the metric composition law (see Remark
5.11
), for any
\mathbf{n}\in\{1,\dots,k\}^{\ell}_{<}
satisfying (
33
) for
\lambda
and
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}_{\leq}
we have that

\mathcal{W}^{\lambda^{\mathbf{n}}}(\mathbf{x};B^{a})=\tilde{W}^{m}[(-\infty,\mathbf{n}+m)\to(\mathbf{x},1)].

(60)

Now, by Theorem
5.9
we have that
(W^{m})^{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}(\tilde{W}^{m})^{0}\sim\mu_{\theta^{m}}
. Moreover,
W^{m}_{i}(0)=\tilde{W}^{m}_{i}(0)=0
for
i\leq m
and we can extract the values of
W^{m}_{i}(0),i\geq m+1
from
(W^{m})^{0}
by the formula in Theorem
5.9
.3. The same formula extracts the values of
\tilde{W}^{m}_{i}(0),i\geq m+1
from
(\tilde{W}^{m})^{0}
by virtue of (
60
) and the fact that
\mathcal{W}^{\lambda^{\mathbf{n}}}(0^{|\mathbf{n}|})=0
whenever
\lambda^{\mathbf{n}}=(y,\dots,y)
for some
y\geq 0
. Putting all this together implies
W^{m}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\tilde{W}^{m}
.

Now, using that
W^{m}_{i}=\tilde{W}^{m}_{i}=B^{a}_{i}
for
i\leq m
and
W^{m}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\tilde{W}^{m}
, letting

\mathcal{F}_{m}
denote the
\sigma
-algebra generated by the lines
B^{a}_{i},i\leq m
formulas (
59
), (
60
) give:

\displaystyle(\mathcal{W}^{\lambda+a}(\mathbf{x};B),\mathbb{E}(\mathcal{W}^{\lambda+a}(\mathbf{x};B)\mid\mathcal{F}_{m}))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}(\mathcal{W}^{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};B^{a}),\mathbb{E}(\mathcal{W}^{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};B^{a})\mid\mathcal{F}_{m})),

(61)

\displaystyle\qquad\mathbb{E}(\mathcal{W}^{\lambda+a}(\mathbf{x};B)\mid\mathcal{F}_{m})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}(\mathcal{W}^{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};B^{a})\mid\mathcal{F}_{m}).

Now, the martingale convergence theorem ensures that

\mathbb{E}(\mathcal{W}^{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};B^{a})\mid\mathcal{F}_{m})\to\mathcal{W}^{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};B^{a})
almost surely as
m\to\infty
, since
\mathcal{W}^{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};B^{a})
is
\sigma(\bigcup_{m=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{F}_{m})
-measurable. We can use this to take the distributional limit of (
61
) as
m\to\infty
to get that

(\mathcal{W}^{\lambda+a}(\mathbf{x};B),\mathcal{W}^{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};B^{a}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}(\mathcal{W}^{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};B^{a}),\mathcal{W}^{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};B^{a})),

which implies that
\mathcal{W}^{\lambda+a}(\mathbf{x};B)=\mathcal{W}^{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};B^{a})
almost surely, as desired.

Case 2:
a+\lambda\in[0,\infty)^{k}_{\leq},a>0,\lambda\in(-\infty,0]^{k}_{\leq}
.
In this case, let
\theta^{m}=(\lambda+a,a^{m})\in[0,\infty)^{m+k}_{\leq}
, and form
W^{m}
from
\theta^{m}
as above.
Let
\mathcal{P}_{\operatorname{RSK}}
be the Pitman transform introduced prior to Corollary
4.12
sending
W^{m}
to an environment with its slopes reversed. Set
X^{m}=\mathcal{P}_{\operatorname{RSK}}W^{m}
.
Since
\lambda(W^{m})=\theta^{m}
is in
\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
, we can apply Proposition
4.10
to recover the lines of
X^{m}
from last passage values across
W^{m}
. In particular, for
j\leq m
we have

\sum_{i=1}^{j}X^{m}_{i}(x)=W^{m}[(-\infty,(k+1,\dots,k+j))\to(x^{j},1)]=\sum_{i=1}^{j}B^{a}_{i}(x).

Here the first equality uses Proposition
4.10
and the second equality uses Theorem
5.9
. Therefore
X_{j}=B^{a}_{j}
for
j\leq m
. We can also recover last passage values in
RW^{m}
from those in
RX^{m}
since
\lambda(RX^{m})=\theta^{m}
is in
\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
:

\displaystyle RX^{m}[(-\infty,(m+1,\dots,m+k))\to(-\mathbf{x},1)]

\displaystyle=RW^{m}[(-\infty,(1,\dots,k))\to(-\mathbf{x},1)]

\displaystyle=W^{m}[(-\infty,(1,\dots,k))\to(\mathbf{x},1)]

\displaystyle=\mathcal{W}^{\lambda+a}(\mathbf{x};B).

(62)

The first equality again uses Proposition
4.10
and the second is easy to see from the definition. The third equality is Theorem
5.9
. Next, let
\tilde{X}^{m}
denote the environment with
X^{m}_{i}=\tilde{X}^{m}_{i}
for
i\leq m
and such that for
1\leq j\leq k
we have

\sum_{i=1}^{j}X^{m}_{m+i}=\bar{\mathcal{B}}^{\lambda}(\mathbf{x},m+1;B^{a}).

Then by the metric composition law (see Remark
5.11
) we have that

\mathcal{W}^{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};B^{a})=R\tilde{X}^{m}[(-\infty,(m+1,\dots,m+k))\to(-\mathbf{x},1)].

(63)

At this point we are in a similar situation to Case 1, with equations (
62
) and (
63
) playing the role of equations (
59
), (
60
) (equivalently, the environments
RX^{m},R\tilde{X}^{m}
take the place of
W^{m},\tilde{W}^{m}
from that case). Taking
m\to\infty
in (
62
) and (
63
) and applying the same martingale argument as above gives that
\mathcal{W}^{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};B^{a})=\mathcal{W}^{\lambda+a}(\mathbf{x};B)
almost surely.

Cases 3 and 4:
\lambda\in(-\infty,0]^{k}_{\leq},a<0
or
a+\lambda\in(-\infty,0]^{k}_{\leq},a<0,\lambda\in[0,\infty)^{k}
.
These are simply cases
1
and
2
applied to the time-reversed environment
RB
.

Case 5:
a>0,\lambda\in(-\infty,0]^{k}_{\leq},a+\lambda\in(-\infty,0]^{k}_{\leq}
.
By Case
2
, we have the almost sure equality
(B^{a})^{-a}=B
. Using this, we can rewrite our desired formula as

\mathcal{W}^{\lambda+a}(\mathbf{x};(B^{a})^{-a})=\mathcal{W}^{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};B^{a}).

This equality holds almost surely by applying Case
3
to the environment
B^{a}
with parameters
-a<0,\lambda+a\in(-\infty,0]^{k}_{\leq}
instead of
a,\lambda
. Case
3
holds for the environment
B^{a}
since we can remove the drift because last commutes with common functional shifts.

Case 6:
a<0,\lambda\in[0,\infty)^{k}_{\leq},a+\lambda\in[0,\infty)^{k}_{\leq}
.
This is case
5
applied to
RB
.
∎

6
RSK for the directed landscape

In this section, we take a final RSK limit to prove Theorem
2.5
. As in the previous section, we will first need to develop a basic theory of multi-path Busemann functions in this setting.

6.1
The extended stationary horizon and the directed landscape

With the machinery we have built studying Brownian LPP, we can now build a comprehensive theory of multi-path Busemann functions for the directed landscape. We start with the analogue of Theorem
5.1
for the directed landscape, which was proven in
[
BSS24
]
and
[
RV21
]
. Throughout the remainder of the paper, we say that a
k
-tuple of continuous functions
\pi=(\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{k})
where
\pi_{i}:(-\infty,t]\to\mathbb{R}
is a
disjoint
k
-tuple
in direction
\theta
to
(\mathbf{x},t)
for some
\theta,\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
, if:

•

\pi_{i}(s)<\pi_{i+1}(s)
for
i=1,\dots,k-1
and
s<t
.

•

\pi(s)/|s|\to\theta
as
s\to-\infty
and
\pi(t)=\mathbf{x}
.

We say that
\pi
is a
(semi-infinite) optimizer
in
\mathcal{L}
if
\pi|_{[s,t]}
is an optimizer for all
s<t
. As per usual, we talk call an optimizer with a single path a geodesic.

Theorem 6.1
.

The following statements hold on a single event of full probability.

1.

(Theorem 3.14,
[
RV21
]
) For every point
p=(x,s)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and every direction
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
, there exist leftmost and rightmost semi-infinite geodesics ending at
p
in direction
\theta
. Call these geodesics
\pi^{\theta,p}
and
\pi_{R}^{\theta,p}
. For fixed
p,\theta
, almost surely
\pi^{\theta,p}=\pi_{R}^{\theta,p}
.

2.

(Theorem 6.3(i),
[
BSS24
]
) For any
t<s
, the functions
(\theta,s)\mapsto\pi^{\theta,(x,s)}(t)
and
(\theta,s)\mapsto\pi_{R}^{\theta,(x,s)}(t)
are nondecreasing in
\theta,s
.

3.

(Theorem 2.5,
[
BSS24
]
) There exists a
random, translation invariant, countably infinite, dense subset
\Xi\subset\mathbb{R}
such that for
\theta\notin\Xi
, if
\pi,\pi^{\prime}
are two semi-infinite geodesics in direction
\theta
, then
\pi(s)=\pi^{\prime}(s)
for all small enough
s
.

4.

(Theorem 5.1,
[
BSS24
]
) Consider
\theta\notin\Xi,p=(y,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and any path
\pi:(-\infty,t]\to\mathbb{R}
with
\pi(s)/|s|\to\theta
as
s\to\infty
. Then the Busemann function

\mathfrak{B}^{\theta}(p;\mathcal{L}):=\lim_{s\to-\infty}\mathcal{L}(\bar{\pi}(s);p)-\mathcal{L}(\bar{\pi}(s);0,0)

exists and does not depend on the choice of
\pi
. Here recall the notation
\bar{\pi}(s):=(\pi(s),s)
. Moreover, for fixed
\pi
, the convergence above is uniform on compact subsets of
p\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

5.

(Theorem 4.3,
[
RV21
]
) For
s<t
and
y\in\mathbb{R}
, we have the metric composition law

\mathfrak{B}^{\theta}(y,t;\mathcal{L})=\max_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\mathfrak{B}^{\theta}(x,s;\mathcal{L})+\mathcal{L}(x,s;y,t).

We can now state analogues of the main results of Section
5.1
for the directed landscape.
The proofs are almost identical to the proofs for Brownian LPP, so we will go through the steps briefly. For
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
and
\theta\in\mathcal{J}^{k}_{<}
define

\begin{split}\mathfrak{B}^{\theta}(\mathbf{x},t;\mathcal{L})&:=\lim_{s\to-\infty}\mathcal{L}(\theta|s|,s;\mathbf{x},t)-\sum_{j=1}^{k}\mathcal{L}(\theta_{j}|s|,s;0,0)\end{split}

(64)

When
t=0
we omit it from the notation, and when
\mathcal{L}
is unambiguously defined, we also omit it from notation, so
\mathfrak{B}^{\theta}(\mathbf{x})=\mathfrak{B}^{\theta}(\mathbf{x},0;\mathcal{L})
.

Proposition 6.2
.

Almost surely, the following claims hold for the directed landscape
\mathcal{L}
.

1.

For every
k\in\mathbb{N},a>0,t\in\mathbb{R}
and every compact box
K=\prod_{i=1}^{k}[\theta_{i}^{-},\theta_{i}^{+}]\subset\mathbb{R}^{k}_{<}
there exist vectors
\mathbf{y},\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{Q}^{k}_{\leq}
with
\mathbf{y}\leq-a^{k}\leq a^{k}\leq\mathbf{z}
such that for any
\theta\in K
the
k
-tuples of semi-infinite leftmost geodesics
\pi^{\theta,(\mathbf{y},t)}:=(\pi^{\theta,(y_{1},t)},\dots,\pi^{\theta,(y_{k},t)})
and
\pi^{\theta,\mathbf{z}}:=(\pi^{\theta,(z_{1},t)},\dots,\pi^{\theta,(z_{k},t)})
are both disjoint
k
-tuples. In particular, they are both disjoint optimizers.

2.

For any
a>0,t\in\mathbb{R},\theta\in\mathcal{J}^{k}_{<}
, there exists
\epsilon>0
, there exists
\mathbf{y}\leq-a^{k}<a^{k}\leq\mathbf{z}
and
T<t
such that whenever
\|\theta_{1}-\theta\|_{\infty}<\epsilon,\|\theta_{2}-\theta\|_{\infty}<\epsilon
:

•

For any
i=1,\dots,k
, any geodesics in direction
\theta_{1,i}
to
(y_{i},t)
and direction
\theta_{2,i}
to
(z_{i},t)
are equal at time
T
.

•

Any geodesics from
\theta_{1,i}
to
(y_{i},t),i=1,\dots,k
are mutually disjoint. Similarly, any geodesics from
\theta_{2,i}
to
(z_{i},t)
are mutually disjoint.

3.

The limit (
64
) exists for all
k\in\mathbb{N},\theta\in\mathcal{J}^{k}_{<},\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq},t\in\mathbb{R}
, and equals

\lim_{s\to-\infty}\mathcal{L}(\bar{\pi}(s);\mathbf{x},t)-\sum_{j=1}^{k}\mathcal{L}(\bar{\pi}(s);0,0)

for any
k
-tuple of paths
\pi=(\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{k})
with direction
\theta
.

4.

There exist semi-infinite optimizers in every direction
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
ending at every point in
(\mathbf{x},s)\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}\times\mathbb{R}
.

5.

For any
\theta\leq\theta^{\prime},\mathbf{x}\leq\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
we have

\mathfrak{B}^{\theta}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},t)+\mathfrak{B}^{\theta^{\prime}}(\mathbf{x},t)\leq\mathfrak{B}^{\theta}(\mathbf{x},t)+\mathfrak{B}^{\theta^{\prime}}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},t).

6.

Let
\theta\in\mathcal{J}^{k}_{<},a\in\mathbb{R},\epsilon>0
be as in part
2
above. If
\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\|_{\infty}<\epsilon
and
\theta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{J}^{k}_{<}
as well, then for all
\mathbf{x}\in[-a,a]^{k}_{\leq}
,

\mathfrak{B}^{\theta}(\mathbf{x},t)-\mathfrak{B}^{\theta}(0^{k},0)=\mathfrak{B}^{\theta^{\prime}}(\mathbf{x},t)-\mathfrak{B}^{\theta^{\prime}}(0^{k},0).

7.

For
\mathbf{x},\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
define

\displaystyle\hat{\mathfrak{B}}^{\theta}(\mathbf{x},t)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\theta^{\prime}\downarrow\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}^{k}_{<}}\mathfrak{B}^{\theta^{\prime}}(\mathbf{x},t)-\mathfrak{B}^{\theta^{\prime}}(0^{k})

(65)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{B}^{\theta}(\mathbf{x},t)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\theta^{\prime}\downarrow\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}^{k}_{<}}\mathfrak{B}^{\theta^{\prime}}(\mathbf{x},t)-\sum_{j=1}^{m(\theta)}\mathfrak{B}^{\theta^{\prime I_{j}(\theta)}}(0^{|I_{j}(\theta)|})

(66)

Then the limits above exists for all fixed
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq},t\in\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

Part
1
follows as in the proof of Lemma
5.4
, uses that translation invariance of the directed landscape in place of translation invariance of Brownian last passage percolation. Now fix
\theta\in\mathcal{J}^{k}_{<},t>0,a>0
and let
K=\prod_{i=1}^{k}[\theta_{i}^{-},\theta_{i}^{+}]\subset\mathbb{R}^{k}_{<}
contain
\theta
in its interior. Let
\mathbf{y},\mathbf{z}
be chosen as in part
1
from
a,K
. Let
\theta_{n}^{-}=\theta-1/n,\theta_{n}^{+}=\theta+1/n
so that for large enough
n
,
\theta_{n}^{\pm}\in K
. Let
\pi^{\theta_{n}^{\pm},\mathbf{y}}
denote the
k
-tuple of leftmost geodesics in direction
\theta_{n}^{\pm}
ending at
(\mathbf{y},t)
. Then both of the monotone sequences
\pi^{\theta_{n}^{-},\mathbf{y}}
and
\pi^{\theta_{n}^{+},\mathbf{z}}
have pointwise limits
\pi,\pi^{\prime}
. We have
\pi^{\theta_{n}^{-},\mathbf{y}}\leq\pi\leq\pi^{\prime}\leq\pi^{\theta_{n}^{+},\mathbf{z}}
for all
n
by monotonicity of geodesics (Theorem
6.1
.2), and so
\pi,\pi^{\prime}
must be
k
-tuples of paths in direction
\theta
.

Next, sequences of geodesics that converge pointwise must converge in overlap to limiting geodesics (Lemma
3.10
.2). Therefore both
\pi,\pi^{\prime}
must both be
k
-tuples of geodesics in direction
\theta
, and hence coalesce together at all small enough times by Theorem
6.1
.4. Moreover, overlap convergence ensures that
\pi^{\theta_{n}^{-},\mathbf{y}}|_{K}=\pi|_{K}
and
{\pi}^{\theta_{n}^{+},\mathbf{z}}|_{K}
for all compact sets
K\subset(-\infty,t)
and all large enough
n
, and hence by coalescence of
\pi,\pi^{\prime}
there exists
T
such that
\pi^{\theta_{n}^{-},\mathbf{y}}(T)={\pi}^{\theta_{n}^{+},\mathbf{z}}(T)=\pi(T)=\pi^{\prime}(T)
for some large
n
, yielding part
2
. Therefore by monotonicity of finite optimizers, for
s
small enough, for any
\mathbf{x}\in[-a,a]^{k}_{\leq}
, we have:

\mathcal{L}(\bar{\pi}^{\theta}(s);\mathbf{x},t)-\sum_{j=1}^{k}\mathcal{L}(\bar{\pi}^{\theta}_{j}(s);0,0)=\mathcal{L}(\bar{\pi}(T);\mathbf{x},t)-\sum_{j=1}^{k}\mathcal{L}(\bar{\pi}_{j}(T);0,0),

yielding part
3
.

For Part
4
, we can establish the existence of optimizers in directions
\theta\in\mathcal{J}^{k}_{<}
as in the proof of Proposition
5.3
. Now consider
\theta\notin\mathcal{J}^{k}_{<}
and let
\theta_{n}^{\pm}
be sequences with
\theta_{n,i}^{-}<\theta_{n}<\theta_{n,i}^{+}
that converge to
\theta
and are in
\mathcal{J}^{k}_{<}
. Let
(\mathbf{x},s)\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}\times\mathbb{R}
, and let
\pi_{n}^{-},\pi_{n}^{+}
be sequences of optimizers to
(\mathbf{x},s)
in directions
\theta_{n}^{-},\theta_{n}^{+}
.

Consider the paths
\tau_{n}^{-}=\pi_{n}^{-}\wedge\pi_{n}^{+}
and
\tau_{n}^{+}=\tau_{n}^{+}=\pi_{n}^{-}\vee\pi_{n}^{+}
. Both
\tau_{n}^{-},\tau_{n}^{+}
always consist of disjoint paths. Moreover, from the definition of path length, it is easy to check that

\|\tau_{n,i}^{+}|_{[0,t]}\|_{\mathcal{L}}+\|\tau_{n,i}^{-}|_{[0,t]}\|_{\mathcal{L}}=\|\pi_{n,i}^{+}|_{[0,t]}\|_{\mathcal{L}}+\|\pi_{n,i}^{-}|_{[0,t]}\|_{\mathcal{L}}.

This forces both
\tau_{n}^{-}
and
\tau_{n}^{+}
to be semi-infinite optimizers in directions
\theta_{n}^{-},\theta_{n}^{+}
with
\tau_{n}^{-}\leq\tau_{n}^{+}
. In other words, in the initial choice of
\pi_{n}^{-},\pi_{n}^{+}
we may assume
\pi_{n}^{-}\leq\pi_{n}^{+}
. Similarly, we may assume
\pi_{1}^{-}\leq\pi_{2}^{-}\leq\dots\leq\pi_{2}^{+}\leq\pi_{1}^{+}
. Therefore letting
\pi=\lim_{n\to\infty}\pi_{n}^{+}
, we have that
\pi
is a
k
-tuple of paths with asymptotic direction
\theta
ending at
(\mathbf{x},1)
. Moreover, for any
r_{1}<r_{2}<r_{3}\in(-\infty,t]
we have that

\mathcal{L}(\bar{\pi}(r_{1});\bar{\pi}(r_{2}))+\mathcal{L}(\bar{\pi}(r_{2});\bar{\pi}(r_{3}))=\mathcal{L}(\bar{\pi}(r_{1});\bar{\pi}(r_{3})),

(67)

where
\bar{\pi}(r_{i})=(\pi(r_{i}),r_{i})
, since this equality holds for all the approximations
\pi_{n}^{+}
and the extended landscape is continuous. Lemma 8.2 in
[
DZ21
]
implies that almost surely, any path
\pi
satisfying (
67
) satisfies
\pi(q)\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{<}
for any rational point
q
. Now, for every
n\in\mathbb{Z}\cap(-\infty,t)
, we can find a disjoint optimizer from
\bar{\pi}(n-1)
to
\bar{\pi}(n)
. We can also find a disjoint optimizer from
\bar{\pi}(\lceil t-1\rceil)
to
\bar{\pi}(t)
.
Concatenating together all of these optimizers yields a
k
-tuple of disjoint paths
\pi^{\prime}
satisfying (
67
). Moreover, the asymptotic direction of
\pi^{\prime}
is still
\theta
, since Lemma
3.16
controls how far our new
k
-tuple
\pi^{\prime}
can wander from the linear interpolation of
\pi|_{(-\infty,t]\cap\mathbb{Z}}
. Therefore
\pi^{\prime}
is a disjoint optimizer in direction
\theta
ending at
(\mathbf{x},1)
.

Part
5,6
follows from the quadrangle inequality for finite values of
\mathcal{L}
(Lemma
3.15
) and in Part
7
, the proof in Proposition
5.8
goes through verbatim.
∎

Next, we aim to identify the law of the process
(\theta,\mathbf{x})\mapsto\mathfrak{B}^{\theta}(\mathbf{x})
. We can guess this law by using convergence of Brownian LPP from Theorem
3.11
. Indeed, let
\mathcal{L}^{a}
be as in that theorem. For
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
and
\theta\in\mathcal{J}^{k}_{<}
, we can again attempt to define
\mathfrak{B}^{\theta}(\mathbf{x},0;\mathcal{L}^{a})
via (
64
), with
\mathcal{L}^{a}
used in place of
\mathcal{L}
. Given an interval
[-b,b]
, this is almost surely well-defined for all
\mathbf{x}\in[-b,b]^{k}_{<}
when
a<4\theta_{1}
, and equals a Busemann function in the original environment
B^{a}
:

\mathfrak{B}^{\theta}(\mathbf{x},0;\mathcal{L}^{a})=\mathcal{B}^{a^{3}/(2a-8\theta)}(\mathbf{x},0;B^{a}).

We can identify the joint law in
\theta,\mathbf{x}
of right-hand side above by Theorem
5.12
:

\begin{split}&\mathcal{B}^{a^{3}/(2a-8\theta)}(\mathbf{x},0;B^{a})=\mathcal{W}^{\sqrt{a^{3}/(a-4\theta)}}(\mathbf{x},0;B^{a})\\
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}&\mathcal{W}^{\sqrt{a^{3}/(a-4\theta)}+a}(\mathbf{x},0;B^{0})=\mathcal{W}^{2\theta+O(\theta^{2}/|a|)}(\mathbf{x},0;B^{0}).\end{split}

(68)

In other words, we should expect that
\mathfrak{B}^{\theta}(\mathbf{x};\mathcal{L})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{W}^{2\theta}(\mathbf{x};B)
jointly in all
\theta,\mathbf{x}
, where
B
is an environment of independent drift-free Brownian motions. When
\mathbf{x},\theta
are both singletons, this was established in
[
BSS24
, Theorem 5.3(iii)]
. The next proposition extends this to the level of the whole extended stationary horizon.

Proposition 6.3
.

For any finite or countable subset
F\subset\bigcup_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
, we have the equality in law

\mathfrak{B}^{\lambda/2}(\mathbf{x};\mathcal{L})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{W}^{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};B)

jointly as continuous functions of
\lambda\in F,\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{|\lambda|}_{\leq}
with respect to the compact topology. Moreover, we have the following convergence in law as
a\to-\infty
:

(\mathfrak{B}^{\theta}(\mathbf{x},0;\mathcal{L}^{-a}),\mathcal{L}^{-a})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}(\mathfrak{B}^{\theta}(\mathbf{x},0;\mathcal{L}),\mathcal{L}).

(69)

Here the first coordinate should be viewed as a function of
\theta\in F,\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{|\theta|}_{\leq}
, with the compact topology, and the second coordinate should be viewed as a function on
\mathfrak{X}_{\uparrow}
with the compact topology.

Note that Proposition
6.3
is technically restricted to only looking at finite dimensional distributions in
\lambda
. This is purely to avoid discussing topological issues involving jumps of the Busemann process. To prove Proposition
6.3
, we need a version of the continuous mapping theorem.

Lemma 6.4
.

Suppose that
X_{n},X
are random variables taking values in a metric space
(S,e)
and that
X_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}X
. Let
f:S\to T
be a Borel-measurable function taking values in another metric space
(T,d)
, and suppose that there are functions
f_{m}:S\to T
such that the following conditions hold:

•

f_{m}(X)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}f(X)
as
m\to\infty
.

•

f_{m}(X_{n})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}f_{m}(X)
as
n\to\infty
for all fixed
m
.

•

\lim_{m\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}(d(f_{m}(X_{n}),f(X_{n}))\wedge 1)=0
.

Then
f(X_{n})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}f(X)
.

Proof.

Let
g:T\to\mathbb{R}
be any
1
-Lipschitz function with
\|g\|_{\infty}\leq 1
. To prove the lemma it is enough to show that
\mathbb{E}g\circ f(X_{n})\to\mathbb{E}g\circ f(X)
as
n\to\infty
. For any
m\in\mathbb{N}
we can write

\displaystyle|\mathbb{E}g\circ f(X_{n})-\mathbb{E}g\circ f(X)|

\displaystyle\leq|\mathbb{E}g\circ f(X_{n})-\mathbb{E}g\circ f_{m}(X_{n})|

\displaystyle+|\mathbb{E}g\circ f_{m}(X_{n})-\mathbb{E}g\circ f_{m}(X)|+|\mathbb{E}g\circ f_{m}(X)-\mathbb{E}g\circ f(X)|.

As we take
n\to\infty
, the middle term on the right-hand side above converges to
0
since
f_{m}(X_{n})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}f_{m}(X)
. Moreover, as we take
m\to\infty
, the final term above converges to
0
by the dominated convergence theorem since
g
is continuous and
f_{m}(X)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}f(X)
. Finally, since
g
is
1
-Lipschitz and has
L^{\infty}
-norm at most
1
, we have:

\lim_{m\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}|\mathbb{E}g\circ f(X_{n})-\mathbb{E}g\circ f_{m}(X_{n})|\leq\lim_{m\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}(d(f_{m}(X_{n}),f(X_{n}))\wedge 1)=0,

where the finally bound uses the second bullet point.
∎

Proof of Proposition
6.3
.

The claimed convergence in law implies the equality in distribution, so we focus on this point. In order to prove this convergence in distribution, it suffices to show that if we consider a finite set
F\subset\bigcup_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
and the compact set

K_{b}:=\{(\theta,\mathbf{x}):\theta\in F,\mathbf{x}\in[-b,b]^{|\theta|}_{\leq}\},

we have convergence in law in (
69
) when
(\theta,x)
is restricted to
K_{b}
and convergence of the first coordinates is with respect to the
L^{\infty}
-norm on functions from
K_{b}\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{\pm\infty\}
. We first deal with the case when
F\subset\bigcup_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{R}^{k}_{<}
. We will also assume that
F
has the following
downward closed
property: if
\theta\in F\cap\mathbb{R}^{k}_{<}
and
I\subset\{1,\dots,k\}
, then
\theta^{I}\in F
as well.

We appeal to the framework of Lemma
6.4
. Let
a_{n}\to-\infty
be an arbitrary sequence. The random variables
X_{n},X
in that lemma will be given by
\mathcal{L}^{a_{n}},\mathcal{L}
; the compact topology on functions
h:\mathfrak{X}_{\uparrow}\to\mathbb{R}
is metrizable and the choice of our metric is not important. The function
f
will be given by mapping
h:\mathfrak{X}_{\uparrow}\to\mathbb{R}
to the pair

f(h):=(\limsup_{s\to-\infty}h(\theta|s|,s;\mathbf{x},t)-\sum_{j=1}^{k}h(\theta_{j}|s|,s;0,0),h):K_{b}\times\mathfrak{X}_{\uparrow}\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{\pm\infty\}.

When
h=\mathcal{L}^{a}
, the first coordinate is
\mathfrak{B}^{\theta}(\mathbf{x},0;\mathcal{L}^{-a})
almost surely on all of
K_{b}
when
a
is small enough by Proposition
5.3
,
and is
\mathfrak{B}^{\theta}(\mathbf{x},0;\mathcal{L})
almost surely on all of
K_{b}
by Proposition
6.2
when
h=\mathcal{L}
. We define
f_{m}(h)

to have the same second coordinate as
f(h)
, but with the first coordinate replaced with the following:

\begin{split}h(\theta t_{m},-t_{m};\mathbf{x},t)&-\sum_{j=1}^{k}h(\theta_{j}t_{m},-t_{m};0,0)\\
&+\Big[\inf_{\mathbf{z}\in[-m,m]^{k}_{\leq}}\sum_{j=1}^{k}h(\theta_{j}t_{m},-t_{m};z_{j},0)-h(\theta t_{m},-t_{m};\mathbf{z},0)\Big].\end{split}

(70)

Here
t_{m}
is a sequence that will tend to
\infty
very quickly with
m
; we set this sequence in the course of the proof. In our setup, Proposition
6.2
ensures that the first condition of Lemma
6.4
is satisfied; note that the shape theorem for
\mathcal{L}
(Lemma
3.16
) guarantees that the bracketed term in (
70
) is
0
with probability tending to
1
for all large enough
m
. The second condition follows since
\mathcal{L}^{a}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\mathcal{L}
as
a\to-\infty
and
f_{m}
is continuous. For the final condition, we show that

\lim_{m\to\infty}\liminf_{a\to-\infty}\mathbb{P}(f_{m}(\mathcal{L}^{a})=f(\mathcal{L}^{a}))=1.

(71)

First, using Lemma
5.7
, we can find
\delta>0
such that the following condition holds with probability
1-1/m
for all sufficiently large
a
:

•

For all
\mathbf{x}\in[-m,m]^{k}_{\leq},\theta\in F,\|\theta^{\prime}-\theta\|_{\infty}\leq\delta
:

\mathfrak{B}^{\theta^{\prime}}(\mathbf{x},0;\mathcal{L}^{-a})-\mathfrak{B}^{\theta^{\prime}}(0^{k},0;\mathcal{L}^{-a})=\mathfrak{B}^{\theta}(\mathbf{x},0;\mathcal{L}^{-a})-\mathfrak{B}^{\theta}(0^{k},0;\mathcal{L}^{-a}).

Here the fact that we can use the same
\epsilon
uniformly over all large enough
a
follows from the computation (
68
), which implies that all claims for
\mathcal{L}^{-a}
are implied by a common claim for an undrifted Brownian environment
B^{0}
. Now, the shape theorems for Brownian LPP (Propositions
3.6
and
3.7
) and the quadrangle inequality imply that there exists some
s_{m}<0
, such that for all large enough
a
, the following bound holds with probability
1-1/m
:

•

For all
\mathbf{y}\leq\mathbf{x}\in[-b,b]^{k}_{\leq},\theta\in F,t<s_{m}
, we have:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{B}^{\theta-\delta}(\mathbf{x},0;\mathcal{L}^{-a})-\mathfrak{B}^{\theta-\delta}(\mathbf{y},0;\mathcal{L}^{-a})

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{L}^{-a}(\theta|t|,t;\mathbf{x},0)-\mathcal{L}^{-a}(\theta|t|,t;\mathbf{y},0)

\displaystyle\leq\mathfrak{B}^{\theta+\delta}(\mathbf{x},0;\mathcal{L}^{-a})-\mathfrak{B}^{\theta+\delta}(\mathbf{y},0;\mathcal{L}^{-a})

Combining this with the previous bullet, as long as we take
t_{m}\leq s_{m}
, with probability tending to
1
we have that with probability at least
1-2/m
, for all large enough
a
, for
(\theta,\mathbf{x})\in K_{b}
we have that the first coordinate of
f(\mathcal{L}^{-a})
at
(\theta,\mathbf{x})
equals

\hat{\mathfrak{B}}^{\theta}(\mathbf{x},0;\mathcal{L}^{-a})+\inf_{\mathbf{z}\in[-m,m]^{k}_{\leq}}\sum_{j=1}^{k}\mathfrak{B}^{\theta_{i}}(z_{i},0;\mathcal{L}^{-a})-\hat{\mathfrak{B}}^{\theta}(\mathbf{z},0;\mathcal{L}^{-a}).

On the other hand, the infimum on the right-hand side equals
0
with probability tending to
1
with
m
, uniformly over all large enough
a
. The uniformity over
a
follows from the representation of the processes
\mathfrak{B}^{\cdot}(\cdot;\mathcal{L}^{-a})
in terms of the Busemann process across
B^{0}
, (
68
). In summary, we have shown (
71
) as desired.

It remains to check the case when
F\subset\bigcup_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
(i.e. elements of
F
can have repeated points). Let

f(h):=(\limsup_{s\to-\infty}h(\theta|s|,s;\mathbf{x},t)-\sum_{j=1}^{m(\theta)}h(\theta^{I_{j}(\theta)}|s|,s;0^{|I_{j}(\theta)|},0),h),

and set the approximations
f_{m}
to be given by replacing
\theta
with
\theta+(0,1/m,\dots,(k-1)/m)
in the definition of
f
. Then by the previous case,
f_{m}(\mathcal{L}^{-a})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}f_{m}(\mathcal{L})
as
a\to\infty
for every fixed
m
so the second condition of Lemma
6.4
is satisfied. The first condition is satisfied by Proposition
6.2
.7. Finally, if we let
f_{1},f_{m,1}
denote the first coordinate of these functions, we have

\lim_{m\to\infty}\limsup_{a\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\|f_{1}(\mathcal{L}^{-a})-f_{m,1}(\mathcal{L}^{-a})\|_{\infty}\wedge 1=0.

This follows again since we know the joint law of
f_{1}(\mathcal{L}^{-a}),f_{m,1}(\mathcal{L}^{-a})
for all fixed
a
: it is simply given via Theorem
5.9
, and this explicit representation in terms of Brownian motions allows us to easily check the above convergence to
0
. This yields the third condition of Lemma
6.4
, so by that lemma the proof is complete.
∎

Given that we have identified the law of the process
\mathfrak{B}
, we can now give the analogue of Lemma
5.10
in the limiting setting of
\mathfrak{B}
.

Corollary 6.5
.

For
\theta,\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
let
\Pi(\theta)=(I_{1},\dots,I_{m(\theta)})
and define

\bar{\mathfrak{B}}^{\theta}(\mathbf{x},t;\mathcal{L})=\lim_{s\to-\infty}\mathcal{L}(\theta|s|,s;\mathbf{x},0)-\sum_{j=1}^{m(\theta)}\mathcal{L}(s,\theta^{I_{j}}|s|;\mathbf{x}^{I_{j}},0).

Then for any fixed
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
, almost surely
\bar{\mathcal{B}}^{\theta}(\mathbf{x},t;\mathcal{L})=\mathcal{B}^{\theta}(\mathbf{x},t;\mathcal{L})
for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq},t\in\mathbb{R}
and

\bar{\mathfrak{B}}^{\theta}(\mathbf{x},t;\mathcal{L})=\lim_{s\to-\infty}\mathcal{L}(\pi(s),s;\mathbf{x},0)-\sum_{j=1}^{m(\theta)}\mathcal{L}(s,\pi^{I_{j}}(s);\mathbf{x}^{I_{j}},0)

for any disjoint
k
-tuple
\pi
ending at
(\mathbf{x},1)
in direction
\theta
.

The proof of Lemma
5.10
applies verbatim to Corollary
6.5
, since that proof only used monotonicity/the quadrangle inequality and a characterization of the Busemann law, which we have in this context by Proposition
6.3
. We leave the details to the reader. Corollary
6.5
also implies a metric composition law for Busemann functions in
\mathcal{L}
, which can be used to prove optimizer uniqueness.

Lemma 6.6
.

Fix
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
. Then the following holds almost surely for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
and
s<t
:

\mathfrak{B}^{\theta}(\mathbf{x},t;\mathcal{L})=\max_{\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}}\mathfrak{B}^{\theta}(\mathbf{z},s;\mathcal{L})+\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{z},s;\mathbf{y},t).

(72)

Proof.

We work on the almost sure set where
\mathfrak{B}^{\theta}=\bar{\mathfrak{B}}^{\theta}
from Corollary
6.5
. Let
\pi
be a semi-infinite optimizer to
(\mathbf{x},t)
in direction
\theta
, and for
r<s\leq t
and
\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
define

\mathfrak{B}^{\theta}_{r}(\mathbf{z},s;\mathcal{L})=\mathcal{L}(\pi(r),r;\mathbf{z},s)-\sum_{j=1}^{m(\theta)}\mathcal{L}(r,\pi^{I_{j}}(r);\mathbf{x}^{I_{j}},0).

Then the identity (
72
) holds with
\bar{\mathfrak{B}}^{\theta}_{r}
in place of
\bar{\mathfrak{B}}^{\theta}
on both sides by the usual metric composition law. Since
\pi
is an optimizer we also have that

\mathfrak{B}^{\theta}_{r}(\mathbf{x},t;\mathcal{L})=\mathfrak{B}^{\theta}_{r}(\pi(s),s;\mathcal{L})+\mathcal{L}(\pi(s),s;\mathbf{y},t).

for all
r<s
. Combining these facts with the pointwise convergence
\mathfrak{B}^{\theta}_{r}\to\bar{\mathfrak{B}}^{\theta}=\mathfrak{B}^{\theta}
from Corollary
6.5
yields the result.
∎

Lemma 6.7
.

Fix
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq},\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
, and
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Then almost surely, there is a unique semi-infinite optimizer in direction
\theta
to the point
(\mathbf{x},t)
.

Proof.

If
\pi
is a semi-infinite optimizer in direction
\theta
to
(\mathbf{x},t)
, then using Lemma
6.6
, for every
s<t
, the point
\pi(s)
must be an argmax for the metric composition law (
72
). Therefore to prove Lemma
6.6
it is enough to show that the optimizer in (
72
) is almost surely uniquely achieved for any fixed
s
. This will imply that
\pi
is a.s. uniquely specified at all rational
s
, and hence by continuity is a.s. unique. The proof that the argmax has a unique maximum can be found in
[
DZ21
, Lemma 7.3]
. (Note: that proof gives uniqueness for the same problem when the function
g(\mathbf{z},s):=\mathfrak{B}^{\theta}(\mathbf{z},s;\mathcal{L})
is replaced by
h(\mathbf{z},s):=\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{y},r;\mathbf{z},s)
for general
(\mathbf{y},r)
. However, the only information about the function
h
used in that proof is a soft probabilistic representation which in the context of our function
g
is given by Theorem
5.9
).
∎

6.2
RSK for the directed landscape

Now that we have defined Busemann functions for the directed landscape, we are ready to define the RSK correspondence in the directed landscape limit and prove Theorem
2.5
. We begin by restating a version of the theorem here.

Theorem 6.8
.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a directed landscape, and for every
a\in\mathbb{R}
define a sequence of lines
B^{a}=(B^{a}_{i},i\in\mathbb{N})
via the formula

\sum_{i=1}^{k}B^{a}_{i}(x)=\mathfrak{B}^{(a/2)^{k}}(x^{k},0;\mathcal{L}).

Then:

1.

(Law of the RSK image) The joint law of
(B^{a},a\in\mathbb{R})
is same as the joint law of the processes
(B^{a},a\in\mathbb{R})
defined in Theorem
5.12
. In particular, each
B^{a}
is a sequence of independent two-sided Brownian motions of drift
a
and we have the Busemann isometry

\mathcal{W}^{\theta}(\mathbf{x};B^{a})=\mathfrak{B}^{(\theta+a)/2}(\mathbf{x};\mathcal{L})

for all
\mathbf{x},\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq},k\in\mathbb{N}
.

2.

(Abstract Invertibility) Define
\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\uparrow}:=\{(x,s;y,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}:t\leq 0\}
. There is a measurable function
f:\mathcal{C}^{\mathbb{N}}(\mathbb{R})\to\mathcal{C}(\mathfrak{X}^{-}_{\uparrow})
such that almost surely
f(B^{0})=\mathcal{L}|_{\mathfrak{X}^{-}_{\uparrow}}
.

3.

(Explicit Inversion Formula
1
) For every
a\in R
, as in Theorem
3.11
, for
(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t)\in\mathfrak{X}^{-}_{\uparrow}:=\{(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t)\in\mathfrak{X}_{\uparrow}:t\leq 0\}
define

\mathcal{L}^{a}(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t)=B^{a}[(\mathbf{x},s)_{a}\to(\mathbf{y},t)_{a}]-\frac{a^{2}}{4}(t-s),

where
(\mathbf{x},s)_{a}=(\mathbf{x}-as/4,{\left\lceil sa^{3}/8\right\rceil}+1)
. Then as
a\to-\infty
,
\mathcal{L}^{a}
converges to
\mathcal{L}
in probability, in either of the following equivalent senses:

(a)

For every compact set
K\subset\mathfrak{X}^{-}_{\uparrow}
we have

\sup_{u\in K}|\mathcal{L}^{a}(u)-\mathcal{L}(u)|\to 0

in probability as
a\to-\infty
.

(b)

For every sequence
a_{n}\to-\infty
there is a subsequence
n_{1}<n_{2}<\dots
such that
\mathcal{L}^{a_{n_{k}}}\to\mathcal{L}
almost surely as
k\to\infty
, in the compact topology on functions on
\mathfrak{X}^{-}_{\uparrow}
.

4.

(Explicit Inversion Formula 2) For every
a\in\mathbb{R}
, extend the Brownian line environments
B^{a}
to environments indexed by
i\in\mathbb{Z}
by the rule that:

\sum_{i=1}^{k}B^{a}_{1-i}(x)=\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathcal{L}(0^{k},0;-(a/2)^{k}t,t)-\mathcal{L}(x^{k},0;-(a/2)^{k}t,t),

and use this to extend the definition of
\mathcal{L}^{a}
to all of
\mathfrak{X}_{\uparrow}
. Then as
a\to-\infty
,
\mathcal{L}^{a}
converges to
\mathcal{L}
in probability as functions in the compact topology on
\mathfrak{X}_{\uparrow}
.

Theorem
2.5
is Theorem
6.8
(iv). Note that we have defined
(\mathbf{x},s)_{a}
using a different rounding convention than in the introduction. The two choices are clearly equivalent, but the latter choice associates
\mathfrak{X}^{-}_{\uparrow}
to
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{N}
.

Theorem
6.8
.1 is a consequence of Proposition
6.3
and the Busemann shear characterization, Theorem
5.12
. Most of the remainder of Section
6
is devoted to proving the abstract inversion result in Theorem
6.8
.2. Given abstract invertibility, we can use the following measure-theoretic lemma to move to the explicit inversion formulas in parts
3,4
.

Lemma 6.9
.

Let
\{X_{n},n\in\mathbb{N}\},X
be random variables taking values in a complete separable metric space
(S,d)
equipped with its Borel
\sigma
-algebra, and let
\{Y_{n},n\in\mathbb{N}\},Y
be random variables taking values in a Banach space
(T,\|\cdot\|)
equipped with its Borel
\sigma
-algebra.
Suppose that:

1.

As
n\to\infty
,
(X_{n},Y_{n})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}(X,Y)
and
X_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\to}}X
.

2.

There is a measurable function
h:S\to T
such that
Y=h(X)
a.s.

Then
Y_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\to}}Y
as
n\to\infty
.

Proof.

For every
\epsilon>0
, by Lusin’s theorem we can find a closed set
E\subset S
such that
h|_{E}
is continuous and
\mathbb{P}(X\notin E)\leq\epsilon
. Next, Dugundji’s extension theorem
[
Dug51
]
states that if
S
is any metric space,
E\subset S
is closed, and
T
is a locally convex topological vector space, then any continuous function from
E\to T
can be extended to a continuous function from
S\to T
. In our setting, since Banach spaces are locally convex, this implies that there exists a continuous function
h_{\epsilon}:S\to T
such that
h|_{E}=h_{\epsilon}|_{E}
.
Now, for
\delta>0
we can write

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\|Y_{n}-Y\|>\delta)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}(X\notin E)+\mathbb{P}(\|Y_{n}-Y\|>\delta,X\in E)

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon+\mathbb{P}(\|Y_{n}-h_{\epsilon}(X)\|>\delta)

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon+\mathbb{P}(\|Y_{n}-h_{\epsilon}(X_{n})\|\geq\delta/2)+\mathbb{P}(\|h_{\epsilon}(X_{n})-h_{\epsilon}(X)\|>\delta/2).

(73)

Here the second inequality uses that
Y=h(X)=h_{\epsilon}(X)
when
X\in E
. Now, since
X_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\to}}X
and
h_{\epsilon}
is continuous,
h_{\epsilon}(X_{n})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\to}}h_{\epsilon}(X)
as well, so the second probability in (
73
) converges to
0
as
n\to\infty
. Also, since both of the functions
\|\cdot\|,h_{\epsilon}
are continuous and
(X_{n},Y_{n})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}(X,Y)
we have that
\|Y_{n}-h_{\epsilon}(X_{n})\|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\|Y-h_{\epsilon}(X)\|
. The random variable
\|Y-h_{\epsilon}(X)\|
equals
0
unless
X\in E
, and so

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}(\|Y_{n}-h_{\epsilon}(X_{n})\|\geq\delta/2)\leq\mathbb{P}(\|Y-h_{\epsilon}(X)\|\geq\delta/2)\leq\mathbb{P}(X\in E)\leq\epsilon.

Therefore as
n\to\infty
, the limsup of (
73
) is at most
2\epsilon
. Letting
\epsilon\to 0
completes the proof.
∎

Proof of Theorem
6.8
.3,
6.8
.4 from Theorem
6.8
.2.

We start with part
3
. Our aim is to appeal to Lemma
6.9
. We first observe that the space
T
of functions
f:\mathfrak{X}_{\uparrow}^{-}\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{\pm\infty\}
is a Banach space in the following norm:

\|f\|=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}2^{-i}\frac{\|f|_{K_{i}}\|_{\infty}}{1+\|f|_{K_{i}}\|_{\infty}},

where
K_{i},i\in\mathbb{N}
is any collection of compact subsets of
\mathfrak{X}^{-}_{\uparrow}
whose union is all of
\mathfrak{X}^{-}_{\uparrow}
. Convergence in probability in
(T,\|\cdot\|)
is equivalent to the two types of convergence described in the statement of the theorem.

Now, fix a sequence
a_{n}\to-\infty
. Our goal will be to apply Lemma
6.9
with
Y_{n}=\mathcal{L}^{a_{n}},Y=\mathcal{L}|_{\mathfrak{X}_{\uparrow}^{-}}
. We let
X_{n},X\in\mathcal{C}^{\mathbb{N}}(\mathbb{R})
be the line ensembles given by

\sum_{i=1}^{k}(X_{n})_{i}(x)=\mathfrak{B}^{0^{k}}(x^{k};\mathcal{L}^{a_{n}}),\qquad\sum_{i=1}^{k}X_{i}(x)=\mathfrak{B}^{0^{k}}(x^{k};\mathcal{L}).

From our construction, we actually have that
X_{n}=X
for all
n
. Moreover, Theorem
6.8
.2 implies that
Y=h(X)
for some measurable function
h
. This uses that
\mathcal{L}|_{\mathfrak{X}^{-}_{\uparrow}}
is defined as a function of
\mathcal{L}|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\uparrow}}
. Therefore Theorem
6.8
.3 follows from Lemma
6.9
and the joint convergence

(\mathcal{L}^{a},\mathfrak{B}^{0^{k}}(\cdot;\mathcal{L}^{a}),k\in\mathbb{N})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}(\mathcal{L},\mathfrak{B}^{0^{k}}(\cdot;\mathcal{L}),k\in\mathbb{N})

(74)

as
a\to-\infty
, which is proven in Proposition
6.3
.

The proof of Theorem
6.8
.4 is essentially the same. In this setting, we instead work with the Banach space of functions from
\mathfrak{X}_{\uparrow}\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{\pm\infty\}
with the compact topology, set
Y_{n}=\mathcal{L}^{a_{n}},Y=\mathcal{L}
and let
X_{n},X\in\mathcal{C}^{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathbb{R})
extend the previous definitions of
X_{n},X
by letting

\sum_{i=1}^{k}(X_{n})_{1-i}(x)=\mathfrak{B}^{0^{k}}(-x^{k};\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{a_{n}}),\qquad\sum_{i=1}^{k}X_{1-i}(x)=\mathfrak{B}^{0^{k}}(-x^{k};\hat{\mathcal{L}}),

where
\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{a}(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t)=\mathcal{L}^{a}(-\mathbf{y},-t;-\mathbf{x},-s)
(and similarly for
\mathcal{L}
). Again,
X_{n}=X
for all
n
, and by Proposition
6.3
and symmetry, we again have the convergence
(X_{n},Y_{n})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}(X,Y)
. Finally, we claim that
Y\in\sigma(X)
. From Theorem
6.8
.2 and symmetry, we have that
\mathcal{L}|_{\mathfrak{X}_{\uparrow}^{-}},\hat{\mathcal{L}}|_{\mathfrak{X}_{\uparrow}^{-}}\in\sigma(X)
. Moreover
\mathcal{L}|_{\mathfrak{X}_{\uparrow}}\in\sigma(\mathcal{L}|_{\mathfrak{X}_{\uparrow}^{-}},\hat{\mathcal{L}}|_{\mathfrak{X}_{\uparrow}^{-}})
by the metric composition law, yielding the result.
∎

As discussed in the introduction, we can prove that the directed landscape on a strip is a measurable function of the Airy line ensemble with relative ease given Theorem
6.8
. We restate the result and prove it here. For this corollary, let

\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\uparrow}=\{(x,s;y,t)\in(\mathbb{R}\times[0,1])^{2}:s<t\}.

Corollary 6.10
.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be the directed landscape, and define process
\mathfrak{A}
as follows:

\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathfrak{A}_{i}(x)=\mathcal{L}(0^{k},0;x^{k},1).

Then
\mathfrak{A}\in\mathcal{C}^{\mathbb{N}}(\mathbb{R})
is a parabolic Airy line ensemble and there is a measurable function
f:\mathcal{C}^{\mathbb{N}}(\mathbb{R})\to\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\uparrow})
such that almost surely

f(\mathfrak{A})=\mathcal{L}|_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\uparrow}}.

Corollary
6.10
is a restatement of Theorem
1.2
.

Proof.

First, let
\mathcal{L}_{1}
be a directed landscape, independent of
\mathcal{L}
and define
\mathcal{L}_{2}
so that

•

\mathcal{L}_{2}(x,s;y,t)=\mathcal{L}_{1}(x,s;y,t)
if
t\leq 0
.

•

\mathcal{L}_{2}(x,s;y,t)=\mathcal{L}(x,s;y,t)
if
s\geq 0
.

•

\mathcal{L}_{2}(x,s;y,t)=\max_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{L}_{1}(x,s;z,0)+\mathcal{L}(z,0;y,t)
if
s<0<t
.

The process
\mathcal{L}_{2}
is another directed landscape by the axioms in Theorem
3.8
. Therefore by Theorem
6.8
and temporal shift invariance, we can reconstruct
\mathcal{L}_{2}
on the set

(\mathbb{R}\times(-\infty,1])^{2}_{\uparrow}

from its Busemann functions

\mathcal{B}^{0^{k}}(x^{k},1),k\in\mathbb{N}.

By the metric composition law for Busemann functions (Lemma
6.6
), we can build these using
\mathcal{L}_{1}
and the extended Airy sheet
\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{x},0;\mathbf{y},1)
. By
[
DZ21
, Theorem 1.3]
, the entire extended Airy sheet
\mathcal{S}
is almost surely a measurable function of
\mathfrak{A}
. Therefore since
\mathcal{L}|_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\uparrow}}
is contained as a restriction of
\mathcal{L}_{2}|_{(\mathbb{R}\times(-\infty,1])^{2}_{\uparrow}}
, we have shown that there is a measurable function
g
such that

g(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathfrak{A})=\mathcal{L}|_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\uparrow}}.

Since
\mathcal{L}_{1}
is independent of
\mathcal{L}
, this implies the corollary by the abstract Lemma
6.11
below.
∎

Lemma 6.11
.

Suppose that
X,Y,Z
are random variables taking values in three Polish spaces
S_{1},S_{2},S_{3}
equipped with their Borel
\sigma
-algebras. Assume that
X
is independent of the pair
(Y,Z)
and that there is a measurable function
g:S_{1}\times S_{2}\to S_{3}
such that almost surely

g(X,Y)=Z.

Then there is a measurable function
g^{\prime}:S_{2}\to S_{3}
such that almost surely
g^{\prime}(Y)=Z
.

Proof.

For a Borel set
A\subset S_{3}
, define
Z^{A}_{Y}:=\mathbb{P}(Z\in A\mid Y)
and similarly define
Z^{A}_{X,Y}:=\mathbb{P}(Z\in A\mid X,Y)
.
It is enough to show that almost surely,
Z^{A}_{Y}\in\{0,1\}
for any Borel set
A\subset S_{3}
. This is true for
Z^{A}_{X,Y}
since
Z=g(X,Y)
so we just need to show that
Z^{A}_{Y}=Z^{A}_{X,Y}
almost surely. Indeed, for any product of Borel sets
B_{1}\times B_{2}\in S_{1}\times S_{2}
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[Z^{A}_{Y}\mathbf{1}(X\in B_{1},Y\in B_{2})]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}(X\in B_{1})\mathbb{E}[Z^{A}_{Y}\mathbf{1}(Y\in B_{2})]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}(X\in B_{1})\mathbb{P}(Z\in A,Y\in B_{2})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}(X\in B_{1},Z\in A,Y\in B_{2}).

Here the first and third equalities use independence of
X
and
(Y,Z)
and the second equality is the definition of conditional expectation. Now, we can extend this by applying Dynkin’s
\pi-\lambda
theorem to get that for all Borel sets
B\subset S_{1}\times S_{2}
we have
\mathbb{E}[Z^{A}_{Y}\mathbf{1}((X,Y)\in B))=\mathbb{P}(Z\in A,(X,Y)\in B)
. Since
Z^{A}_{Y}
is also
\sigma(X,Y)
-measurable since it is
\sigma(Y)
-measurable this implies that
Z^{A}_{Y}
is a version of the conditional probability
Z^{A}_{X,Y}
, as desired.
∎

6.3
Single-slit and double-slit Busemann functions

In the remainder of Section
6
we will prove Theorem
6.8
.2. The starting point for the proof lies in the observation that certain semi-infinite optimizers are frozen in the Brownian environments
B^{a}
, and so differences of certain Busemann functions give Busemann functions for paths in restricted domains. Under the limit where
a\to-\infty
and
\mathcal{L}^{a}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\mathcal{L}
we can show that the same phenomenon holds in
\mathcal{L}
, allowing us to construct ‘single-slit’ and ‘double-slit’ Busemann functions. Let us explain more precisely what we mean.

In the Brownian environments
B^{a}
, the leftmost optimizer
\pi=(\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{k+1})
in direction
(0^{k},\theta)
ending at
((x^{k},y),1)
has the following specific form:

i.

\pi_{i}(z)=i
for all
z\leq x,i\leq k
.

ii.

\pi_{k+1}
is a semi-infinite geodesic restricted to avoid the set
D_{x,k}:=(-\infty,x)\times\{1,\dots,k\}
. That is, for any
t<z
, the path
\pi|_{[t,z]}
has maximal length among all path
\tau
from
(t,\pi(t))
to
(z,1)
with
\mathfrak{g}\tau\cap D_{x,k}=\emptyset
, where
\mathfrak{g}\tau=\{(r,\tau(r)):r\in[t,z]\}
.

Indeed, for fixed
x
, then for large enough
y
, the leftmost semi-infinite geodesic
\tau
to
(y,1)
in direction
\theta
will be disjoint from the paths
\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{k}
above. Hence
(\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{k},\tau)
will be an optimizer since both
(\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{k})
and
\tau
are separately optimizers. Monotonicity of optimizers then implies that even if
y
is not large enough so that the semi-infinite geodesic to
(y,1)
is disjoint from
\pi_{k}
, the leftmost optimizer
\pi
in direction
(0^{k},\theta)
ending at
((x^{k},y),1)
must take the above form.
Since the paths
(\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{k})
also form the optimizer from from
(0^{k},\infty)
to
(x^{k},1)
we can actually recover the relative length of
\pi_{k+1}
from multi-path Busemann function values across the Brownian environment. This is immediate once we set up the definitions correctly.

Setting some notation, for
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\mathbb{N}}(\mathbb{R})
, points
(x,n),(y,m)
with
x\leq y
and
n\geq m
, and a set
U\subset\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}
such that there is at least one path
\pi
from
(x,n)\to(y,m)
with
\mathfrak{g}\pi\subset U
define

f[(x,n)\to(y,m)\mid U]=\sup_{\pi:\mathfrak{g}\pi\subset U}\|\pi\|_{f},

where the supremum is over all paths
\pi
from
(x,n)\to(y,m)
with
\mathfrak{g}\pi\subset U
. For
\theta\geq 0
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
, we also define the restricted Busemann function

\mathcal{B}^{\theta}(x;f\mid U)=\lim_{t\to\infty}f[(t,{\left\lfloor\theta t\right\rfloor}+1)\to(x,1)\mid U]-f[(t,{\left\lfloor\theta t\right\rfloor}+1)\to(0,1)].

(75)

In (
75
), the term we subtract off does not depend on the set
U
that we are restricting to. The implies that restricted Busemann functions
\mathcal{B}^{\theta}(x;f\mid U)
are monotone in
U
with respect to set inclusion.

Lemma 6.12
.

Fix
k\in\mathbb{N},x\leq y
and
\theta>0
, and let
B\in\mathcal{C}^{\mathbb{N}}(\mathbb{R})
be an environment of independent Brownian motions of drift
0
. Then almost surely,

\mathcal{B}^{(0^{k},\theta)}((x^{k},y);B)-\mathcal{B}^{0^{k}}(x^{k};B)=\mathcal{B}^{\theta}(y;B\mid D_{x,k}^{c}).

Proof.

Setting some notation, let
\mathcal{B}_{t}^{\theta}(x;f\mid U)
denote the right-hand side of (
75
) before taking a limit in
t
. Similarly, let
\mathcal{B}_{t}^{\theta}(\mathbf{x};f)
denote the right-hand side of (
54
) before taking a limit in
t
. Then for
t\leq x
we have

\mathcal{B}^{(0^{k},\theta)}_{t}((x^{k},y);B)-\mathcal{B}^{0^{k}}_{t}(x^{k};B)=\mathcal{B}^{\theta}_{t}(y;B\mid D_{x,k}^{c}).

Taking a limit in
t
then gives the result.
∎

Lemma
6.12
shows that certain restricted Busemann functions for the Brownian environments
B^{a}
(and hence also
\mathcal{L}^{a}
) can be constructed from the multi-path Busemann process for
\mathcal{L}
. We will examine what this reveals about the environments
\mathcal{L}^{a}
as
a\to-\infty
.
Consider a point
(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}\times(-\infty,0)
, and look at the set

\{(x^{\prime},t^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}\times(-\infty,0]:(x^{\prime},t^{\prime})_{a}\in D_{(x,t)_{a}}\}.

As
a\to-\infty
, this set converges to the ray
(-\infty,x]\times\{t\}
. This suggests that there should be a way to take a limit of Lemma
6.12
to recover a Busemann function in the directed landscape for paths restricted to the slit

S_{x,t}^{-}:=\mathbb{R}\times(-\infty,0]\setminus(-\infty,x)\times\{t\}.

In fact, by a symmetry argument we will be able to prove something stronger: not only can we approximate single-slit Busemann functions for
\mathcal{L}
, but we can also approximate
double-slit Busemann functions
. These are Busemann functions of the form

\mathfrak{B}^{\theta}(y;\mathcal{L}\mid S_{u}):=\lim_{s\to-\infty}\mathcal{L}(\theta|s|,s;y,0\mid S_{u})-\mathcal{L}(\theta|s|,s;0,0),

where
u=(x,s;y,t)
and
U(u)=S^{-}_{x,t}\cap S^{+}_{x,t}
, where
S^{+}_{y,t}=\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus((y,\infty)\times\{t\})
. Here
\theta,y\in\mathbb{R}
, and just as in finite LPP, for a set
S
and
(p;q)=(x,s;y,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
, we write

\mathcal{L}(p;q\mid S)=\sup_{\pi:p\to q,\mathfrak{g}\pi\subset S}\|\pi\|_{\mathcal{L}},

where the supremum is over all paths
\pi
from
p
to
q
with
\mathfrak{g}\pi:=\{(r,\pi(r)):r\in[s,t]\}\subset S\}.
We have omitted the time coordinate from the
\mathfrak{B}^{\theta}
notation since it will always be
0
. We also omit the landscape
\mathcal{L}
when it is clear from context.

The next proposition states our precise result.

Proposition 6.13
.

Fix
u=(x,s;y,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
with
t<0
, and let
z,\mu\in\mathbb{R}
. For
n\in\mathbb{N}
, define

x_{n}=-|s|^{2/3}n^{1/3}/2+x,\quad s_{n}=-|s|^{-1/3}n^{1/3},\quad y_{n}=|t|^{2/3}n^{1/3}/2+y,\quad t_{n}=|t|^{-1/3}n^{1/3},

and as usual let
x_{n}^{n},s_{n}^{n},y_{n}^{n},t_{n}^{n}
denote vectors of repeated elements in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Then the following convergence holds in probability:

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathfrak{B}^{(s_{n}^{n},\mu,t_{n}^{n})}(x_{n}^{n},z,y_{n}^{n};\mathcal{L})-\mathfrak{B}^{(s_{n}^{n},t_{n}^{n})}(x_{n}^{n},y_{n}^{n};\mathcal{L})=\mathfrak{B}^{\theta}(z;\mathcal{L}\mid S_{u}).

(76)

We will prove Proposition
6.13
in Section
6.4
and
6.5
. In the remainder of this section, we use Proposition
6.13
to prove Theorem
6.8
.2. This requires a straightforward metric composition law for double-slit Busemann functions. We state this law together with a metric composition law for single-slit Busemann functions, which we will need later on.

Lemma 6.14
.

Almost surely, for all
\theta\in\mathcal{J},u=(x,s;y,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
with
t<0
and
w\in\mathbb{R}
, the double-slit and single-slit Busemann functions
\mathfrak{B}^{\theta}(w\mid S_{u}),\mathfrak{B}^{\theta}(w\mid S_{x,s}^{-})
exist and we have the following metric composition laws:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{B}^{\theta}(w\mid S_{u})

\displaystyle=\max_{z_{1}\geq x,z_{2}\leq y}\mathfrak{B}^{\theta}(z_{1},s)+\mathcal{L}(z_{1},s;z_{2},t)+\mathcal{L}(z_{2},t;w,0).

(77)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{B}^{\theta}(w\mid S_{x,s}^{-})

\displaystyle=\max_{z_{2}\leq y}\mathfrak{B}^{\theta}(z_{2},t)+\mathcal{L}(z_{2},t;w,0).

(78)

Proof.

We only prove (
77
) as the proof of (
78
) is similar but simpler.
Pick
\theta^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}\cap(\theta,\infty)
.
For every
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, let
\pi^{n}
denote the leftmost semi-infinite geodesic to
(n,0)
in direction
\theta^{\prime}
. By translation invariance of the directed landscape, there exists some random
N\in\mathbb{Z}
such that if
n\geq N
then
\pi^{n}(s)>x
. Therefore for
n>N
we have:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{B}^{\theta}(w\mid S_{u})

\displaystyle=\lim_{r\to-\infty}\max_{z_{1}\geq x,z_{2}\leq y}\mathfrak{B}(\theta|r|,r;z_{1},s)-\mathfrak{B}(\theta|r|,r;0,0)+\mathcal{L}(z_{1},s;z_{2},t)+\mathcal{L}(z_{2},t;w,0)

\displaystyle=\lim_{r\to-\infty}\max_{z_{1}\in[x,\pi^{n}(s)],z_{2}\leq y}\mathfrak{B}(\theta|r|,r;z_{1},s)-\mathfrak{B}(\theta|r|,r;0,0)+\mathcal{L}(z_{1},s;z_{2},t)+\mathcal{L}(z_{2},t;w,0)

\displaystyle=\max_{z_{1}\in[x,\pi^{n}(s)],z_{2}\leq y}\mathfrak{B}^{\theta}(z_{1},s)+\mathcal{L}(z_{1},s;z_{2},t)+\mathcal{L}(z_{2},t;w,0)

Here the first equality is simply the metric composition law for
\mathcal{L}
. The second equality uses that for small enough
r
we have
\pi^{n}(r)>\theta|r|
, and so also using that
\pi^{n}(s)>x
, the geodesic from
(\theta|r|,r)
to
(w,0)
that stays in
S_{u}
will be to the left of
\pi^{n}
, and so in the metric composition law we can maximize over
z_{1}\leq\pi^{n}(s)
. The third equality uses the compact convergence of Busemann functions (Theorem
6.1
.4). Taking
n\to\infty
, we have
\pi^{n}(s)\to\infty
by translation invariance of
\mathcal{L}
, yielding (
77
).
∎

Proof of Theorem
6.8
.2 given Proposition
6.13
.

Proposition
6.3
and Theorem
5.12
imply that we can almost surely reconstruct the whole multi-path Busemann process
(\theta,\mathbf{x})\mapsto\mathfrak{B}^{\theta}(\mathbf{x},0;\mathcal{L})
from
B^{0}
. Moreover, Proposition
6.13
implies that for all rational choices of
w,\theta
, and
u=(x,s;y,t)
with
t<0
, we can almost surely reconstruct the double-slit Busemann functions
\mathfrak{B}^{\theta}(w\mid S_{u})
from
(\theta,\mathbf{x})\mapsto\mathfrak{B}^{\theta}(\mathbf{x},0;\mathcal{L})
.

Now, fix rational points
s<t<0
and introduce the shorthand
\{x,y\}=(x,s;y,t)
. We can define the
shock measure

\mu_{s,t}
for the time interval
(s,t)
by the following formula:

\mu_{s,t}([x_{1},x_{2}]\times[y_{1},y_{2}])=\mathcal{L}\{x_{2},y_{2}\}+\mathcal{L}\{x_{1},y_{1}\}-\mathcal{L}\{x_{1},y_{2}\}-\mathcal{L}\{x_{2},y_{1}\}.

(79)

This defines a positive measure by the quadrangle inequality for
\mathcal{L}
. Moreover, given the measure
\mu_{s,t}
, we can reconstruct the Airy sheet
\mathcal{L}\{\cdot,\cdot\}
via ergodicity of the Airy process
\mathcal{L}(0,0;x,1)+x^{2}
(see
[
PS02
, Section 5]
). Indeed, for any fixed
x_{1}<x_{2}
and
y_{1}
, the following holds almost surely:

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}[\mu_{s,t}([x_{1},x_{2}]\times[y_{1},y_{1}+j])-\mathbb{E}\mu_{s,t}([x_{1},x_{2}]\times[y_{1},y_{1}+j])]

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}\{x_{1},y_{1}\}-\mathcal{L}\{x_{2},y_{1}\}-\mathbb{E}(\mathcal{L}\{x_{1},y_{1}\}-\mathcal{L}\{x_{2},y_{1}\})

\displaystyle+\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}(\mathcal{L}\{x_{2},y_{1}+j\}-\mathcal{L}\{x_{1},y_{1}+j\})-\mathbb{E}((\mathcal{L}\{x_{2},y_{1}+j\}-\mathcal{L}\{x_{1},y_{1}+j\}))

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}\{x_{1},y_{1}\}-\mathcal{L}\{x_{2},y_{1}\}-\mathbb{E}(\mathcal{L}\{x_{1},y_{1}\}-\mathcal{L}\{x_{2},y_{1}\}).

Here the final equality uses ergodicity of the two shifted and rescaled Airy processes
\mathcal{L}\{x_{1},\cdot\}-\mathbb{E}\mathcal{L}\{x_{1},\cdot\}
and
\mathcal{L}\{x_{2},\cdot\}-\mathbb{E}\mathcal{L}\{x_{2},\cdot\}
. This equality holds almost surely, simultaneously for all rational
x_{1},x_{2},y_{1}
. Next, using ergodicity of the shifted and rescaled Airy process
\mathcal{L}\{\cdot,y_{1}\}-\mathbb{E}\mathcal{L}\{\cdot,y_{1}\}
we have that almost surely

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{L}\{x_{1},y_{1}\}-\mathcal{L}\{x_{1}+i,y_{1}\}-\mathbb{E}(\mathcal{L}\{x_{1},y_{1}\}-\mathcal{L}\{x_{1}+i,y_{1}\})=\mathcal{L}\{x_{1},y_{1}\}-\mathbb{E}\mathcal{L}\{x_{1},y_{1}\}.

Therefore almost surely, for all rational
x_{1},y_{1}
the value
\mathcal{L}\{x_{1},y_{1}\}
can be reconstructed from
\mu_{s,t}
and hence by continuity, so can the whole Airy sheet
\mathcal{L}\{\cdot,\cdot\}
.

Therefore if we can reconstruct
\mu_{s,t}([x_{1},x_{2}]\times[y_{1},y_{2}])
almost surely for any rational
s,t,x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2}
from the collection of double-slit Busemann functions with rational parameters, then we can reconstruct all increments
\mathcal{L}(x,s;y,t)
for
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
and
s<t\in\mathbb{Q}\cap(-\infty,0)
and hence the full directed landscape on
\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\uparrow}
by continuity.

Fix
s,t,x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2}
, and define

X^{a}=\mathfrak{B}^{-a}(a\mid S_{\{x_{2},y_{2}\}})+\mathfrak{B}^{-a}(a\mid S_{\{x_{1},y_{1}\}})-\mathfrak{B}^{-a}(a\mid S_{\{x_{1},y_{2}\}})-\mathfrak{B}^{-a}(a\mid S_{\{x_{2},y_{1}\}}).

We claim that in probability we have the convergence

\lim_{a\to\infty}X^{a}=\mu_{s,t}([x_{1},x_{2}]\times[y_{1},y_{2}]),

(80)

from which it follows that
\mu_{s,t}([x_{1},x_{2}]\times[y_{1},y_{2}])
is a measurable function of the double slit Busemann environment. Note that with a bit more work, it can be shown that (
80
) in fact holds almost surely.

Fix
x\in\{x_{1},x_{2}\},y\in\{y_{1},y_{2}\}
. By Lemma
6.14
we have that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{B}^{-a}(a\mid S_{\{x,y\}})=\max_{z_{1}\geq 0,z_{2}\leq 0}\mathfrak{B}^{-a}(z_{1}+x,s)+\mathcal{L}\{z_{1}+x,z_{2}+y\}+\mathcal{L}(z_{2}+y,t;a,0).

(81)

We aim to show that

\mathfrak{B}^{-a}(a\mid S_{\{x,y\}})=\mathfrak{B}^{-a}(x,s)+\mathcal{L}\{x,y\}+\mathcal{L}(y,t;a,0)+Y^{a}(x,y),

(82)

where the error
Y^{a}(x,y)
converges to
0
in distribution as
a\to\infty
for any fixed
x,y
. Indeed, we have the following symmetries of
\mathcal{L}
, see Lemma
3.9
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(z+y,t;a,0)-\mathcal{L}(y,t;a,0)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}t^{1/3}A(t^{-2/3}z)+\frac{2z(a-y)-z^{2}}{t},

\displaystyle\qquad\mathfrak{B}^{-a}(z+x,s)-\mathfrak{B}^{-a}(x,s)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}B(z)-2az.

Here
A
is a (stationary) Airy
2
process, and
B
is a Brownian motion without drift,
and both equalities are jointly as continuous functions of
z
. These identities, together with the shape bound on
\mathcal{L}\{x,y\}
(Lemma
3.16
) imply that
Y^{a}_{x,y}\to 0
in probability as
a\to\infty
, as desired. The claim (
80
) then follows immediately from (
82
) and (
79
).
∎

6.4
From multi-path to restricted Busemann functions

In the remainder of Section
6
we prove Proposition
6.13
. We start with two geometric results that will allow us to connect multi-path Busemann functions to restricted Busemann functions in
\mathcal{L}
.

Lemma 6.15
.

Let
(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t)\in\mathfrak{X}_{\uparrow}
, and let
\pi=(\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{k})
be the a.s. unique optimizer from
(\mathbf{x},s)
to
(\mathbf{y},t)
. Fix
m\in\{0,\dots,k\}
, and consider
x_{m}\leq a\leq x_{m+1}
and
y_{m}\leq b\leq y_{m+1}
, where we use the convention that
x_{0}=y_{0}=-\infty
and
x_{k+1}=y_{k+1}=\infty
. Let

\displaystyle R

\displaystyle=\{(z,r)\in\mathbb{R}\times[s,t]:\pi_{m}(r)<z<\pi_{m+1}(r)\},

and let
\bar{R}
be the closure of
R
. Here again we use the convention that
\pi_{0}=-\infty,\pi_{k+1}=\infty
.
Let
\mathbf{x}_{a}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{m},a,x_{m+1},\dots,x_{k})\in\mathbb{R}^{k+1}
and similarly define
\mathbf{y}_{b}
. Then a.s.

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t)+\mathcal{L}(a,s;b,t\mid R)\leq\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{x}_{a},s;\mathbf{y}_{b},t)\leq\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t)+\mathcal{L}(a,s;b,t\mid\bar{R}).

(83)

Proof.

First, observe that if
\tau
is any path from
(a,s)
to
(b,t)
that stays in
R
, then
(\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{m},\tau,\pi_{m+1},\dots,\pi_{k})
is a disjoint optimizer from
(\mathbf{x}_{a},s)
to
(\mathbf{y}_{b},t)
, and so

\|\tau\|_{\mathcal{L}}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\|\pi_{i}\|_{\mathcal{L}}\leq\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{x}_{a},s;\mathbf{y}_{b},t),

and the first inequality in (
83
) follows. Next, let
\tilde{\pi}=(\tilde{\pi}_{1},\dots,\tilde{\pi}_{m},\tau,\tilde{\pi}_{m+1},\dots,\tilde{\pi}_{k})
be a disjoint
k
-tuple from
(\mathbf{x}_{a},s)
to
(\mathbf{y}_{b},t)
. By monotonicity of optimizers (Lemma
3.13
), have that
\pi_{m}\leq\tau\leq\pi_{m+1}
, so
\|\tau\|_{\mathcal{L}}\leq\mathcal{L}(a,s;b,t\mid\bar{R})
. Also,
\sum_{i=1}^{k}\|\tilde{\pi}_{i}\|_{\mathcal{L}}\leq\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t)
, and the second inequality follows.
∎

Lemma
6.15
has the following consequence for Busemann functions in
\mathcal{L}
. For this proposition, we say that a sequence of functions
\pi_{n}:[0,\infty)\to[-\infty,\infty]
converges to a lower semicontinuous limit
\pi:[0,\infty)\to[-\infty,\infty]
in the
epigraph topology
and write
\pi_{n}\to_{\mathfrak{e}}\pi
if:

\inf_{I}\pi_{n}\to\inf_{I}\pi

(84)

as
n\to\infty

for all open closed intervals
I=[a,b]
with
0\leq a<b<\infty
. The name comes of the fact that this convergence is equivalent to Hausdorff convergence of the epigraphs of
\pi_{n}
to the epigraph of
\pi
. We require that
\pi
be lower semicontinuous so that the limit is unique. We say that
\pi_{n}
converges to an upper semicontinuous function
\pi
in the
hypograph topology
and write
\pi_{n}\to_{\mathfrak{h}}\pi
if (
84
) holds with
\sup
in place of
\inf
. These topologies make the space of lower/upper semicontinuous functions from
[0,\infty)\to[-\infty,\infty]
compact.

Proposition 6.16
.

Consider deterministic sequences
\mathbf{x}^{n},\lambda^{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}_{\leq}
and
\mathbf{y}^{n},\kappa^{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}_{\leq}
. Let
\pi^{n}
be the a.s. unique semi-infinite optimizer in direction
\lambda^{n}
to
(\mathbf{x}^{n},0)
and let
\tau^{n}
be the a.s. unique semi-infinite optimizer in direction
\kappa^{n}
to
(\mathbf{y}^{n},0)
. Next, for
x\in\mathbb{R},t<0
, let
R_{x,t}^{\pm}:[0,\infty)\to[-\infty,\infty]
be the function with
R_{x,t}^{\pm}(t)=x
and
R_{x,t}^{\pm}(r)=\pm\infty
for
r\neq t
, and suppose that the following conditions hold almost surely:

•

For some
u=(x,s;y,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
with
t<0
, we have
\pi^{n}_{n}\to_{\mathfrak{h}}R^{-}_{x,s}
and
\tau^{n}_{1}\to_{\mathfrak{e}}R^{+}_{y,t}
as
n\to\infty
.

•

For any
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
, there exists
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\pi^{n}_{n}(r)<\alpha r<\tau^{n}_{1}(r)

for all
r\leq s-1
and
n\geq n_{0}
.

Then for any
\mu,z\in\mathbb{R}
, the following two convergences hold a.s.

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathfrak{B}^{(\lambda^{n},\mu,\kappa^{n})}(\mathbf{x}^{n},z,\mathbf{y}^{n})-\mathfrak{B}^{(\lambda^{n},\kappa^{n})}(\mathbf{x}^{n},\mathbf{y}^{n})

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{B}^{\mu}(z\mid S_{u})

(85)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathfrak{B}^{(\mu,\kappa^{n})}(z,\mathbf{y}^{n})-\mathfrak{B}^{\kappa^{n}}(\mathbf{y}^{n})

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{B}^{\mu}(z\mid S_{y,t}^{+}).

(86)

To prove Proposition
6.16
, we need two easy preliminary results.

Lemma 6.17
.

Let
u=(x,s;y,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
, and let
R\subset[s,t]\times\mathbb{R}
. Let
[s^{\prime},t^{\prime}]\subset[s,t]
, and let
\tau:[s^{\prime},t^{\prime}]\to\mathbb{R}
be any geodesic with
\mathfrak{g}\tau\subset R
. Then we can write

\mathcal{L}(x,s;y,t\mid R)=\sup_{\pi}\|\pi\|_{\mathcal{L}},

where the supremum is over all paths
\pi
for which the set

\{r\in[s,t]:\pi(r)=\tau(r)\}

is a (possibly empty) closed interval.

Proof.

Suppose that
\pi
is any path from
(x,s)
to
(y,t)
with
\mathfrak{g}\pi\subset R
and non-empty intersection with
\tau
. Let
r_{1}<r_{2}\in[s,t]
be the first and last times when
\pi
and
\tau
agree. Since
\tau
is a geodesic, we have that

\|\pi\|_{\mathcal{L}}\leq\|\pi|_{[s,r_{1}]}\oplus\tau|_{[r_{1},r_{2}]}\oplus\pi|_{[r_{2},t]}\|_{\mathcal{L}},

and since
\tau
stays in
R
, so does the path
\pi|_{[s,r_{1}]}\oplus\tau|_{[r_{1},r_{2}]}\oplus\pi|_{[r_{2},t]}
. Here
\oplus
denotes paths concatenation. Since this path overlaps with
\tau
on a closed interval, the lemma follows.
∎

Lemma 6.18
.

Fix
u=(x,s;y,t)
with
t<0
and a point
(z,r)
with
r<s
and let
w\in\mathbb{R}
. Then almost surely, the restricted landscape values
\mathcal{L}(z,r;w,0\mid S_{u}),\mathcal{L}(z,r;w,0\mid S_{y,t}^{+})
are achieved by paths that stay in the interior of the sets
S_{u},S_{y,t}^{+}
respectively.

Proof.

We only treat the
S_{u}
case, as the
S_{y,t}^{+}
case is similar but simpler. By metric composition,

\mathcal{L}(z,r;w,0\mid S_{u})=\max_{z_{1}\geq x,z_{2}\leq y}\mathcal{L}(z,r;z_{1},s)+\mathcal{L}(z_{1},s;z_{2},t)+\mathcal{L}(z_{2},t;w,0).

(87)

This maximum must be achieved by the landscape shape theorem (Lemma
3.16
). To complete the proof, it suffices to show that this maximum is achieved at a point
(Z_{1},Z_{2})
with
Z_{1}>x,Z_{2}<y
almost surely.
We show this for
Z_{1}
as the proof for
Z_{2}
is similar. First, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the maximization problem

\max_{z_{1}\geq x}\mathcal{L}(z,r;z_{1},s)+\mathcal{L}(z_{1},s;-n,t)

is almost surely achieved uniquely at a point
Y_{n}>x
. This follows since both the functions
z_{1}\mapsto\mathcal{L}(z,r;\cdot,s),z_{1}\mapsto\mathcal{L}(z_{1},s;-n,t)
are locally absolutely continuous with respect to Brownian motion (see
[
CH14
]
) and independent. Now, monotonicity of geodesics implies that if
(Z_{1},Z_{2})
is any pair achieving the maximum in (
87
), then
Z_{1}\geq Y_{n}
whenever
-n<Z_{2}
. Hence
Z_{1}<x
almost surely, as desired.
∎

Proof of Proposition
6.16
.

We only treat the double-slit convergence (
85
), as the single-slit convergence (
86
) is similar but simpler.
We first use Lemma
6.15
to bound the difference
\mathfrak{B}^{(\lambda^{n},\mu,\kappa^{n})}(\mathbf{x}^{n},z,\mathbf{y}^{n})-\mathfrak{B}^{(\lambda^{n},\kappa^{n})}(\mathbf{x}^{n},\mathbf{y}^{n})
above and below.

First, observe that the two bullet points in the lemma guarantee that
x^{n}_{n},\lambda^{n}_{n}\to-\infty
as
n\to\infty
and
y^{n}_{1},\kappa^{n}_{1}\to\infty
as
n\to\infty
. Moreover, these bullets implies that
\pi^{n},\tau^{n}
are disjoint for large enough
n
, and so
(\pi^{n},\tau^{n})
is a semi-infinite disjoint optimizer in direction
(\lambda^{n},\kappa^{n})
to
((\mathbf{x}^{n},\mathbf{y}^{n});0)
. Now let

\tilde{\pi}^{n}_{\mu}(r)=(\pi^{n}(r),\mu|r|,\kappa^{n}(r)),\qquad\mathbf{z}^{n}:=(\mathbf{x}^{n},z,\mathbf{y}^{n}).

By Corollary
6.5
, we have that almost surely:

\begin{split}&\mathfrak{B}^{(\lambda^{n},\mu,\kappa^{n})}(\mathbf{x}^{n},z,\mathbf{y}^{n})-\mathfrak{B}^{(\lambda^{n},\kappa^{n})}(\mathbf{x}^{n},\mathbf{y}^{n})\\
=&\lim_{r\to-\infty}\mathcal{L}(\tilde{\pi}^{n}_{\mu}(r),r;\mathbf{z}^{n},0)-\mathcal{L}((\pi^{n},\kappa^{n})(r),r;(\mathbf{x}^{n},\mathbf{y}^{n}),0)-\mathcal{L}(\mu|r|,r;z,0).\end{split}

(88)

Next, define

R_{n}:=\{(z,r)\in\mathbb{R}\times(-\infty,0]:\pi^{n}_{n}(r)<z<\tau^{n}_{1}(r)\}.

By Lemma
6.15
, (
88
) is bounded above by

X_{n}^{+}:=\limsup_{r\to-\infty}\mathcal{L}(\mu|r|,r;z,0\mid\bar{R}_{n})-\mathcal{L}(\mu|r|,r;z,0),

(89)

and bounded below by

X_{n}^{-}:=\liminf_{r\to-\infty}\mathcal{L}(\mu|r|,r;z,0\mid R_{n})-\mathcal{L}(\mu|r|,r;z,0).

(90)

To complete the proof, we just need to show that for all large enough
n
:

X_{n}^{-}=X_{n}^{+}=\mathfrak{B}^{\mu}(z\mid S_{u}).

(91)

Now, we can find points
z_{-}<z<z_{+}\in\mathbb{R}
such that there are leftmost semi-infinite geodesics
\pi_{\pm}
in direction
\mu\pm 1
to
(z_{\pm},0)
such that

\pi_{-}(s)<x<\pi_{+}(s),\qquad\pi_{-}(t)<y<\pi_{+}(t).

(92)

Now, let
M>0
be large enough so that
\pi^{-}(r)\leq\mu|r|\leq\pi^{+}(r)
for all
r\leq-M
. By the second bullet point, there exists
M^{\prime}>M
such that
\pi_{\pm}|_{(-\infty,-M^{\prime}]}\subset R_{n}
for all large enough
n
. Therefore by Lemma
6.17
, for large enough
n
, in the definition of

\mathcal{L}(\mu|r|,r;z,0\mid A)

where
A=R_{n},\bar{R}_{n},S_{u}
,
it suffices to consider paths that stay in the set

G:=\{(w,r^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}\times(-\infty,-M^{\prime}]:\pi_{-}(r^{\prime})\leq w\leq\pi_{+}(r^{\prime})\}\cup(\mathbb{R}\times[-M^{\prime},0]).

Next, by Lemma
6.18
, for each
m\in\mathbb{N}
almost surely we can find paths
\tau_{-},\tau_{+}
that achieve the restricted landscape values

\mathcal{L}({\left\lfloor\pi_{-}(-{\left\lceil M^{\prime}\right\rceil})\right\rfloor},-{\left\lceil M^{\prime}\right\rceil};z_{-},0\mid S_{u})
and
\mathcal{L}({\left\lceil\pi_{+}(-{\left\lceil M^{\prime}\right\rceil})\right\rceil},-{\left\lceil M^{\prime}\right\rceil};z_{+},0\mid S_{u})
and stay in the interior of the set
S_{u}
. The first bullet point then implies that
\mathfrak{g}\tau_{\pm}\subset R_{n}
for all large enough
n
. Therefore for large enough
n
and
r\geq M^{\prime}
, it suffices to further restrict our paths to the region

G^{\prime}:=G\cap(\{(w,r^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}\times(-M^{\prime},0]:\tau_{-}(r^{\prime})\leq w\leq\tau_{+}(r^{\prime})\}\cup\mathbb{R}\times(-\infty,-M^{\prime}])

in the definitions of
\mathcal{L}(\mu|r|,r;z,0\mid A)

where
A=R_{n},\bar{R}_{n},S_{u}
.
Noting that
G^{\prime}\subset S_{u}\cap R_{n}\cap\bar{R}_{n}
for all large enough
n
, we get that

\mathcal{L}(\mu|r|,-r;z,0\mid R_{n})=\mathcal{L}(\mu|r|,-r;z,0\mid\bar{R}_{n})=\mathcal{L}(\mu|r|,-r;z,0\mid S_{u})

for
r\geq M^{\prime}
and all large enough
n
. This yields (
91
), completing the proof.
∎

6.5
Optimizers converging to rays

To prove Proposition
6.13
, by the subsequential characterization of convergence in probability it is enough to show that for any subsequence
Y\subset\mathbb{N}
that there is a further subsequence
Y^{\prime}\subset Y
such that (
76
) holds almost surely along
Y^{\prime}
.
By Proposition
6.16
, we can show this by studying the shape of the optimizer
\pi^{n}
in direction
s^{n}_{n}
to
(x^{n}_{n},0)
and the optimizer
\tau^{n}
in direction
t^{n}_{n}
to
(y^{n}_{n},0)
. These optimizers are a.s. unique by Lemma
6.7
. More precisely, it is enough to show that for any subsequence
Y\subset\mathbb{N}
, there is a further subsequence
Y^{\prime}\subset Y
along which the two bullets in Proposition
6.16
hold a.s. Using the subsequential characterization of convergence in probability and a Cantor diagonalization argument, this follows from the following proposition.

Proposition 6.19
.

The following three claims hold:

•

For any
a\in(s,0)
and
b\in(t,0)
, as
n\to\infty
we have

\sup_{r\in[a,0]}\pi^{n}_{n}(r)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\to}}-\infty,\qquad\inf_{r\in[b,0]}\tau^{n}_{1}(r)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\to}}\infty.

•

For any
\alpha\in\mathbb{R},\epsilon>0
,

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}(\sup_{r\leq s-\epsilon}\pi^{n}_{n}(r)-\alpha r>0)=0,\qquad\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}(\inf_{r\leq s-\epsilon}\tau^{n}_{1}(r)-\alpha r<0)=0.

•

\sup_{r\leq 0}\pi^{n}_{n}(r)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\to}}x
and
\inf_{r\leq 0}\tau^{n}_{n}(r)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\to}}y
.

The proof of the claims in Proposition
6.19
for
\pi^{n}
and
\tau^{n}
are symmetric, so we only prove the claims for
\pi^{n}
. Moreover, by spatial stationarity and KPZ scale invariance of
\mathcal{L}
it suffices to prove the proposition in the special case when
x=0,s=-1
. In this case
x_{n}=-n^{1/3}/2,s_{n}=-n^{1/3}
.

To prove Proposition
6.19
, define
F^{n}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
by

F^{n}(\lambda,w)=\mathfrak{B}^{((-n^{1/3})^{n},\lambda)}((-n^{1/3}/2)^{n},w)-\mathfrak{B}^{(-n^{1/3})^{n}}((-n^{1/3}/2)^{n}).

Then letting
\pi_{\lambda,w}
denote the a.s. unique semi-infinite geodesic in
\mathcal{L}
in direction
\lambda
to
(w,0)
, we have the following simple observation.

Lemma 6.20
.

For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\lambda,w\in\mathbb{R}
with
\lambda>-n^{1/3}
and
w>-n^{1/3}/2
the following two events differ on a null set:

1.

the geodesic
\pi_{\lambda,w}
is disjoint from the path
\pi^{n}_{n}
.

2.

F^{n}(\lambda,w)=\mathfrak{B}^{\lambda}(w)
.

Proof.

This follows since

\mathfrak{B}^{((\lambda,(n^{1/3})^{n}))}(w,(n^{1/3}/2)^{n})=\mathfrak{B}^{(n^{1/3})^{n}}((n^{1/3}/2)^{n})+\mathfrak{B}^{\lambda}(w)

if and only if there is an optimizer in direction
(n^{1/3})^{n}
to
((n^{1/3}/2)^{n},0)
which is disjoint from a geodesic to
(w,0)
in direction
\lambda
.
∎

We can study the joint law of
F^{n}(\lambda,w),\mathfrak{B}^{\lambda}(w)
by using Theorem
5.12
and the isometry in Theorem
6.8
.1. This yields the following result.

Lemma 6.21
.

Consider the function
G^{n}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}\times\{0,1\}
given by

G^{n}(\lambda,w)=(F^{n}(\lambda,w),\mathbf{1}(F^{n}(\lambda,w)=\mathfrak{B}^{\lambda}(w)).

Then as
n\to\infty
, the finite dimensional distributions of
G^{n}
converge to those of

G(\lambda,1):=(\mathcal{B}^{\lambda}(w\mid S^{-}_{0,-1}),\mathbf{1}(\mathcal{B}^{\lambda}(w;\mathcal{L}\mid S^{-}_{0,-1}))=\mathfrak{B}^{\lambda}(w)).

Proof.

By Theorem
5.12
and the isometry in Theorem
6.8
.1, we can rewrite
F^{n}
in terms of the Brownian environment
B^{-2n^{1/3}}
. Indeed, by Lemma
6.12
we have

\displaystyle\mathfrak{B}^{((-n^{1/3})^{n},\lambda)}

\displaystyle((-n^{1/3}/2)^{n},w)-\mathfrak{B}^{(-n^{1/3})^{n}}((-n^{1/3}/2)^{n})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{W}^{2(\lambda+n^{1/3})}(w;B^{-2n^{1/3}}\mid D^{c}_{(-n^{1/3}/2,n)})

\displaystyle=\max_{-n^{1/3}/2\leq z\leq w}\mathcal{W}^{2(\lambda+n^{1/3})}(z,n+1;B^{-2n^{1/3}})+B^{-2n^{1/3}}[(z,n)\to(w,1)].

We also have the identity

\displaystyle\mathfrak{B}^{\lambda}(w)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{W}^{2(\lambda+n^{1/3})}(w,1;B^{-2n^{1/3}})

\displaystyle=\max_{z\leq w}\mathcal{W}^{2(\lambda+n^{1/3})}(z,n+1;B^{-2n^{1/3}})+B^{-2n^{1/3}}[(z,n)\to(w,1)].

From these two formulas, the lemma then follows from the fact that

z\mapsto\mathcal{W}^{2(\lambda+n^{1/3})}(z-n^{1/3}/2,n+1;B^{-2n^{1/3}})-\mathcal{W}^{2(\lambda+n^{1/3})}(-n^{1/3}/2,n+1;B^{-2n^{1/3}})

is a Brownian motion of drift
2\lambda
(e.g. Theorem
5.9
), the convergence

B^{-2n^{1/3}}[(z-n^{1/3}/2,n)\to(w,1)]-n^{2/3}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\mathcal{L}(z,-1;w,0)

in the compact topology on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
(Theorem
3.11
), and the Brownian LPP shape theorems (Propositions
3.6
,
3.7
) to control the argmax locations above. Here we have used that we can identify the law of
G
from a Brownian motion and
\mathcal{L}(z,-1;w,0)
using (
78
).
∎

Proof of Proposition
6.19
.

Fix
r_{0}>-1
and
m\in\mathbb{R}
. Then by Lemma
6.20
, for
\lambda>-n^{1/3},w>-n^{1/3}/2
we have

\mathbb{P}(\sup_{r\in[r_{0},0]}\pi^{n}_{n}(r)>m)\leq\mathbb{P}(\sup_{r\in[r_{0},0]}\pi_{\lambda,w}(r)>m)+\mathbb{P}(F^{n}(\lambda,w)\neq\mathfrak{B}^{\lambda}(w))

Taking
n\to\infty
and using Lemma
6.21
shows that the right-hand side above converges to

\mathbb{P}(\sup_{r\in[r_{0},0]}\pi_{\lambda,w}(r)>m)+\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{B}^{\lambda}(w;\mathcal{L}\mid S^{-}_{0,-1})\neq\mathfrak{B}^{\lambda}(w)).

(93)

Taking
\lambda=k,w=-k+\log k
in (
93
), shear and spatial stationarity of
\mathcal{L}
(Lemma
3.9
.2,4) implies that (
93
) converges to
0
as
k\to\infty
. This yields the first bullet. The second bullet is similar. Indeed, by Lemma
6.20
and Lemma
6.21
for any fixed
\lambda,w
we have that

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\to\infty}

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\sup_{r\leq-1-\epsilon}\pi^{n}_{n}(r)-\alpha r>0)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}(\sup_{r\leq-1-\epsilon}\pi_{\lambda,w}(r)-\alpha r>0)+\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{B}^{\lambda}(w;\mathcal{L}\mid S^{-}_{0,-1})\neq\mathfrak{B}^{\lambda}(w)).

Taking
\lambda=-k,w=k+\log k
, the right-hand side above converges to
0
as
k\to\infty
. The final bullet requires a slightly different idea. For each
n
, let

R_{n}=\{(y,t)\in\mathbb{R}\times(-\infty,0]:y>\pi^{n}_{n}(t)\}.

By the first two bullet points in Proposition
6.19
, the law of the set
R_{n}^{c}
is tight with respect to the local Hausdorff topology on closed subsets of
[-\infty,\infty]\times(-\infty,0]
.
Moreover, any subsequential limit of this law is the law of a random ray
[-\infty,X]\times\{-1\}
, where
X
is a
[-\infty,\infty]
-valued random variable. Note that the proof of the first bullet actually shows that
X<\infty
almost surely, since in that proof we showed that

\lim_{k\to\infty}\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}(\pi_{-k,k+\log k},\pi^{n}_{n}\text{ are disjoint})=1.

To prove the final bullet, we must show that
X=0
almost surely. By Lemma
6.15
we have:

\mathfrak{B}^{0}(b;\mathcal{L}\mid R_{n})-\mathfrak{B}^{0}(2b;\mathcal{L}\mid\bar{R}_{n})\leq F^{n}(0,b)-F^{n}(0,2b)\leq\mathfrak{B}^{0}(b;\mathcal{L}\mid\bar{R}_{n})-\mathfrak{B}^{0}(2b;\mathcal{L}\mid R_{n}).

(94)

Now, Lemma
6.21
implies that for every fixed
w\in\mathbb{R}
we have that

F^{n}(0,w)-F^{n}(0,2w)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}[\sup_{x\geq 0}B(x)+\mathcal{L}(x,-1;w,0)]-[\sup_{x\geq 0}B(x)+\mathcal{L}(x,-1;2w,0)],

where
B
is an independent Brownian motion. The symmetries of
\mathcal{L}
imply that the right-hand side above equals

Z(w)+3w^{2},
where
Z(w),w\leq 0
is a tight collection of random variables. On the other hand, consider a subsequence along which all the random variables

R_{n}^{c},\mathcal{L},\mathfrak{B}^{0}(w;\mathcal{L}\mid R_{n})-\mathfrak{B}^{0}(2w;\mathcal{L}\mid\bar{R}_{n}),\mathfrak{B}^{0}(w;\mathcal{L}\mid\bar{R}_{n})-\mathfrak{B}^{0}(2w;\mathcal{L}\mid R_{n}),w\in\mathbb{Z}

jointly converge in distribution to limits
[-\infty,X]\times\{-1\},\tilde{\mathcal{L}},Y_{w}^{-},Y_{w}^{+},w\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Here we allow
Y_{w}^{-},Y_{w}^{+}
to possibly take on the values
\pm\infty
so that we do not need to address tightness of the prelimiting sequence. As before, we allow
X
to take on the value
-\infty
. For every
w\in\mathbb{Z}
we have that

\displaystyle[\sup_{x>X}\tilde{B}(x)+\tilde{\mathcal{L}}(x,-1;w,0)]-[\sup_{x\geq X}\tilde{B}(x)+\tilde{\mathcal{L}}(x,-1;2w,0)]\leq Y_{w}^{-}\leq Y_{w}^{+}

\displaystyle\leq[\sup_{x\geq X}\tilde{B}(x)+\tilde{\mathcal{L}}(x,-1;w,0)]-[\sup_{x>X}\tilde{B}(x)+\tilde{\mathcal{L}}(x,-1;2w,0)],

where
\tilde{B}(x)=\mathfrak{B}^{0}(x,-1;\mathcal{L})
is another Brownian motion of variance
2
. Now, if
X=-\infty
with positive probability, then on this event the metric composition law for Busemann functions implies that
Y_{w}^{+}=Y_{w}^{-}=B(w)-B(2w),
where
B
is a Brownian motion. This contradicts the asymptotic growth of the random variables
Z(w)+3w^{2}
. On the other hand, if
X>-\infty
, then the shape theorem for
\mathcal{L}
(Lemma
3.16
) then implies that as
w\to-\infty
we have that
Y_{w}^{-},Y_{w}^{+}
are equal to
3w^{2}-3wX
plus lower order terms. This contradicts the asymptotic growth of the random variables
Z(w)+3w^{2}
unless
X=0
almost surely.
∎
